### [1] [EXAONE Forecast for Finance](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.04239)
*Seunghan Lee,Jaehoon Lee,Jun Seo,Tae Yoon Lim,Dongwan Kang,Hwanil Choi,Minjae Kim,Sungdong Yoo,Junhyeok Kang,Sangjun Han,Soonyoung Lee,Wonbin Ahn*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: EXAONE Finance is a financial time-series foundation model using an attention-free architecture (causal 1D convolution for temporal mixing and group-aware pooling MLP for variate mixing) with masked context augmentation, pretrained on a large-scale multi-asset financial corpus (equities, FX, commodities, crypto, fixed income, macro indicators), achieving SOTA on the FinVerse benchmark across point-forecast accuracy, cross-sectional asset ranking, and portfolio profitability.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: General-domain time-series foundation models (TSFMs) are poorly suited to financial forecasting due to (1) quadratic computational cost of self-attention with sequence length and variate count, (2) the assumption of fully observed inputs which fails in real financial panels with intermittent missingness, and (3) pretraining corpora that do not capture the unique dynamics of financial markets.

Method: EXAONE Finance replaces self-attention with two linear-time operators: (1) a causal 1D convolution for temporal mixing and (2) a group-aware pooling MLP for variate mixing. It also employs masked context augmentation during training, exposing the model to contiguous missing spans to improve robustness to missingness. The model is pretrained on a large-scale financial corpus spanning equities, FX, commodities, crypto-assets, fixed income, and macroeconomic indicators.

Result: On the FinVerse financial forecasting benchmark covering diverse asset classes, EXAONE Finance achieves state-of-the-art performance, ranking first across all three evaluation tiers: point-forecast accuracy, cross-sectional asset ranking, and portfolio profitability.

Conclusion: An attention-free, linear-time architecture combined with missingness-aware training and financial-domain pretraining corpus enables a financial TS foundation model that outperforms existing approaches on comprehensive financial forecasting benchmarks, addressing the computational and data challenges inherent to financial time-series modeling.

Abstract: This technical report presents EXAONE Forecast for Finance (EXAONE Finance), a financial time series (TS) foundation model (TSFM) tailored to financial forecasting. Recent TSFMs achieve strong zero-shot performance through large-scale pretraining. However, they are primarily developed for general-domain TS and largely rely on self-attention backbones whose computational cost grows quadratically with sequence length and variate count. Moreover, they assume fully observed inputs and are pretrained on corpora that fail to capture the unique dynamics of financial markets. These limitations hinder their applicability to finance, where long, many-channel, intermittently observed panels are common. To address these challenges, EXAONE Finance adopts an attention-free architecture, replacing self-attention with two simple yet effective linear-time operators: 1) a causal 1D convolution for temporal mixing and 2) a group-aware pooling multi-layer perceptron (MLP) for variate mixing. Furthermore, a masked context augmentation exposes the model to contiguous missing spans during training, improving robustness to the missingness pervasive in financial markets. EXAONE Finance is pretrained on a large-scale financial corpus covering not only equities but also foreign exchange, commodities, crypto-assets, fixed income, and macroeconomic indicators. On FinVerse, a financial forecasting benchmark covering diverse asset classes, EXAONE Finance attains state-of-the-art performance, ranking first across all three evaluation tiers---point-forecast accuracy, cross-sectional asset ranking, and portfolio profitability.

</details>


### [2] [From Matching Models to Recruiting Agents: A Systematized Narrative Review of AI Recruitment Systems, Evaluation, and Governance](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.04286)
*Ziyi Zhao,Guanzheng Wei*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This systematized narrative review traces the evolution of AI in recruitment from bilateral retrieval and ranking through neural person-job matching, LLM components, and tool-using recruiting agents. It analyzes 40 representative works organized across three coupled transitions (similarity to reciprocal suitability, model to compound workflow, offline prediction to evidence-aligned evaluation) and introduces a staged mapping from evaluation evidence to defensible claims, plus an agenda for reciprocal, evidence-grounded, temporally controlled, selective, and auditable recruitment systems.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Recruitment AI has evolved from automating profile pairs and ranked lists to multi-stage workflows that retrieve evidence, compare candidates, and support or execute actions. The field lacks a structured synthesis tracing this development and identifying persistent evaluation gaps—such as behavioral labels confounding exposure, preference, and qualification; limited external validity from private/synthetic data; and pipeline failures hidden by final-output scores.

Method: A systematized narrative review using a purposive search and coding protocol (updated through 23 July 2026, with targeted updates through 2 September 2026), organizing 40 representative works with supporting industrial and legal sources. The synthesis analyzes evidence across six levels (field, pair, list, case, trajectory, and outcome) spanning document understanding, retrieval, ranking, assessment, interviewing, sourcing, and human handoff—deliberately framed not as a prevalence estimate.

Result: The review identifies persistent gaps: behavioral labels confound exposure, preference, and qualification; private and synthetic data limit external validity; final-output scores conceal pipeline failures; and within the coded set, privacy is not directly evaluated and no row jointly evaluates utility, fairness, privacy, and security. It produces a staged mapping from evaluation evidence to the strongest defensible claim, along with an agenda for reciprocal, evidence-grounded, temporally controlled, selective, and auditable systems.

Conclusion: Progress in recruitment AI should be judged by whether workflows retrieve the right evidence, preserve uncertainty, support contestable decisions, and improve outcomes under explicit cost and risk constraints—rather than by benchmark scores alone. The staged evidence-to-claim mapping offers researchers and practitioners a framework for making defensible claims proportionate to their evaluation evidence.

Abstract: Artificial intelligence in recruitment has shifted the object being automated from profile pairs and ranked lists to multi-stage workflows that retrieve evidence, compare candidates, and support or execute actions. This systematized narrative review traces that development from bilateral retrieval and behavioral ranking through neural person--job matching, large language model (LLM) components, and tool-using recruiting agents. Using a purposive search and coding protocol updated through 23 July 2026, plus targeted updates through 2 September 2026, we organize 40 representative works with supporting industrial and legal sources. This synthesis is not a prevalence estimate. We analyze three coupled transitions: from similarity to reciprocal suitability, from a model to a compound workflow, and from offline prediction to evidence- and productivity-aligned evaluation. Across document understanding, retrieval, ranking, assessment, interviewing, sourcing, and human handoff, we distinguish field-, pair-, list-, case-, trajectory-, and outcome-level evidence. Persistent gaps arise because behavioral labels confound exposure, preference, and qualification; private and synthetic data limit external validity; final-output scores conceal pipeline failures; and, within the coded set, privacy is not directly evaluated and no row jointly evaluates utility, fairness, privacy, and security. These observations describe the coded set rather than the field as a whole. We therefore introduce a staged mapping from evaluation evidence to the strongest defensible claim, together with an agenda for reciprocal, evidence-grounded, temporally controlled, selective, and auditable systems. Progress should be judged by whether workflows retrieve the right evidence, preserve uncertainty, support contestable decisions, and improve outcomes under explicit cost and risk constraints.

</details>


### [3] [Harbor Adapters and Harbor-Index: Infrastructure and a Curated Meta-Dataset for Large-Scale Agentic Evaluation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.04298)
*Lin Shi,Haowei Lin,Zixuan Zhu,Xiaoyue Zhou,Xiang Li,Xiangning Lin,Yaxuan Deng,Han Xu,Yuangang Li,Shanda Li,Zizhao Chen,Hanwen Xing,Harsh Raj,Bo Chen,Quan Shi,Steven Dillmann,Yipeng Gao,Puneesh Khanna,Ruofan Lu,Chao Beyond Zhou,Michael Yang,Robert Zhang,Siyuan Chai,Jiayu Chang,Yizhao Chen,Xiaokun Chen,Yiwei Dai,Wenting Yang,Hange Liu,Minghao Liu,Zihan Wang,Adnan El Assadi,Benedikt Stroebl,E. Kelly Buchanan,Han Meng,Junwei He,Longxuan Yu,Radin Shayanfar,Yukyung Lee,Zhikang Dong,Allen G Hart,Anjiang Wei,Anurag Kashyap,Arpandeep Khatua,Audrey Jixin Zheng,Chengrui Ma,David Heineman,Dubing Chen,Hai-Anh Trinh,Haishuo Fang,Hefan Zhang,Hui Shen,Issa Sugiura,Jiankai Sun,Jiechao Gao,Junhong Lin,Junnan Li,Kai Yang,Lei Hsiung,Maoyu Wang,Mengze Tang,Nabil Omi,Negin Raoof,Nicholas Edwards,Octavia Guo,Orfeas Menis Mastromichalakis,Pengliang Ji,Przemysław Hejman,Qi Qi,Qunshu Lin,Richard Zhuang,Rui Yang,Ruichen Zheng,Ryan Marten,Shaghayegh Fazliani,Shizheng Hou,Sicong Jiang,Sijie Li,Song Bian,Terry Yue Zhuo,Tianqing Wu,Tom Tang,Wanjia Zhao,Weihao Xuan,Wenhua Liang,Xian Liu,Xin Lan,Xuan Zhang,Xuandong Zhao,Yanchuan Tang,Yifan Jiang,Yijiang Li,Yitong Guan,Yizhi Li,Yonghui Liu,Yuheng Tang,Yujun,Mao,Yunfei Zhao,Yuxin Wang,Yuxuan Tang,Zhenheng Tang,Zhifei Li,Ziruo Wang,Ziyu She,Kaiyuan Liu,Iheb Chaabane,Yuxin Tang,Xiangyi Li,Andy Konwinski,Boxuan Li,Leon Liangyu Chen,Alex Dimakis,Nicholas Carlini,Soroush Vosoughi,Di He,Etash Guha,Benjamin Feuer,Mike Merrill,Ludwig Schmidt,Alex Shaw*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Harbor Adapters is a unified evaluation infrastructure that ports over 80 agentic benchmarks, evaluates 8 models across 54 benchmarks, and introduces Harbor-Index—a curated set of 82 difficult tasks from 29 benchmarks where no model-harness configuration exceeds 30% pass rate.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Evaluating agents on agentic benchmarks is challenging due to complex environments and fragmented agent integration requirements, making large-scale, standardized comparison difficult.

Method: The work has three components: (1) benchmark adapters porting over 80 benchmarks, validated via code review and parity experiments; (2) large-scale evaluation of 8 models across 54 benchmarks using Terminus-2 and 3 native harnesses; (3) Harbor-Index, 82 curated tasks from 29 benchmarks refined through difficulty filtering, AI/human audit, and an audit-and-fix loop.

Result: No model-harness configuration on Harbor-Index exceeds 30% pass rate; the strongest model (GPT-5.5 with Codex) achieves 28.0%. The suite enables broader analysis of agent capabilities and failure modes.

Conclusion: Harbor Adapters and Harbor-Index provide open-source artifacts for more reliable, comprehensive, and affordable agentic evaluation, preserving challenge and breadth while reducing cost.

Abstract: Evaluating agents on the growing number of agentic benchmarks is challenging because they often require complex environments and agent integrations. We introduce Harbor Adapters, a unified evaluation infrastructure for agentic benchmarks. Our work makes three contributions. First, we develop benchmark adapters that port more than 80 benchmarks to evaluate arbitrary agents, and validate them through rigorous code review and parity experiments. Second, we conduct a large-scale evaluation of 8 models spanning capability tiers across 54 benchmarks; every model is run with Terminus-2 and with one of 3 native harnesses. This enables a broader analysis of agent capabilities and failure modes than was previously possible. Third, we introduce Harbor-Index, a curated set of 82 difficult, diverse, and high-quality tasks spanning 29 benchmarks, refined from the adapted suite through difficulty filtering, AI and human audit, and an audit-and-fix loop. Harbor-Index preserves the challenge and breadth of large-scale agentic evaluations while being affordable to run; no evaluated model-harness configuration exceeds 30% pass rate, and the strongest (GPT-5.5 with Codex) reaches 28.0%. We release the adapters, evaluation results, in-depth analysis, and Harbor-Index as open-source artifacts to support more reliable and comprehensive evaluation of language-model agents.

</details>


### [4] [Data-Optimized Contingency Screening: A Machine Learning Approach to Power System Security](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.04300)
*Joshua Salako,Folajimi Osikomaiya,Olakorede Olamiju*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This study applies machine learning algorithms (KNN, RF, SVM) to classify power system contingency security into safe, moderate, or severe classes using N-k contingency scenarios on IEEE-14 and IEEE-30 bus systems. The approach combines Newton-Raphson load flow with Overall Performance Index (OPI), and evaluates four preprocessing configurations (normalized, SMOTE-balanced, PCA-transformed, and combined SMOTE+PCA). RF achieved the highest F1 scores (0.97 on IEEE-30, 0.86 on IEEE-14), PCA contributed more to overall performance than SMOTE, and the work demonstrates ML as a scalable real-time alternative to traditional contingency analysis.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Power system security is critical for stability and reliability during disruptions. Traditional contingency analysis can be computationally expensive and slow, motivating the need for faster, scalable techniques. Effective classification of contingencies into security levels enables proactive decision-making and prevents large-scale blackouts, which this study addresses through machine learning.

Method: The study uses Newton-Raphson load flow to extract system data from N-k contingency scenarios (k=1,2,3) on IEEE-14 and IEEE-30 bus systems, with the Overall Performance Index (OPI) as the safety measure. Data preprocessing applies SMOTE for class imbalance and PCA for dimensionality reduction across four configurations (normalized, SMOTE-balanced, PCA-transformed, combined). Three classifiers—KNN, Random Forest, and SVM—are trained and evaluated using precision, recall, and F1 score, with emphasis on severe contingency classes.

Result: Random Forest achieved the highest F1 scores of 0.97 on IEEE-30 and 0.86 on IEEE-14. SVM improved classification accuracy significantly with PCA transformation. KNN performed best with SMOTE and PCA conversion combined. Overall, PCA contributed more to model performance than SMOTE. While SMOTE improved recall, it also introduced false positives, representing a trade-off with accuracy.

Conclusion: Machine learning provides a scalable and powerful alternative to traditional contingency analysis for power system security assessment. The findings demonstrate the feasibility of real-time security classification, with RF being the most effective classifier and PCA playing the more significant role in performance enhancement compared to SMOTE.

Abstract: Ensuring the security of the power system is essential for stability and reliability, especially in the event of disruption. Effective classification of contingency in power systems enables proactive decision-making and mitigates large-scale breakdowns and failures. This study explores the use of machine learning algorithms to classify security levels of contingencies in power systems into safe, moderate or severe classes. For this approach, Newton-Raphson load flow method extracts system data from contingency scenarios, using Overall Performance Index (OPI) as safety measure. For data pre-processing, Synthetic Minority Over-Sampling Technique (SMOTE) and Principal Component Analysis (PCA) is used to address class imbalance and reduce dimensionality, respectively. K-Nearest Neighbours (KNN), Random Forest (RF) and Support Vector Machines (SVM) is trained and evaluated on datasets generated through N-k contingency scenarios for k equal 1, 2, and 3 on IEEE-14 and IEEE-30 bus systems using four hybrid pre-processing configurations: normalized, SMOTE-balanced, PCA-transformed, and a combined SMOTE PCA-transformed. Performance is assessed by precision, recall and F1 score, with priority given to the severe contingency classes. The RF achieved the highest F1 scores of 0.97 in IEEE-30 and 0.86 in IEEE-14, SVM benefits significantly from PCA and improves the accuracy of the classification, while KNN is best suited for SMOTE and PCA conversion. The findings show that PCA contributes more than SMOTE to the overall performance of the model. However, SMOTE improves recall but can introduce false positives and is therefore a compromise of accuracy. This study highlights machine learning as a scalable and powerful alternative to traditional contingency analysis, which improves the assessment of security in real time.

</details>


### [5] [Iris: Climbing to the Search Frontier](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.04304)
*Ziyuan Liu,Hengqi Liu,Zichuan Wang,Yang Qin,Jiachen Liang,Xu Chu,Shaowei Chen,Yuantao Gu,Mu Chuan*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper presents Iris-mini (35B-A3B) and Iris-pro (397B-A17B), two search agents trained using a sophisticated pipeline involving reverse-constructed multi-hop web-search tasks, SFT from filtered trajectories, and RL against live search in an alternating "SFT-RL climbing" procedure. With inference-time context management, they achieve state-of-the-art results among open-source search agents on BrowseComp, BrowseComp-ZH, DeepSearchQA, and HLE.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The authors aim to build high-performing open-source search agents at different scales, addressing the need for strong web-search reasoning agents. They also highlight that inference-time context management matters more on these benchmarks than most reported system differences, so they emphasize evaluating with and without it under identical conditions.

Method: Tasks are reverse-constructed from hyperlink structures of a web corpus: multi-hop chains over an entity graph from seed pages and out-links, with non-answer entities rewritten into descriptive references to prevent string-matching shortcuts. Only questions that a reference model fails closed-book but solves with evidence are admitted. Trajectories are generated and filtered at trajectory and turn levels before SFT. Policy is optimized via RL against live search with reward judging and observation summarization served inside the training cluster, over-long rollouts interrupted and resumed from committed prefixes. SFT and RL alternate in "SFT-RL climbing," feeding hardest solved and most efficient rollouts back into the next supervised pass. All evaluations use a single ReAct agent with no sub-agents or test-time verification.

Result: With context management enabled, Iris-mini and Iris-pro score 82.2/84.8/86.9/52.3 and 88.6/85.1/92.9/56.4 on BrowseComp, BrowseComp-ZH, DeepSearchQA, and HLE respectively, achieving the strongest results among open-source search agents in their parameter ranges.

Conclusion: The paper demonstrates that a carefully designed data pipeline, SFT-RL climbing training recipe, and inference-time context management enable state-of-the-art search agent performance at two model scales. The authors plan open release of model weights and complete training/evaluation recipes.

Abstract: We present Iris-mini and Iris-pro, two search agents trained at the 35B-A3B and 397B-A17B scales, together with the data pipeline and training recipe behind them. Tasks are reverse-constructed from the hyperlink structure of a web corpus: we author multi-hop chains over an entity graph distilled from a seed page and its out-links, rewrite every non-answer entity into a descriptive reference so that no clue can be resolved by string matching, and admit only questions that a reference model fails closed-book yet solves once the supporting evidence is supplied. These questions are then turned into trajectories, which are filtered at both the trajectory and the turn level before SFT. The policy is then optimized by RL against live search, with the reward judge and the observation summarizer served inside the training cluster, and with over-long rollouts interrupted at the request level and resumed from their committed prefix at the next step. We alternate the two stages in a procedure we call SFT-RL climbing, returning the hardest solved and most efficient rollouts of each RL round to the next supervised pass. Because inference-time context management is worth more on these benchmarks than most reported differences between systems, we evaluate every benchmark both with and without it, holding the tool set, the context limit, and the judge fixed. All results come from a single ReAct agent, with no sub-agents and no test-time verification. With management enabled, on BrowseComp, BrowseComp-ZH, DeepSearchQA, and HLE the two models reach $82.2/84.8/86.9/52.3$ and $88.6/85.1/92.9/56.4$, the strongest overall results among open-source search agents in their respective parameter ranges. We plan to release the model weights together with the complete recipe for data construction, training, and evaluation.

</details>


### [6] [A Removal Based Approach to Improve LLM Faithfulness at Test-Time](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.04343)
*Qinglan Luo,S M A Nahian,John Guttag,S. Mazdak Abulnaga,Katie Matton*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper introduces a test-time method to improve the faithfulness of LLM explanations by targeting incompleteness—removing concepts not credited in the model's explanation from the input and re-querying the model, which eliminates unmentioned influences while preserving mentioned ones. The approach is model-agnostic, requires no parameter modification, and improves explanation faithfulness across two datasets, multiple model families, and two metrics.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: LLM explanations used for auditing model behavior can be unfaithful. Existing approaches are either training-time methods requiring weight access and heavy compute, or test-time methods that largely address unsoundness but not incompleteness (omission of factors that actually influence the answer). There is a need for a test-time method that directly targets incompleteness.

Method: The proposed approach removes from the input any concepts not credited in the model's explanation and re-queries the model on the reduced input. This eliminates unmentioned influences while preserving the influence of concepts that were explicitly credited, thereby targeting incompleteness at inference time without modifying model parameters.

Result: Across two datasets, multiple model families, and two independent faithfulness metrics, the method improves explanation faithfulness compared to both standard prompting and prompting designed to encourage faithfulness.

Conclusion: The test-time approach provides a flexible, model-agnostic mechanism for reducing hidden influences in LLM explanations, improving the reliability and safety of LLM-assisted decision making without requiring access to model weights or additional training.

Abstract: Large language models (LLMs) are increasingly used for consequential decisions, making their explanations an important tool for auditing model behavior. Unfortunately, these explanations can be unfaithful, failing to reflect the actual reasoning underlying the model's decisions. We consider a setting in which an LLM provides both an answer and an explanation in response to a question. We identify two distinct dimensions of unfaithful explanations: incompleteness, meaning that the explanation omits factors that influence the answer, and unsoundness, meaning that the explanation cites factors that did not influence the model's answer. Existing approaches to improving LLM faithfulness include training-time methods, which require access to model weights and extensive computational resources, and test-time methods that largely focus on addressing unsoundness. We introduce a test-time approach that directly targets incompleteness. We remove from the input the concepts not credited in the model's explanation and re-query the model on the reduced input. This eliminates unmentioned influences while preserving the influence of mentioned concepts. Across two datasets, multiple model families, and two independent faithfulness metrics, our approach improves explanation faithfulness compared to both standard prompting and prompting to encourage faithfulness. Our method is model-agnostic and can be applied at inference time without modifying model parameters, providing a flexible mechanism for reducing hidden influences and improving the reliability and safety of LLM-assisted decision making.

</details>


### [7] [Why Better Models Can Create Riskier Systems: Evidence from LLM Agents in Financial Markets](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.04373)
*Jillian Ross,Eric So,Zoe De Simone,Charles Pozniak,Andrew W. Lo*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Improving individual LLM capability can degrade system-level outcomes due to correlated behavior arising from shared training and architectures, creating a non-diversifiable risk floor. Tested via agent-based financial market simulations, the paper identifies a "capability paradox" where more capable models behave more alike, amplifying shared errors while only helping when shared reasoning is accurate.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: LLMs are increasingly deployed in high-stakes real-world systems, yet capability improvements are typically evaluated at the individual model level. The paper challenges this assumption by asking whether improving individual capability necessarily improves system-level outcomes, especially in multi-agent settings where behavioral correlations across models matter.

Method: The authors develop a general theoretical framework demonstrating how behavioral correlation among LLMs creates a non-diversifiable risk floor. They test this framework's predictions using agent-based simulations in financial markets populated with LLM traders of varying general-purpose capability levels, measuring correlation in behavior and market-level risk under both accurate and shared-misinformation conditions.

Result: (1) Frontier LLMs exhibit significantly correlated behavior that increases with capability; (2) when shared reasoning is accurate, increasing agent participation reduces market-level risk; (3) when agents share a common misinformation environment, the same correlated behavior becomes a liability, degrading system-level outcomes.

Conclusion: The findings reveal a capability paradox: improving individual models does not necessarily produce better system-level outcomes. Whether these dynamics generalize to other domains beyond financial markets remains an open empirical question.

Abstract: Large language models (LLMs) are being deployed at scale in consequential real-world systems, from financial markets to content moderation to hiring. We show that improving individual model capability can degrade rather than improve system-level outcomes. We hypothesize that shared training and architectures can lead more capable LLMs to behave more similarly, creating correlated actions that do not diversify away. We develop a general framework showing how this correlation creates a non-diversifiable risk floor and test its predictions in financial markets using an agent-based simulation with LLM traders of varying general-purpose capability. We find that: (1) frontier LLMs exhibit significantly correlated behavior that increases with capability; (2) when their shared reasoning is accurate, increasing agent participation reduces market-level risk; and (3) when agents share a common misinformation environment, the same correlated behavior becomes a liability. Together, these results identify a capability paradox: improving individual models does not necessarily produce better system-level outcomes. Whether the same dynamics arise in other domains is an open empirical question.

</details>


### [8] [Corporate Language Model (CLM): Transforming Tacit and Fragmented Enterprise Knowledge into a Sovereign, Auditable, and Executable Corporate Intelligence Layer](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.04377)
*Fabricio C. Avini,Guilherme Trez*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper introduces the Corporate Language Model (CLM), a framework transforming a firm's structured, unstructured, multimodal, and tacit knowledge into an ontology-grounded enterprise foundation. CLM integrates five capability planes and four architectural pillars (Neurosymbolic Mesh, Skill Graph, Living Digital Twins, Deep Security Layer) with a Spec-as-Code paradigm, enabling grounded reasoning and governed execution. It presents three key contributions and evidence from a JCI-accredited hospital in Brazil demonstrating three of six maturity stages under LGPD.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Enterprise AI deployments fail not due to model inadequacy but because organizations lack a structured substrate encoding decision-making, negotiation, and execution processes. Generic LLMs lack firm-specific ontological priors, RAG is brittle with no executable action path, and static playbooks cannot reason or adapt, necessitating an architecture where tacit-knowledge capture, ontological grounding, sovereign deployment, and auditable actuation are co-designed from the start.

Method: CLM is constructed from five capability planes and four architectural pillars: a Neurosymbolic Mesh coupling generative models with knowledge graphs; a Skill Graph typing and composing reusable tactics, personas, objections, and goals; Living Digital Twins modeling functional areas as reasoning surrogates; and a Deep Security Layer enforcing sovereignty, traceability, and human oversight. A Spec-as-Code paradigm bridges grounded intent and executable artifacts. The framework is instantiated and evaluated at a JCI-accredited tertiary hospital in Brazil under LGPD compliance.

Result: Four contributions are established: CLM is defined as a distinct object of study; the Skill Graph enables compositional explainability by construction; the Wisdom Listener effect is proposed, showing tacit-capable foundations compound in value with use linked to dynamic capabilities and organizational learning; and empirical evidence from the Brazilian hospital instantiates three of six maturity stages under LGPD.

Conclusion: CLM represents one instantiation of a foundation-centric class of enterprise architectures addressing the root cause of AI deployment failure—the absence of organization-specific structural substrates. The framework positions not models but firm-specific, ontology-grounded, governable foundations as the critical unit of enterprise AI success.

Abstract: Enterprise AI deployments fail not from model inadequacy, but because organizations lack a structured substrate encoding how they decide, negotiate, and execute. Generic LLMs carry no firm-specific ontological priors; RAG remains brittle, with no path to executable action; static playbooks encode logic but cannot reason or adapt. This demands an architecture treating tacit-knowledge capture, ontological grounding, sovereign deployment, and auditable actuation as co-designed from the start. This paper introduces the Corporate Language Model (CLM), a framework transforming a firm's structured, unstructured, multimodal, and tacit knowledge into an ontology-grounded enterprise foundation upon which reasoning and governed execution are composed. CLM has five capability planes and four architectural pillars: a Neurosymbolic Mesh coupling generative models with a knowledge graph; a Skill Graph where reusable tactics, personas, objections, and goals are typed and composed; Living Digital Twins modeling functional areas as reasoning surrogates; and a Deep Security Layer enforcing sovereignty, traceability, and human oversight. A Spec-as-Code paradigm bridges grounded intent and executable artifact. CLM is one instantiation of this foundation-centric class. Four contributions follow: CLM is defined as a distinct object of study; the Skill Graph is introduced for compositional explainability by construction; the Wisdom Listener effect is proposed, whereby tacit-capable foundations compound in value with use, connecting to dynamic capabilities and organizational learning; and evidence from a JCI-accredited tertiary hospital in Brazil instantiates three of the six maturity stages under LGPD.

</details>


### [9] [HarvestBench: Measuring Whether LLM Agents Will Pay to Avoid Killing Animals](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.04444)
*Jasmine Brazilek,Miles Tidmarsh,Matthias Endres,Anshuman Singh,Jeremiah Miller*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: HarvestBench is a fully reproducible farm-simulation gridworld benchmark that prices the avoidance of side-effect harm (killing animals vs. inanimate obstacles) in LLM-driven agents, measuring what models pay to avoid harm rather than what they say about harm. Across nine models and over 7,000 decisions, kill rates ranged from 0.4% to 98.8%, were not ordered by capability, and were strongly influenced by moral briefings.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing benchmarks measure side effects but do not place a cost on avoidance nor frame the side effect as a living creature. The authors aim to quantify what an LLM agent will actually sacrifice to avoid harming a living entity during goal pursuit, rather than relying on stated moral preferences.

Method: HarvestBench is a reinforcement learning gridworld farm simulation where LLM sub-agents coordinate two tractors for a cooperative corn harvest with animals in the field. When an animal blocks the path, the autopilot asks whether to drive over it (free) or swerve (posted fuel cost). Decisions are made without memory, harm is never named in the goal, and kills are compared against two controls (rocks and hay bales). A second test offers crops from a neighbor's field. No LLM grader is used; a scorer counts events from the game log for full reproducibility.

Result: Across nine models and 7,201 priced decisions (3,951 involving animals), kill rates ranged from 0.4% (Terra, Sol) to 98.8% (GPT-4o-mini), unrelated to model capability. Four of six models were price-sensitive at the 5% level (elasticities 0.09-1.69). All nine models drove over wild animals more often than farmed animals regardless of map geometry. Moral briefing had the largest effect: under it, five of six reasoning models kept kill rates under 6%, while removing it raised kill rates above 84% in all six.

Conclusion: HarvestBench provides a reproducible, economically-grounded measure of agents' revealed preferences about harming living creatures during goal pursuit, showing that stated morality (briefing) and actual behavior (kill rates under price pressure) can diverge dramatically and that mercy is not a function of model capability.

Abstract: Benchmarks for the side effects an agent causes on the way to a goal already exist, but HarvestBench is the first to put a price on avoiding the side effect and to name that side effect as a living creature. It is a farm simulation: LLM sub-agents drive a crew of two tractors through a cooperative corn harvest, with animals in the field. The environment is a reinforcement learning gridworld, every decision is made without memory, and the harm is never named in the goal. When an animal blocks a tractor's route the autopilot stops and asks the model whether to drive on, at no fuel cost, or swerve around it for a posted fuel price. Kills are compared against two controls: rocks, which damage the tractor and are hit under 1% of the time by every model, and hay bales, which are harmless and not alive. Models can also take crops from the neighbor's field instead of their own, a second test of what they treat as moral.
  Across nine models and 7,201 priced decisions, 3,951 involved an animal rather than a hay bale or a rock. Kill rates range from 0.4% to 98.8%, with Terra and Sol the most merciful and GPT-4o-mini the most cruel, and they are not ordered by capability. Four of six models were sensitive to price at the 5% level, with elasticities from 0.09 to 1.69. All nine drove over wild animals more often than farmed animals on the default map, and the direction held at every map geometry in every model with room to move. The briefing mattered most: under the morality briefing the kill rate was under 6% in five of six reasoning models, and removing it raised the kill rate above 84% in all six.
  HarvestBench uses no LLM grader. The scorer counts events in the game log, so it is fully reproducible, and it measures what a model will pay to avoid harm rather than what it says about harm.

</details>


### [10] [PerfReasoning: How Well Do LLMs Reason on Hardware Performance?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.04476)
*Dan Zhao,Karthikeyan Sankaralingam,Christos Kozyrakis,Qijing Huang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Introduces PerfReasoning, a benchmark evaluating LLMs as both direct performance reasoners and generators of analytical performance-model code for hardware design, revealing that reasoning-based Q&A is achievable (over 90% for strongest closed-source models) but reliable model construction remains far harder (below 15% for most configurations except GPT-5.6 Sol).


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Performance modeling is essential yet requires structured reasoning about computation, data reuse, storage, and movement; existing benchmarks do not assess LLMs' ability to produce reliable analytical performance models, motivating a dedicated benchmark.

Method: Constructs PerfReasoning, a benchmark with workload, architecture, and mapping specifications where models compare mappings and predict off-chip traffic and buffer requirements. Evaluates models on reasoning-based Q&A and code generation for analytical performance models. Explores task-specific RL and multi-round self-revision prompting as improvement strategies.

Result: Strongest closed-source models exceed 90% on reasoning Q&A; best open-weight model reaches 82.4%. For model construction, GPT-5.6 Sol exceeds 80% pass rate while all other model configurations average below 15% with high variance. Task-specific RL improves a 4B model's mapping-reasoning accuracy by 15.7 points, while feedback-free multi-round self-revision is unreliable.

Conclusion: PerfReasoning exposes the gap between plausible architectural reasoning and reliable performance-model construction, and will be publicly released to support reproducible evaluation and track progress.

Abstract: Performance modeling is central to hardware design and software optimization, yet constructing these models requires structured reasoning about computation, data reuse, storage, and movement. We introduce PerfReasoning, a benchmark that evaluates LLMs both as direct performance reasoners and as generators of analytical performance-model code. Given workload, architecture, and mapping specifications, models compare mappings and predict off-chip traffic and buffer requirements. The strongest closed-source models exceed 90% on reasoning-based Q&A, and the best open-weight model reaches 82.4%. However, model construction is substantially harder: while GPT-5.6 Sol exceeds 80% pass rate, all other model configurations average below 15% and vary markedly across runs. Task-specific RL raises a 4B model's mapping-reasoning accuracy by 15.7 points, whereas feedback-free multi-round self-revision prompting is not reliably effective. PerfReasoning exposes the gap between plausible architectural reasoning and reliable performance-model construction. We will publicly release the benchmark to support reproducible evaluation and track future progress.

</details>


### [11] [When Quantization Breaks Memory: Recurrent-State Write-Back in Low-Precision Temporal Inference](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.04490)
*Ismail Erbas,Xavier Intes,Vikas Pandey*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper introduces the concept of "recurrent-state write-back"—the rule governing how quantized recurrent states are stored and returned across time steps—and demonstrates that 4-bit state storage in GRU and LSTM encoder-decoders for fluorescence lifetime imaging causes 70x-300x estimation errors when applied post-training. Error feedback, residual memory, and direction memory recover accuracy without retraining, while matched training learns compatibility with the coarse state interface, establishing write-back as a key design consideration for quantized recurrent inference.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: While quantization is widely used to reduce computational and memory demands of neural-network inference, its effect on recurrent networks is conceptually distinct because the quantized state is stored and returned at the next time step, meaning the storage rule can alter subsequent computations. This paper aims to isolate and characterize this effect, which has been underappreciated in prior work on quantized recurrent inference.

Method: The authors introduce "recurrent-state write-back" to denote the state-storage rule, and study it in a compact GRU encoder-decoder (and an independently trained LSTM) for fluorescence lifetime imaging, estimating τ1 and τ2 from high-noise time-resolved signals. They hold the trained model fixed and replace continuous state propagation with deterministic 4-bit state storage, then compare against continuous baselines. They evaluate three error-feedback mechanisms (error feedback, residual memory, direction memory), perform precision sweeps, and conduct matched-training experiments to test whether compatibility with the state interface can be learned.

Result: Deterministic 4-bit state storage increases τ1 and τ2 estimation errors by approximately 70x and 300x, respectively, when applied to a fixed trained model. Failure arises when repeated small updates below the write threshold leave the stored state nearly static while the network continues proposing changes. Error feedback, residual memory, and direction memory recover accuracy post-training without retraining. Precision sweeps reveal that increasing state precision can worsen a fixed recurrent solution, whereas matched training learns compatibility with the coarse state interface. The LSTM replicates the failure and error-feedback recovery, with cell state exhibiting greater sensitivity than hidden state to coarse write-back.

Conclusion: Recurrent-state write-back is a key determinant of low-precision recurrent dynamics. The state-storage interface should be treated as a central design consideration for quantized recurrent inference, with matched training or post-training error-correction mechanisms (error feedback, residual memory, direction memory) serving as viable remedies.

Abstract: Quantization is widely used to reduce the computational and memory demands of neural-network inference. In recurrent networks, however, the quantized state is stored and returned at the next time step, so the rule used to store that state can alter subsequent computations. Here, we introduce recurrent-state write-back to denote this rule and isolate its effect in a compact GRU encoder--decoder for fluorescence lifetime imaging, a molecular imaging modality used in quantitative biological imaging. A central task is estimating two lifetime parameters, the short-lived component τ1 and the long-lived component τ2, from high-noise time-resolved fluorescence signals. Holding the trained model fixed, replacing continuous state propagation with deterministic 4-bit state storage increases estimation errors for τ1 and τ2 by approximately 70x and 300x, respectively. Failure occurs when repeated small updates remain below the write threshold, leaving the stored state nearly fixed while the network continues to propose change. Error feedback, residual memory, and direction memory carry information from these suppressed updates across time and recover accuracy without retraining. Precision sweeps show that increasing state precision can worsen a fixed recurrent solution, while matched training shows that compatibility with the state interface can be learned. To test whether this behavior extends beyond the GRU, we repeat the post-training intervention in an independently trained LSTM, where coarse write-back reproduces the failure, error feedback restores accuracy, and state-specific interventions reveal greater sensitivity of the cell state than the hidden state. Our results establish recurrent-state write-back as a key determinant of low-precision recurrent dynamics and identify the state-storage interface as a central design consideration for quantized recurrent inference.

</details>


### [12] [ResLearn-XR: Residual Learning for Network Traffic and Quality-of-Experience-Aware Modeling in Extended Reality](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.04493)
*Yoga Suhas Kuruba Manjunath,Jie Gao,Lian Zhao*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: ResLearn-XR is a residual learning framework for predicting eXtended Reality (XR) network traffic and estimating Quality-of-Experience (QoE) risk, using a two-stage temporal learning structure with residual components operating in value space for traffic forecasting and logit space for QoE risk estimation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: XR network traffic is bursty and non-stationary, posing challenges for accurate traffic prediction and QoE risk estimation; existing single-stage models lack adaptability to these dynamics.

Method: ResLearn-XR uses a two-stage temporal learning structure: a base sequence prediction model augmented with task-specific residual learning components. Residual learning operates in value space for continuous traffic forecasting and in logit space for probabilistic QoE risk estimation. A Data Descriptor Algorithm (DDA) is introduced as a causal feature-construction module that converts packet-level application-layer observables into frame-timing-aware descriptors suitable for encrypted traffic analysis. The framework is trained on a custom XR Traffic-QoE dataset pairing continuous traffic traces with session-level user-reported QoE labels.

Result: ResLearn-XR reduces SMAPE by up to 17.84% across frame-count, frame-size, and inter-arrival-time prediction, and reduces QoE-risk estimation SMAPE by up to 87.8% over single-stage baselines.

Conclusion: The residual learning framework effectively improves adaptability to bursty XR traffic dynamics and significantly outperforms single-stage baselines in both traffic forecasting and QoE risk estimation.

Abstract: We present ResLearn-XR, a residual learning framework for predicting eXtended Reality (XR) network traffic and estimating Quality-of-Experience (QoE) risk. ResLearn-XR adopts a two-stage temporal learning structure comprising a base sequence prediction model augmented with task-specific residual learning components to improve adaptability to bursty, non-stationary XR traffic dynamics. The residual learning stages operate in the value space for continuous XR traffic forecasting and in the logit space for probabilistic QoE risk estimation. \rev{For the QoE-risk branch, we introduce a Data Descriptor Algorithm (DDA), a causal feature-construction module that converts packet-level application-layer observables into frame-timing-aware descriptors suitable for encrypted traffic analysis. We also construct an XR Traffic-QoE dataset that pairs continuous XR traffic traces with session-level user-reported QoE labels. ResLearn-XR reduces SMAPE by up to 17.84% across frame-count, frame-size, and inter-arrival-time prediction, while reducing QoE-risk estimation SMAPE by up to 87.8% over single-stage baselines.

</details>


### [13] [Rethinking Indirect Prompt Injection as a Test-Time Search Problem](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.04495)
*Duong M. Nguyen,Joon Sik Kim,Blazej Manczak,Vaikkunth Mugunthan*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper formulates indirect prompt injection as a test-time search problem over a task-dependent attack surface and introduces an agentic attacker with a dedicated search harness. Results show that increasing attacker test-time compute improves vulnerability discovery, and explicit strategy management is crucial for sustained gains at larger budgets.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To provide a more principled formulation of indirect prompt injection attacks and highlight that attack success is not a budget-independent property of the victim but depends on the attacker's search procedure and compute budget.

Method: The authors introduce an agentic attacker with a dedicated search harness that performs three key functions: environment reconnaissance, structured reasoning over attack strategies, and adaptive evaluation using victim-agent feedback. They evaluate this across heterogeneous tasks and perform ablations.

Result: Increasing attacker test-time compute improves both vulnerability discovery and exploitation across tasks. Ablations reveal that explicit strategy management is important for avoiding redundant search and sustaining gains at larger compute budgets.

Conclusion: Agentic security evaluations should characterize both the attacker's search procedure and compute budget. The attacker's adaptive search over system attack surfaces represents an important and underexplored security risk for tool-using agents.

Abstract: We formulate indirect prompt injection as a test-time search over a task-dependent attack surface induced by the environment, user task, and injection task. To operationalize this formulation, we introduce an agentic attacker with a dedicated search harness that performs environment reconnaissance, structured reasoning over attack strategies, and adaptive evaluation using victim-agent feedback. Across heterogeneous tasks, we find that increasing attacker test-time compute improves vulnerability discovery and exploitation, while ablations show that explicit strategy management is important for avoiding redundant search and sustaining gains at larger budgets. These results suggest that agentic security evaluations should characterize both the attacker's search procedure and compute budget, rather than treating attack success as a budget-independent property of the victim. More broadly, our findings identify the attacker's adaptive search over the system attack surfaces as an important and underexplored security risk for tool-using agents.

</details>


### [14] [BioSync: Transformer-Based Cross-Modal Fusion for a Multimodal Physiological Digital Biomarker](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.04504)
*Seyed Mahmoud Sajjadi Mohammadabadi*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: BioSync is a multimodal composite digital biomarker framework that combines cardiac, neural, behavioral, and speech measurements into a continuous BioSync Index (BSI) using a wide-and-deep architecture with multi-head self-attention over modality tokens plus a linear feature-concatenation branch. Evaluated on two synthetic cohorts (cognitive decline and metabolic-autonomic), BioSync marginally outperforms standard concatenation and pure-attention ablations, showing robustness under modality dropout, although real-cohort validation remains outstanding.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Wearable and mobile device measurements of physiological state are partial and noise-sensitive when considered individually. Prior methods typically rely on simple feature concatenation or early/late fusion, lacking a principled framework for jointly modeling noisy multimodal observations under the BEST biomarker framework. The paper addresses the gap of a continuous composite digital biomarker that can exploit joint information across modalities.

Method: BioSync applies multi-head self-attention to modality tokens, alongside a linear branch whose hypothesis class subsumes standard feature concatenation, forming a combined wide-and-deep architecture. The design is justified by latent-variable measurement theory, positing that joint observations may carry information not available from individual modalities. Training employs modality-dropout for robustness. Evaluations include two literature-informed synthetic cohorts: a four-modality cognitive-decline cohort (HRV, EEG, actigraphy, speech) and a metabolic-autonomic cohort built on the AI-READI wearable schema, with comparisons against concatenation, pure-attention ablations, and single-modality/early-fusion/late-fusion baselines.

Result: In the cognitive cohort, BioSync achieved AUC 0.928 vs. 0.926 for concatenation. In the metabolic cohort, BioSync achieved accuracy/F1 of 0.764/0.766 vs. 0.756/0.758 for concatenation. BSI correlated with latent severity (r=0.91 cognitive, r=0.68 metabolic). Pure-attention ablation yielded AUC 0.911, isolating gains from the wide-and-deep hybrid. Under matched modality-dropout training, BioSync outperformed concatenation at five of six cognitive-corruption rates and at the highest metabolic rate. Cognitive AUC exceeded five published reference values, though dataset differences preclude controlled benchmarking.

Conclusion: The wide-and-deep architecture combining modality self-attention with a linear concatenation branch provides consistent, if modest, improvements over standard fusion approaches in synthetic evaluations, with enhanced robustness to modality corruption. However, the paper acknowledges that validation on real cohorts is necessary and that evidence remains synthetic; no controlled benchmark claim is made against published reference values.

Abstract: Cardiac, neural, behavioral, and speech measurements from wearable and mobile devices provide partial, noise-sensitive views of physiological state. BioSync combines these measurements into the \textbf{BioSync Index (BSI)}, a continuous composite digital biomarker defined under the BEST framework. The model applies multi-head self-attention to modality tokens and adds a linear branch whose hypothesis class includes standard feature concatenation. This architecture is motivated by latent-variable measurement theory and by the possibility that joint observations contain information unavailable from individual modalities. We evaluated BioSync on two literature-informed synthetic cohorts: a four-modality cognitive-decline cohort using HRV, EEG, actigraphy, and speech, and a metabolic-autonomic cohort structured around the public AI-READI wearable schema. In the cognitive cohort, BioSync and concatenation obtained AUCs of 0.928 and 0.926, respectively. In the metabolic cohort, BioSync obtained accuracy/F1 of 0.764/0.766, compared with 0.756/0.758 for concatenation. The BSI correlated with latent severity in both cohorts ($r=0.91$ and $r=0.68$). A pure-attention ablation obtained cognitive-cohort AUC 0.911, locating the increase to 0.928 in the combined wide-and-deep architecture. With matched modality-dropout training, BioSync led concatenation at five of six cognitive-cohort corruption rates and at the highest metabolic-cohort rate. Its cognitive-cohort AUC was also higher than five published digital-biomarker reference values, although differences in datasets and tasks preclude a controlled benchmark claim. Comparison with single-modality, early-fusion, and late-fusion designs across six prespecified criteria identifies the model's computational properties; validation on real cohorts remains necessary.

</details>


### [15] [What Does Multi-Harness RL Learn? Credit Assignment and Portability in Coding Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.04518)
*Chenqian Le,Jiayi Cheng,Qijia He,Runhao Li,Yinghao Li,Xupeng Chen*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper isolates the effect of grouping rule in multi-harness agent RL for repository-level coding. Training a Qwen3-8B policy on the same frozen task records from four harnesses with either Within (per-harness advantage groups) or Cross (harnesses pooled) GRPO, the study finds via 24,000 sealed evaluations that the evaluation harness is the dominant variable (2.14% to 9.27% solve rate, 4.3x factor) while the grouping rule is not: Cross minus Within is +0.25 pp on a held-out harness (CI includes zero), within noise of each rule's own seed variance (0.42-0.45 pp). Cross-harness credit yields harness-specific config adaptation but no more portable capability than within-harness credit.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Multi-harness RL recipes combine two choices—exposing policy to several harnesses and comparing rewards within relative-advantage groups—but these are confounded in prior work. The authors isolate whether pooling harnesses in a single GRPO group (Cross) versus keeping one group per harness (Within) actually improves learning or generalization to unseen harnesses.

Method: From a single Qwen3-8B supervised warm start, the authors replay identical frozen task-harness records from Aider, OpenHands, Qwen Code, and SWE-agent under two GRPO grouping rules (Within and Cross) with equal update counts and training seeds. Every checkpoint is scored against a sealed SWE-bench Verified oracle across the four source harnesses plus a held-out minimal harness, totaling 24,000 evaluations. Analyses include seed ranges, confidence intervals, out-of-fold classifier recovery of the generating harness from task advantages, action distributions, and an on-policy re-collection sanity check.

Result: Evaluation harness moves mean solve rate from 2.14% to 9.27% (4.3x factor), while the training recipe moves it by 1.16. The grouping rule is not significant: Cross minus Within on held-out harness is +0.25 pp (95% CI [-0.48, +1.02]) at 8 attempts/task, +0.16 ([-0.41, +0.72]) pooled over three seeds whose individual estimates change sign. Each rule's own seed range (0.42-0.45 pp) exceeds the group difference. Both rules show largest gains on the same source harness. Cross's pooled advantage encodes harness identity (+4.48 pp above shuffled baseline classifier recovery) whereas Within's does not, yet both reach the same held-out score and action distribution. On-policy re-collection of half the data does not change these findings.

Conclusion: Cross-harness credit (pooling harnesses in one advantage group) produces configuration adaptation to each harness rather than more portable capability than within-harness credit. The grouping boundary is not the driving factor in multi-harness RL outcomes; the evaluation harness dominates. Multi-harness RL reports should state the grouping rule explicitly and validate under an unseen harness.

Abstract: Agent reinforcement learning (RL) increasingly runs through full execution harnesses, and a multi-harness recipe mixes two choices: exposing the policy to several harnesses, and comparing their rewards inside one relative-advantage group. We isolate the second choice in repository-level coding. From one Qwen3-8B supervised warm start we replay the same frozen task-harness records from Aider, OpenHands, Qwen Code, and SWE-agent, with the same number of updates, under two rules for group-relative policy optimization (GRPO), Within (one group per task-harness pair) and Cross (harnesses pooled within a task), and score every checkpoint with a sealed SWE-bench Verified oracle on four source harnesses and a minimal harness held out of training. The evaluation harness is the dominant variable: across 24,000 sealed evaluations it moves the mean solve rate from 2.14\% to 9.27\%, a factor of 4.3, where the training recipe moves it by 1.16. The grouping rule is not. On the held-out harness, Cross minus Within is +0.25 pp, 95\% confidence interval [-0.48, +1.02], at eight attempts per task, and +0.16 [-0.41, +0.72] pooled over three training seeds whose individual estimates change sign. Each rule's own seed range, 0.42 to 0.45 pp, exceeds the difference between them. Both rules place their largest gains on the same source harness. The pooled advantage carries the harness: an out-of-fold classifier recovers the generating harness from Cross's advantage +4.48 pp above the shuffled-label baseline and from Within's not at all, and the two rules still reach the same held-out score and action distribution inside each harness. Re-collecting half the training data on-policy does not change this. Cross-harness credit yields configuration adaptation and no more portable capability than within-harness credit. Multi-harness RL reports should state the grouping boundary and test under an unseen harness.

</details>


### [16] [MaxKernel: Agentic Kernel Generation for TPUs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.04523)
*Shangkun Wang,Nina Cai,Charles Hoong,Julian Walker,Gerson Kroiz,George Vanica,Deepak Patil,Andi Gavrilescu,Hassan Sipra,Sethu Sankaran*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: MaxKernel is a multi-agent system that leverages LLMs with real-time compiler feedback to generate high-performance TPU kernels through three paradigms: Human-in-the-Loop, Autonomous optimization, and Graph-Based Autonomous Search, all sharing specialized sub-agents for planning, implementation, debugging, testing, and profiling. It achieves expert-level performance on 50+ kernel tasks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Designing high-performance custom kernels for accelerators like TPUs requires deep hardware expertise and is highly complex. The paper aims to automate kernel generation by combining LLMs with real-time compiler feedback in an agentic framework, reducing the need for manual expert tuning.

Method: MaxKernel is a multi-agent system with three paradigms: (1) HITL agent for collaborative step-by-step design; (2) Autonomous agent running a fully automated metric/trace-driven optimization loop; (3) Graph-Based Autonomous Search that scales the auto agent for global design-space exploration. All share a pool of specialized sub-agents for planning, implementation, self-debugging, testing, and hardware profiling.

Result: Evaluated on JaxBench (50 diverse TPU kernel tasks) and real-world workloads from open-source SOTA models, MaxKernel consistently generates highly optimized kernels that match expert hand-tuned baselines and delivers significant performance gains.

Conclusion: LLM-based multi-agent systems with compiler feedback can effectively automate TPU kernel generation across varying levels of human involvement, achieving expert-level performance. The system is open-sourced for further research and application.

Abstract: Designing and authoring high-performance custom kernels for accelerators is a complex task that requires deep hardware-level expertise. Large Language Models (LLM) can be leveraged together with real-time compiler feedback to build agentic systems for kernel generation. In this work, we present MaxKernel, a multi-agent system that implements three distinct paradigms for TPU kernel development: (1) a Human-in-the-Loop (HITL) agent for collaborative, step-by-step design; (2) an Autonomous (Auto) agent that executes a fully automated, metric/trace-driven optimization loop; and (3) a Graph-Based Autonomous Search that scales the Auto agent for global exploration of the design space. All three paradigms leverage a shared pool of specialized sub-agents to handle planning, implementation, self-debugging, testing, and hardware profiling. We evaluate MaxKernel on JaxBench, a comprehensive suite of 50 diverse kernel tasks for TPUs, alongside complex, real-world workloads from state-of-the-art open-source models. We demonstrate that MaxKernel consistently generates highly optimized implementations, matching expert hand-tuned baselines and delivering significant performance across the benchmark. Our agent is open-sourced and available https://github.com/AI-Hypercomputer/accelerator-agents/tree/main/MaxKernel.

</details>


### [17] [Towards a universal language of concepts: A survey](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.04528)
*Aishni Parab*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper proposes programs as a universal representation language for concept learning, reviewing computational models that use programs as concept representations and evaluating their contribution toward this goal.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Humans can learn and generalize novel concepts from sparse data because they represent knowledge in rich structural formats. The paper seeks to identify a universal representational language for concepts, proposing programs as a strong candidate.

Method: A review of computational models of concept learning that employ programs as their concept representation, with an evaluation of these models' contributions toward establishing programs as a universal representational language.

Result: The paper presents an assessment of program-based concept learning models and their collective progress toward a universal representation format for concepts.

Conclusion: Programs offer a promising and possibly universal format for representing concepts, enabling learning and generalization from sparse data akin to human capabilities.

Abstract: Humans can learn and generalize novel concepts from sparse data because they express knowledge in rich structural formats. In this paper, we propose that programs are a strong candidate for universal representation of concepts. We review computational models of concept learning that use programs as their concept representation and evaluate their contribution toward a universal representational language.

</details>


### [18] [Data-Driven Discovery of Composition-Dependent Constitutive Models for Hyperelasticity and Viscoelasticity of Digital Materials](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.04541)
*Josué García-Ávila,Beijun Shen,Manuel K. Rausch,Mary C. Boyce,Adrián Buganza-Tepole*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper presents a data-driven multi-material constitutive modeling framework for 3D-printed digital materials, extending the Bergström-Boyce viscoelastic formulation to generalize across material compositions and loading rates by using neural ODEs and constrained neural networks to learn strain-energy functions and dissipative evolution laws.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Classical finite-strain viscoelastic models rely on closed-form strain energy functions and internal variable evolution laws, which lack flexibility when a single constitutive model must generalize across multiple material compositions (stiff/compliant constituent mixtures) and loading rates. These digital materials exhibit strongly nonlinear, composition-dependent, and rate-dependent dissipative behavior spanning an order of magnitude in stiffness.

Method: The framework retains the Bergström-Boyce structure: multiplicative kinematics, invariant-based strain-energy functions, and a scalar dissipative evolution law along the normalized nonequilibrium deviatoric stress. The equilibrium branch is handled either by directly predicting closed-form parameters as functions of composition or by learning a polyconvex strain-energy function via neural ODEs. The nonequilibrium branch kinetics are similarly learned via closed-form parameters across compositions or constrained artificial neural networks.

Result: Using multi-rate uniaxial compression data across multiple material compositions, the framework captures rate-dependent stiffness and hysteresis across compositions while preserving thermodynamic consistency.

Conclusion: The proposed data-driven approach generalizes the classical viscoelastic model across different material compositions and loading rates, offering flexibility beyond closed-form constitutive models while maintaining thermodynamic rigor.

Abstract: Digital materials fabricated by multi-material 3D printing are designed as controlled mixtures of stiff and compliant constituents, yielding effective responses that span more than an order of magnitude in apparent stiffness and exhibit strongly nonlinear, composition-dependent, and rate-dependent dissipative behavior. Classical finite-strain viscoelastic models represent such behavior with closed-form strain energy functions for equilibrium and non-equilibrium stresses as well as evolution of internal variables, which may limit flexibility when a single constitutive model is expected to generalize across materials and loading rates. Here, we present a data-driven multi-material constitutive modeling framework that generalizes a formulation by Bergström and Boyce. The proposed framework retains the structure of the classical model, namely multiplicative kinematics, invariant-based strain-energy functions, and a scalar dissipative evolution law directed along the normalized nonequilibrium deviatoric stress. For the equilibrium branch, the data-driven discovery framework either directly predicts closed-form model parameters as functions of composition or automatically constructs a polyconvex strain-energy function using neural ordinary differential equations (NODEs). The nonequilibrium branch kinetics are learned similarly, either by directly identifying closed-form parameters across compositions or by using appropriately constrained artificial neural networks. Using multi-rate uniaxial compression data across multiple material compositions, we show that the proposed formulation captures rate-dependent stiffness and hysteresis across compositions while preserving thermodynamic consistency.

</details>


### [19] [From Answers to Interpretations: Rethinking Ambiguity-Induced Aleatoric Uncertainty Estimation in LLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.04543)
*Omer Nahum,Niv Nayman,Jonathan Fhima,Alon Zolfi,Jeremy Levy,Shai Mazor,Paolo Favaro*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper argues that estimating aleatoric (ambiguity-induced) uncertainty in LLMs does not require querying answers to clarified inputs. Instead, the authors propose a clarification-only approach that estimates this uncertainty directly from the space of plausible interpretations, yielding better ambiguity detection (AUROC 63.34 vs 60.85), lower computational cost (4-26x fewer output tokens, 2.2-3.5x fewer API calls), and lower correlation with epistemic uncertainty.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Distinguishing aleatoric uncertainty (due to input ambiguity/underspecification) from epistemic uncertainty (due to model knowledge limitations) is critical for reliable LLM deployment. Existing decomposition methods require generating multiple clarifications, querying the model for answers under each, and comparing them—which is costly, redundant, and subject to epistemic leakage that can mislead the estimate.

Method: The authors provide theoretical support for the claim that answers are unnecessary for identifying ambiguity. They propose a clarification-only approach that directly estimates ambiguity-induced aleatoric uncertainty from the space of plausible interpretations of the input, without generating answers to clarified inputs. This is evaluated using ambiguity detection as an operational proxy across three benchmarks.

Result: The clarification-only approach outperforms answer-based decomposition methods: higher AUROC (63.34 vs 60.85), significantly reduced computational cost (4-26x reduction in output tokens and 2.2-3.5x reduction in API calls), and substantially lower correlation with epistemic uncertainty estimates.

Conclusion: Ambiguity-induced aleatoric uncertainty is better estimated from the interpretation space than from the response space. Estimating uncertainty directly from plausible interpretations is both more accurate and more efficient than comparing answers across clarifications.

Abstract: A key challenge in reliable LLM deployment is recognizing when uncertainty reflects irreducible variability in the task rather than limitations in the model's knowledge. In language tasks, a central source of such aleatoric uncertainty is input ambiguity or underspecification, where multiple interpretations remain plausible. Existing decomposition methods estimate aleatoric uncertainty by generating multiple clarifications of the input, querying the model for an answer under each clarification, and comparing the resulting answers. We argue that answers are not necessary for identifying ambiguity: they are often redundant, add avoidable cost, and can mislead through epistemic leakage. We support this claim theoretically, and propose a clarification-only approach that estimates this ambiguity-induced component directly from the space of plausible interpretations, without answers to the clarified inputs. Using ambiguity detection as an operational evaluation across three benchmarks, this direct approach improves AUROC (63.34 vs. 60.85), reduces computational cost by 4-26x in output tokens and 2.2-3.5x in API calls, and yields estimates with substantially lower correlation with epistemic uncertainty. Overall, our results suggest that ambiguity-induced aleatoric uncertainty is better estimated from the interpretation space than from the response space.

</details>


### [20] [IPGeoAI: Transformer-Based Geolocation with LLM Semantic Fusion](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.04559)
*Avinash Kadimisetty,Andy Jinqing Yu,Philip Favaloro,Wenlong Liu,Xiaolu Xiong*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: IPGeoAI is a deep learning model that reframes city-level IP geolocation as a sequential modeling task using Transformer Encoders and Zero-Shot LLM Feature Extraction, fusing semantic AS descriptions with network topology via Multi-Head Cross-Attention. It improves city-level accuracy by 6% over a leading vendor and drives statistically significant gains in downstream production metrics.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Traditional heuristic and database-driven IP geolocation methods struggle with the complex, non-linear allocation patterns of modern network infrastructures, particularly in the rapidly growing IPv6 space and transient mobile networks, necessitating a more sophisticated approach.

Method: The approach: (1) reframes geolocation as a sequential modeling task using a Transformer Encoder to capture hierarchical dependencies in IP subnet structures; (2) integrates unstructured semantic context via a Zero-Shot LLM Feature Extraction pipeline that converts raw AS descriptions into structured metadata (e.g., 'University' vs. 'ISP', 'Global' vs. 'Local'); (3) fuses these semantic signals into the network via a Multi-Head Cross-Attention module to bridge numerical topology and semantic identity; (4) adopts a hierarchical inference strategy refining coarse-grained country signals for city-level prediction.

Result: IPGeoAI significantly outperforms a leading external vendor in city-level granularity on a proprietary dataset spanning 200,000 cities, achieving a 6% improvement in city-level accuracy with 100% traffic coverage. In large-scale online production tests, the model achieved a statistically significant +0.35% improvement in 1st-tier downstream use case metrics.

Conclusion: The paper demonstrates that combining Transformer-based sequential modeling with LLM-extracted semantic features provides a robust, scalable solution for city-level IP geolocation, notably extending coverage to 100% of traffic while improving accuracy and downstream performance.

Abstract: Accurate city-level IP Geolocation is an important enabler for the modern digital ecosystem, underpinning services ranging from local content delivery and targeting to digital rights enforcement. However, traditional heuristic and database-driven methods often struggle to resolve the complex, non-linear allocation patterns of modern network infrastructures, particularly within the exploding IPv6 address space and transient mobile networks. In this paper, we introduce IPGeoAI, a novel deep learning model architecture that reframes geolocation from a static lookup problem to a sequential modeling task. Our approach utilizes the Transformer Encoder to capture hierarchical dependencies inherent in IP subnet structures. We propose a method to resolve geographic ambiguity by integrating unstructured semantic context via a Zero-Shot LLM Feature Extraction pipeline. We utilize Large Language Models to transform raw, noisy Autonomous Systems (AS) descriptions into structured, domain-specific metadata (such as 'University' vs. 'ISP' or 'Global' vs. 'Local') via an offline pre-computation process. By fusing these semantic signals into the network via a Multi-Head Cross-Attention module, we bridge the gap between numerical network topology and real-world semantic identity. Extensive offline evaluation on a proprietary dataset spanning 200,000 cities demonstrates that IPGeoAI significantly outperforms a leading external vendor in city-level granularity. By adopting a hierarchical inference strategy that refines coarse-grained country signals, our model achieves a 6% improvement in city-level accuracy while extending coverage to 100% of the traffic. Furthermore, in large-scale online production tests, the model drove a statistically significant +0.35% improvement in our 1st-tier downstream use cases metric.

</details>


### [21] [Reducing Hallucinated Transcripts in Whisper via Hallucination Space Projection](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.04561)
*Maryam Abbasihafshejani,Murtuza Jadliwala*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper proposes a training-free, inference-time method to reduce hallucinated transcripts produced by Whisper ASR on non-speech inputs by estimating a low-rank hallucination-associated subspace from calibration data and projecting decoder activations away from it during inference. Two variants (always-on and gated) achieve substantial hallucination reduction (HR from 31.31% down to 2.44% and 3.74% respectively) at the cost of modest WER degradation on genuine speech.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Whisper, despite being a widely used ASR foundation model, can produce fluent hallucinated transcripts when given inputs with little or no speech, which degrades its reliability. Existing solutions likely require retraining or architectural modifications; the goal is to suppress hallucinations without additional training.

Method: The method is training-free and operates at inference time. A compact hallucination-associated subspace is estimated from non-speech calibration data using low-rank projection of decoder activations. During inference, decoder hidden states are projected away from this subspace. Two variants are proposed: (1) always-on projection applied to all inputs, and (2) gated projection applied only when Whisper predicts the input is likely non-speech.

Result: On non-speech benchmarks, always-on projection reduces average hallucination rate (HR) from 31.31% to 2.44% (92.21% relative reduction), while gated projection reduces HR to 3.74% (88.05% relative reduction) with lower false rejection of genuine speech. On LibriSpeech, gated projection increases absolute WER by 0.33-4.39 percentage points with false-rejection rates of 0.41-9.97% across model and split settings.

Conclusion: Low-rank activation projection can substantially suppress Whisper hallucinations without retraining, offering a controllable trade-off between hallucination suppression and speech recognition performance.
