The gated variant provides a favorable balance by achieving near-equivalent hallucination reduction with lower false rejections of genuine speech.

Abstract: Whisper is a widely used foundation model for automatic speech recognition (ASR), but its generative decoder can produce fluent hallucinated transcripts for inputs containing little or no speech. We propose a training-free, inference-time method to reduce these hallucinations using low-rank projection of decoder activations. A compact hallucination-associated subspace is estimated from non-speech calibration data, and decoder hidden states are projected away from this subspace during inference.
  We evaluate two variants: always-on, which applies projection to all inputs, and gated, which applies it only when Whisper predicts that an input is likely non-speech. Across non-speech benchmarks, always-on projection reduces average hallucination rate (HR) from 31.31% to 2.44%, a 92.21% relative reduction, while gated projection reduces HR to 3.74%, an 88.05% relative reduction, with lower false rejection of genuine speech. On LibriSpeech, gated projection increases absolute word error rate (WER) by 0.33-4.39 percentage points and yields false-rejection rates (FRR) of 0.41--9.97% across model and split settings.
  These results show that low-rank activation projection can substantially suppress Whisper hallucinations without retraining, while providing a controllable trade-off between hallucination suppression and speech recognition performance.

</details>


### [22] [La Agente Óptima: Towards Agentic Self-Driving Laboratories](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.04564)
*Marcel Müller,Jiaru Bai,Willi Gottstein,Abhijoy Mandal,Mohammad Nazeri,Elia Savino,Yanlin Fang,Sujoy Das,Sergio Pablo García Carrillo,Yeonghun Kang,Juan B. Pérez-Sánchez,Simone Pilon,Martin Fitzner,Timothy Noël,Frank Gu,Varinia Bernales,Alán Aspuru-Guzik*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper presents La Agente Óptima, an agentic framework that autonomously constructs and supervises Bayesian optimization campaigns for self-driving laboratories by separating LLM reasoning from executed optimization loops. It maintains persistent optimization state, intervenes only when interpretation or campaign revision is needed, and keeps decisions auditable. Evaluated on ablation studies, five digital tasks, and two physical platforms, Óptima successfully corrected a mid-run measurement failure in a contact angle campaign and improved yield from 30% to 59% in a five-day flow-chemistry campaign at lower cost than human-directed approaches.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Self-driving laboratories often require human specialists to translate scientific objectives into executable closed-loop campaigns and to adjust them as data and operating conditions evolve, limiting accessibility and scalability of SDL-assisted discovery.

Method: Óptima is an agentic framework that separates LLM reasoning from executed Bayesian optimization campaigns. It runs repetitive optimization loops consistently with a persistent optimization state, returns control to the LLM agent only when progress requires interpretation or campaign revision, and maintains auditable records of all decisions. It was evaluated through ablation studies, five digital discovery tasks, and two physical platforms.

Result: In a closed-loop contact angle campaign, Óptima autonomously identified and corrected a mid-run measurement failure, bringing the contact angle from 71.4 to 67.8 degrees (just above the target 64-66 degree range), and correctly inferred the target was likely unattainable with available reagents, recommending a formulation change. In a five-day multi-objective flow-chemistry campaign, Óptima increased yield from 30% to 59% over 23 experiments, using less starting material and lower cost than a human-directed campaign while selecting a more mass-efficient operating point.

Conclusion: LLM-based agents can make rigorous, long-running optimization campaigns accessible to domain scientists without specialist setup, expanding the scope of self-driving laboratories.

Abstract: Self-driving laboratories (SDLs) combine automated experimentation with adaptive decision-making to accelerate scientific discovery. Their operation nevertheless often depends on human specialists who translate scientific objectives into executable closed-loop campaigns. Specialists adjust them as data and operating conditions change. Here, we present La Agente Óptima, an agentic framework that constructs and supervises Bayesian optimization campaigns across computational and experimental systems while maintaining a persistent optimization state. By separating large language model (LLM) reasoning from executed campaigns, Óptima runs repetitive optimization loops consistently, returns control to the agent only when progress requires interpretation or campaign revision, and keeps every decision auditable. We evaluate Óptima across ablation studies, five digital discovery tasks, and two physical platforms. Throughout, Óptima maintained executable campaigns as both the scientific problem and execution environment evolved. In a closed-loop contact angle optimization campaign, Óptima identified and corrected a mid-run measurement failure, bringing the contact angle from 71.4 to 67.8 degrees, just above the 64-66 degree range. From this result, Óptima correctly inferred that the target was likely unattainable with the available reagents and recommended changing the formulation. In a five-day multi-objective flow-chemistry campaign, Óptima increased the yield from 30% to 59% over 23 experiments. Despite substantial inference costs, it cost less and used substantially less starting material than a human-directed campaign, while selecting a more mass-efficient operating point. These results show that LLM-based agents can make rigorous, long-running optimization campaigns accessible to domain scientists without specialist setup, expanding the scope of SDLs.

</details>


### [23] [Extremely Sparse Supervision Incentivizes Reasoning Ability](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.04565)
*Zhishuai Liu,Xingzi Xu,Mehmet Saygin Seyfioglu,Pan Xu,Karim Bouyarmane*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper challenges the assumption that effective post-training of large language models requires intensive token-level supervision. In the on-policy distillation (OPD) setting, the authors discover that supervising only one or two tokens per reasoning trajectory (as few as 0.05% of all tokens) can match or surpass full-token training in improving reasoning capabilities. This finding is consistent across nine teacher–student configurations, multiple model scales, mathematical and coding reasoning tasks, Llama models, and PPO-based RLVR.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Prevailing post-training methods optimize over massive numbers of tokens under the implicit assumption that effective learning must be token-intensive. The authors revisit this assumption in the on-policy distillation setting, questioning whether dense token-level supervision is truly necessary for effective reasoning skill acquisition.

Method: The paper conducts experiments in the on-policy distillation (OPD) setting, which provides dense teacher supervision at every generated token. Using the Qwen3 family, they systematically restrict supervision to extremely sparse token subsets (as few as one or two tokens per reasoning trajectory, ~0.05% of tokens) and compare against full-token training. They evaluate across nine teacher–student configurations spanning different model scales on math and coding reasoning tasks, extending validation to Llama models and PPO-based RLVR.

Result: Sparse supervision of only one or two tokens per trajectory matches or surpasses full-token training in improving reasoning ability in most cases, despite excluding the vast majority of tokens from the training objective. This phenomenon is consistently observed across all nine teacher–student configurations, different model families, and both distillation and RL-based training paradigms.

Conclusion: The results challenge the token-intensive assumption in post-training and suggest that reasoning can be effectively incentivized through extremely sparse supervision, potentially resembling natural learning processes where reflection on a few critical reasoning steps suffices. This points to new directions for understanding and designing more efficient post-training algorithms.

Abstract: Large language models demonstrate increasingly strong reasoning capabilities through effective post-training. Yet, prevailing post-training methods optimize over massive numbers of tokens, implicitly assuming that effective learning must be token-intensive. We revisit this assumption in the on-policy distillation (OPD) setting, which naturally admits dense teacher supervision at every generated token. Using the Qwen3 family, we discover a counter-intuitive phenomenon: reasoning can be effectively incentivized by an extremely small fraction of generated tokens--as few as one or two tokens per reasoning trajectory, corresponding to only 0.05% of all tokens. Surprisingly, this sparse supervision in most cases matches or surpasses full-token training in improving reasoning ability, despite excluding the vast majority of generated tokens from the training objective. This phenomenon is consistently observed across nine teacher--student configurations spanning different model scales on mathematical reasoning tasks, and is further validated on coding reasoning, Llama models and Proximal Policy Optimization (PPO)-based reinforcement learning with verifiable reward (RLVR). Interestingly, such extremely sparse supervision may be closer to the natural learning process: rather than correcting every step word by word, one reflects on a few critical reasoning steps, updates prior understanding, and continues the trial-and-error, avoiding micro-level corrections while remaining remarkably effective. Overall, our results challenge the assumption that effective post-training must be token-intensive and point to a new direction for understanding and designing more efficient post-training algorithms.

</details>


### [24] [Does the Selected Object Reach the Reader? Auditing Identity Handoffs in Grounded Language-Model Pipelines](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.04579)
*Siddharth Vohra,Runmin Jiang,Xiaomo Li,Min Xu*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper audits the gap between dataset-linked object retrieval and actual selected-object retrieval in grounded language-model pipelines across three stages (selection, retrieval, answering). Analyzing 600 HybridQA questions, it finds that "recall" commonly measures the dataset-traced passage rather than the decoded selected object, and these two identities differ on 329 of 1,792 resolvable records. Body-only BM25 omits the selected object on 26.6% of records at cutoff five, while hybrid retrieval with reranking omits it on only 1.0%. Frozen reader experiments show the aligned object's presence is associated with 28.6–31.0 points higher exact match, and removing it sharply lowers accuracy while removing a similar-length passage does not. The paper releases the Returned-Object Profile (ROP) for executable auditing.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Grounded language-model pipelines involve selecting an object, retrieving passages for it, and answering using that evidence. If the selected object must reach the reader but is lost, the handoff breaks. However, standard benchmark recall checks the dataset-linked object, which can differ from the actually selected object—creating a mismatch that masks retrieval failures and misattributes errors.

Method: The authors audit 600 HybridQA questions across three selector families, resolving 1,463–1,792 records. They compare dataset-traced passage identity against decoded selected-object identity under retrieval configurations including exact key lookup, exact title matching, body-only BM25 at cutoff five, and hybrid retrieval with reranking. They quantify identity mismatches and ranking disagreements (top-five checks disagree on 106/1,792 records). They run frozen reader comparisons associating the aligned object's presence with exact-match performance, plus a deliberately selected 64-item cohort ablation removing the aligned passage versus a similar-length comparison passage.

Result: Exact key lookup and title matching return the object every time when identities align. Body-only BM25 omits the selected object on 389 records (26.6%) at cutoff five, whereas hybrid retrieval with reranking omits it on only 14 (1.0%). The two object identities differ on 329/1,792 resolvable records. Frozen reader comparisons associate the aligned object's presence with 28.6–31.0 points higher exact match. In the 64-item ablation cohort, removing the target passage sharply lowers exact match while removing a similar-length comparison passage does not reproduce this drop.

Conclusion: Benchmark recall as conventionally measured can diverge substantially from return-of-selected-object recall in grounded QA pipelines, due to identity mismatch between the dataset-traced object and the decoded selected object. This mismatch can obscure retrieval failures and misattribute reader performance drops. The authors propose the Returned-Object Profile (ROP)—an executable record containing the target, returned-ID field, cutoff, membership rule, and complete expected population—with data and offline replay for reproducible auditing.

Abstract: Grounded language-model pipelines can be divided into three stages: selecting an object, retrieving passages for it, and using that evidence to answer. If the selected object must reach the reader, losing it breaks the handoff. Benchmark recall checks the dataset-linked object, which can differ. We audit 600 HybridQA questions across three selector families. On 1,463 resolvable records where the selected object matches the dataset-traced passage, exact key lookup and exact title matching return the object every time. With every ranked rule given the same decoded selected title, body-only BM25 omits it on 389 records (26.6%) at cutoff five, while hybrid retrieval with reranking omits it on 14 (1.0%). The two identities differ on 329 of 1,792 resolvable records. With original-question rankings, their top-five checks disagree on 106 records (5.9%). Frozen reader comparisons associate the aligned object's presence with 28.6 to 31.0 points higher exact match. In a deliberately selected 64-item cohort, removing that passage sharply lowers exact match, while removing a similar-length comparison passage does not reproduce the drop. We release the Returned-Object Profile (ROP), an executable record of the target, returned-ID field, cutoff, membership rule, and complete expected population, with data and an offline replay.

</details>


### [25] [$τ^τ$-Bench: An Environment for End-To-End, Realistic Agent Construction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.04611)
*Quan Shi,Keshav Dhandhania,Karthik Narasimhan,Victor Barres*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Introduces ττ-bench (hyper-tau-bench), a benchmark that tasks AI systems with building complete customer-service agents from realistic engagement materials (business records, a client with requirements, a production API, existing codebase, and constraints on serving cost/models). Strongest configuration (Claude Opus 5 under Claude Code) passes only 23.9% of evaluation simulations versus an 82.2% expert-authored ceiling, revealing systematic weaknesses in deep reasoning, client communication, and experimentation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing benchmarks do not adequately measure whether an AI system can deliver a production-grade agent under real client-engagement conditions, even as agent construction is increasingly being delegated to coding agents.

Method: The benchmark spans 53 tasks across four domains. A developer agent receives business records, a client with requirements, a production API, an inherited codebase, and constraints on serving cost and models. The agent must build a customer-service agent, which is scored by deployment against held-out simulated users. Results are compared against an expert-authored reference ceiling.

Result: The strongest configuration (Claude Opus 5 under Claude Code) passes only 23.9% of evaluation simulations, while an expert-authored reference achievement scores 82.2%. Failure patterns mirror human agent-developer mistakes: shallow queries instead of deep record comprehension, minimal client communication, and insufficient experimentation with agent architecture and serving budget.

Conclusion: ττ-bench aims to turn cooperative agent building into a measurable benchmark target for coding agents, highlighting a substantial gap between current model capabilities and expert-level performance in realistic agent-development engagements.

Abstract: LLM agents are rapidly becoming production software, deployed to handle customer service, adjudicate disputes, and operate internal systems. Notably, the work of building them is increasingly handed to coding agents, yet existing benchmarks say little about whether an AI system can deliver one under the conditions of a real client engagement. We introduce $τ^τ$-bench (pronounced hyper-tau-bench), a benchmark that makes agent construction the task. A developer agent is given the records a business actually keeps, a client who holds requirements, a production API that operations must run through, a codebase to inherit, and limits on serving cost and models: the same starting point a real engagement provides. From these it must deliver a complete customer-service agent, scored by deploying that agent against held-out simulated users. Across 53 tasks spanning four domains, the strongest configuration, Claude Opus 5 under Claude Code, passes just 23.9% of evaluation simulations. Meanwhile, an expert-authored reference ceiling scores 82.2%. The failures mirror ones human agent developers see: models issue shallow queries in place of deep comprehension of the records, communicate almost nothing to the client, and experiment too little with agent architecture and serving spend, shipping the first design that runs. We aim for $τ^τ$-bench to turn the work of cooperative agent building into a measurable target for coding agents.

</details>


### [26] [Leveraging Imperfect Restoration for Data Availability Attack](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.04627)
*Yi Huang,Jeremy Styborski,Mingzhi Lyu,Fan Wang,Adams Kong*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper proposes Imperfect Restoration Poisoning (IRP), a novel data availability attack that achieves strong poisoning effects against both supervised and self-supervised learning while preserving high image quality, outperforming existing baselines including the state-of-the-art CUDA method.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing Data Availability Attacks (DAAs) often excel against either Supervised Learning (SL) or Self-Supervised Learning (SSL), but not both. The current best model-free approach, CUDA, has underwhelming effectiveness against SSL and suffers from a severe trade-off between image quality and poisoning effect, motivating the need for a better method.

Method: The authors first conduct a theoretical analysis of CUDA to uncover its sub-optimal gradients and the strategy it uses to induce class-wise bias for data poisoning. Based on these insights, they design IRP, a novel poisoning method that aims to preserve high image quality while achieving strong poisoning effects across both SL and SSL scenarios.

Result: Extensive experiments comparing IRP against eight baselines across both SL and SSL settings, along with evaluations against five representative defense methods, demonstrate the superiority of IRP.

Conclusion: IRP establishes itself as a superior data availability attack that overcomes CUDA's limitations by combining high image quality with strong poisoning effectiveness across both supervised and self-supervised learning paradigms.

Abstract: The abundance of online data is at risk of unauthorized usage in training deep learning models. To counter this, various Data Availability Attacks (DAAs) have been devised to make data unlearnable for such models by subtly perturbing the training data. However, existing attacks often excel against either Supervised Learning (SL) or Self-Supervised Learning (SSL) scenarios. Among these, a model-free approach that generates a Convolution-based Unlearnable Dataset (CUDA) stands out as the most robust DAA across both SSL and SL. Nonetheless, CUDA's effectiveness against SSL is underwhelming and it faces a severe trade-off between image quality and its poisoning effect. In this paper, we conduct a theoretical analysis of CUDA, uncovering the sub-optimal gradients it introduces and elucidating the strategy it employs to induce class-wise bias for data poisoning. Building on this, we propose a novel poisoning method named Imperfect Restoration Poisoning (IRP), aiming to preserve high image quality while achieving strong poisoning effects. Through extensive comparisons of IRP with eight baselines across SL and SSL, coupled with evaluations alongside five representative defense methods, we showcase the superiority of IRP. Code: https://github.com/lyumingzhi/IRP

</details>


### [27] [SiLR: Structure-Preserving Admission and Process Reward for LLM Tool Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.04629)
*Chenyu Zhou,Qiliang Jiang,Shuning Wu,Xu Zhou*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper introduces SiLR, a runtime gate for LLM tool agents that uses a product order over branch-level violation states instead of a scalar aggregate score. The authors prove no scalar surrogate is sound for this admission order, and demonstrate that SiLR significantly outperforms terminal and scalar gates on Gym-ANM and CityLearn benchmarks, is robust to magnitude-redistribution attacks, and outperforms scalar projections when used as a GRPO process reward.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing runtime gates for LLM tool agents in a ReAct loop are cast as filters with scalar aggregate scoring. The authors identify the "scalar projection trap": aggregate-score gates accept locally improving proposals and commit trajectories to plateaus, particularly under post-violation recovery where progress must be admitted while the system remains in violation. They argue the failure is representational rather than a tunable threshold issue.

Method: SiLR shadow-executes each proposal and admits it under a product order over the branch-level violation state, considering overloaded-branch support and per-branch severity together, rather than collapsing them into a scalar score. The authors prove that no scalar surrogate is sound for this product order, establishing the impossibility of scalar approaches. Evaluation is conducted on mined Gym-ANM scenarios and CityLearn across three model families, including robustness testing against magnitude-redistribution attacks and dual-constraint-family scenarios. SiLR is also reused as a GRPO process reward and compared against scalar projections.

Result: SiLR recovers 21/21 multi-action episodes compared to 0/21 for terminal gating and 9/21 for the best scalar gate on mined Gym-ANM scenarios. The terminal-versus-structured dichotomy holds across three model families and CityLearn. Under a magnitude-redistribution attack, both scalar and support-only baselines are defeated while the full per-branch predicate contains the attack. With two constraint families active, every scalar projection admits physically unsafe actions; support-only admits 63.2% of 42,410 actions (product order admits 0). As a GRPO process reward, SiLR outperforms its count projection in every mined scenario and is the only tested reward whose ungated policy exceeds the untrained base (0.844 vs. 0.778).

Conclusion: Scalar projection fundamentally loses the violation geometry at both design points (gating and reward), and only the full product order over branch-level violation states is structurally sufficient for safe and effective admission in LLM tool agents. The result establishes a representational impossibility result for scalar gates and demonstrates the necessity of structured, per-branch violation predicates.

Abstract: A runtime gate for an LLM tool agent is usually cast as a filter. In a ReAct loop a rejected proposal is followed by another at the same state, so the gate is a search operator over the proposal stream whose admission criterion shapes which trajectories are reachable. We study post-violation recovery admission, where progress must be admitted while the system is still in violation, and identify the scalar projection trap: an aggregate-score gate accepts a locally improving proposal and commits the trajectory to a plateau. SiLR instead shadow-executes each proposal and admits it under a product order over the branch-level violation state (overloaded-branch support and per-branch severity). We prove that no scalar surrogate is sound for this order, so the failure is representational, not a matter of threshold tuning. On mined Gym-ANM scenarios, SiLR recovers 21/21 multi-action episodes against 0/21 for terminal and 9/21 for the best scalar gate, significant across the full 24-scenario benchmark. The terminal-versus-structured dichotomy holds across three model families and in CityLearn. Because admission rests on deterministic simulation, the LLM lies outside the trust boundary: a magnitude-redistribution attack that defeats both scalar and support-only baselines is contained only by the full per-branch predicate. With two constraint families active, every tested scalar projection admits physically unsafe actions; support-only admits the largest fraction (63.2% of 42,410; product order 0). In the hardest dual-family traces, scalar gates recover only through that unsafe class. Reused as a GRPO process reward, it outperforms its count projection in every mined scenario and is the only tested reward whose ungated policy exceeds the untrained base (0.844 vs. 0.778). Scalar projection loses the violation geometry at both design points; only the full product order is structurally sufficient.

</details>


### [28] [A Cost-Aware Agentic Architecture for NL-to-SQL over Nested Enterprise Schemas, with a New Benchmark](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.04641)
*Yoga Sri Varshan Varadharajan,Ajay Yadav,Ritesh Goru,Prateek Chaudhury,Constantine Caramanis,Prateek Jain,Divyateja Pasupuleti,Sunil Kumar Pandey*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper introduces the DevRev NL2SQL benchmark (900 execution-verified queries on graph-like, deeply nested enterprise schemas) with the Semantic Depth Score (SDS) rubric, and presents a cost-aware single-generation agentic architecture for schema selection, metadata retrieval, and error repair that achieves 91.7% answer correctness on this benchmark (54.6 points above the next-best baseline) and is competitive with leading systems on Spider 2.0 Snowflake.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing NL2SQL benchmarks focus on academic datasets that do not reflect the graph-like, semi-structured, deeply nested schema complexity found in production enterprise environments, leaving a gap between benchmark performance and real-world applicability.

Method: Two complementary contributions: (1) the DevRev NL2SQL benchmark comprising 900 execution-verified queries with nested-type and link-graph structure, plus a schema-agnostic Semantic Depth Score (SDS) rubric for analytical reasoning depth; (2) a cost-aware single-generation agentic architecture with dedicated schema-selection, metadata-retrieval, and error-repair components tailored to enterprise schema requirements.

Result: The proposed system achieves 91.7% answer correctness on the DevRev NL2SQL benchmark, a 54.6 percentage-point margin over the next-best baseline; on Spider 2.0 Snowflake it remains competitive with leading systems at a single-generation operating point.

Conclusion: Production-grade NL2SQL requires benchmarks and architectures that account for nested and graph-structured enterprise schemas; the DevRev NL2SQL benchmark with SDS provides a suitable evaluation regime, and the cost-aware single-generation agentic approach demonstrates strong accuracy and efficiency.

Abstract: Natural-language-to-SQL systems have ad- vanced rapidly on academic benchmarks, yet production enterprise schemas exhibit graph- like, semi-structured, deeply nested structure that current benchmarks do not measure. We make two complementary contributions. First, we introduce the DevRev NL2SQL bench- mark: 900 execution-verified queries with nested-type and link-graph structure, accom- panied by the Semantic Depth Score (SDS), a schema-agnostic rubric for analytical reasoning depth. Second, we present a cost-aware single- generation agentic architecture whose schema- selection, metadata-retrieval, and error-repair components are designed for the requirements this regime imposes. On the DevRev NL2SQL benchmark the system attains 91.7% answer correctness, a margin of 54.6 percentage points over the next-best baseline; on the Spider 2.0 Snowflake public dataset, it is competitive with leading systems at a single-generation operating point.

</details>


### [29] [Continual Graph Memory for Adaptive Recommendation under Intent Drift](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.04651)
*Hao Nguyen Ngoc,Tung Nguyen,Nguyen Thi Hanh,Hoang Thai Dinh,Nguyen Xuan Tung*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: CGM-Rec is a continual graph memory framework for adaptive recommendation under intent drift. It maintains a Semantic Graph Memory (conservatively updated via quality-gated typed operations for stable knowledge) and an Episodic Lesson Memory (fast-reactive memory for recent outcomes and failures). During testing, parameters remain frozen and adaptation occurs only through memory writes. Under a frozen-parameter, one-pass reranking protocol, CGM-Rec outperforms neural and LLM baselines, improving HR@1 by up to 29.58% over the strongest LLM baseline on Bundle and achieving HR@5 of 0.5941 on ML-100K.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Traditional KG-enhanced recommendation systems treat knowledge graphs as a static retrieval substrate, which is brittle to evolving user intents, noisy metadata, and recurring failure patterns. The paper addresses adaptive recommendation under intent drift where feedback reveals whether relational evidence is useful, missing, or misleading.

Method: CGM-Rec treats the graph state as writable memory with two complementary components: (1) a Semantic Graph Memory updated conservatively through quality-gated typed operations for storing stable, high-confidence relational knowledge, and (2) an Episodic Lesson Memory acting as fast reactive memory that learns recent outcomes, failure cases, and corrective hints. At test time, model parameters remain frozen, and adaptation occurs solely through memory writes.

Result: Under a frozen-parameter, one-pass reranking protocol across multiple recommendation settings, CGM-Rec improves over evaluated neural and LLM-based baselines on most metrics. Under sampled-candidate reranking, CGM-Rec improves HR@1 by up to 29.58% over the strongest LLM baseline on Bundle, and outperforms K-RagRec on metadata-rich ML-100K with HR@5 of 0.5941 versus 0.4746.

Conclusion: CGM-Rec demonstrates that decoupling semantic graph evolution from episodic adaptation in a writable memory framework enables effective adaptive recommendation under intent drift without updating model parameters, outperforming both neural and LLM-based baselines.

Abstract: This paper studies adaptive recommendation under intent drift, where feedback from each recommendation outcome can reveal whether the relational evidence used for ranking is useful, missing, or misleading. While Knowledge Graphs (KGs) provide essential semantic structure to handle these shifts, traditional KG-enhanced systems treat the graph as a static retrieval substrate, making it brittle to evolving intents, noisy metadata, and recurring failure patterns. This paper proposes CGM-Rec, a continual graph memory framework for adaptive recommendation. CGM-Rec treats the graph state as a writable memory and maintains two complementary components. Therein, a Semantic Graph Memory is updated conservatively through quality-gated typed operations for storing stable and high-confidence relational knowledge. Meanwhile, an Episodic Lesson Memory acts as a fast reactive memory that learns recent outcomes, failure cases, and corrective hints. During testing, model parameters remain frozen and adaptation occurs only through memory writes. We evaluate CGM-Rec under a frozen-parameter, one-pass reranking protocol, where encoders and prompts remain fixed during testing and adaptation occurs only through memory writes. Experiments across multiple recommendation settings show that CGM-Rec improves over evaluated neural and LLM-based baselines on most metrics. Particularly, under sampled-candidate reranking, CGM-Rec improves HR@1 by up to 29.58% over the strongest LLM baseline on Bundle, and outperforms K-RagRec on metadata-rich ML-100K with HR@5 of 0.5941 versus 0.4746.

</details>


### [30] [Harness-agnostic detection and immunization of reward hacking in self-evolving language models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.04665)
*Rongxin Yang,Yang Liu,Shang Luo,Haoxuan Jia,Chongyang Zhang,Hao Zheng,Yingguang Yang,Yulin Huang,Jianshen Zhang,Yongzhi Qi,Kefu Xu,Congjing Ran,Bin Chong*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: HackProbe is a black-box monitoring system that detects reward hacking in self-evolving language models by maintaining a frozen comparison core with a rotated fresh layer, using four proxy-based tests with Sidak-corrected p-values, plus a bandwidth-limited reselection layer for risk-aware immunization.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Self-evolving language models that optimize imperfect proxy scores are vulnerable to reward hacking, where sustained selection over time widens the gap between the proxy and the true capability. Existing approaches lack effective detection and mitigation for this problem in black-box settings where weights and activations are inaccessible.

Method: HackProbe attaches to arbitrary self-evolving loops via two black-box hooks. It uses a secret, distribution-fixed comparison core for cross-generation comparability plus a rotated fresh layer to resist co-adaptation. Four tests cover level gap, scale-aligned divergence with online change-point detection, capability stagnation, and conditional confidently-wrong rate; Sidak correction yields calibrated family-wise p-values. A risk-aware immunization layer reselects from the proposal pool using the core and a structural gaming footprint, disclosing at most log2 Pi bits per generation.

Result: On a controlled prompt-level host with four injected hacking channels, HackProbe achieves 0.763 AUROC vs 0.663 for the best baseline, and reduces false-positive rate from 0.706 to 0.434. Bandwidth-limited reselection is the only immunization level gaining more under hacking (+5.2 points) than it loses on clean runs (-4.7), though per-channel effects are mostly not individually significant.

Conclusion: The paper proves a detectability bound linking target error rates to required probe size, characterizes the benefits and limits of probe rotation, and demonstrates that HackProbe can both detect reward hacking in black-box settings and selectively immunize self-evolving models against it without sacrificing more capability on clean runs than it preserves under attack.

Abstract: Self-evolving language models improve by proposing candidate updates and keeping whatever raises a visible score. When that score is an imperfect proxy for the capability one actually wants, sustained selection widens the gap between the two. This is reward hacking. We introduce HackProbe, a monitor that attaches to an arbitrary self-evolving loop through two black-box hooks, with no access to weights or activations. It keeps a secret, distribution-fixed comparison core, whose frozen distribution makes its capability proxy comparable across generations, alongside a rotated fresh layer that hardens the bank against co-adaptation. Four tests built on that proxy cover the level gap, a scale-aligned divergence with online change-point detection, capability stagnation, and a conditional confidently-wrong rate; a Sidak correction turns them into a calibrated family-wise p-value. Diagnosis alone recovers nothing, so a risk-aware immunization layer reselects an honest candidate from the proposal pool using the core together with a purely structural gaming footprint, disclosing at most log2 Pi bits per generation to the host. We prove a detectability bound that converts a target error rate into an explicit probe-size budget, and we delimit what probe rotation does and does not buy. On a controlled prompt-level host with four injected hacking channels and ground-truth labels, HackProbe reaches 0.763 AUROC against 0.663 for the strongest baseline and cuts the false-positive rate from 0.706 to 0.434. Its bandwidth-limited reselection is the only immunization level that returns more true capability under hacking, 5.2 points on average, than it forfeits on clean runs, 4.7; per-channel effects are mostly not individually significant.

</details>


### [31] [ERPBench: Evaluating LLM Agents for Enterprise Decision-Making Across Competitive Market Ecologies](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.04667)
*Xinran Zhang,Pengrui Lu,Lyumanshan Ye,Pengfei Liu*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: ERPBench is a novel execution-instrumented benchmark for enterprise decision agents, testing whether LLM-agent performance conclusions transfer across two matched competitive market ecologies (Solo vs. Arena) within a six-round ERP simulation. Results show that the leading model differs across ecologies—DeepSeek wins Solo while Gemini wins Arena—and task-level winners agree on only 21 of 100 problems, demonstrating that enterprise-agent rankings are not reliably transferable across competitive market settings.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing evaluations of LLM agents for enterprise workflows rarely test whether business-decision conclusions (i.e., which agents perform best) generalize across different competitive market ecologies. This gap undermines confidence in benchmark-driven agent selection for real-world enterprise deployment.

Method: ERPBench uses a six-round enterprise resource planning (ERP) simulation with coupled pricing, production, procurement, inventory, finance, and shared-market competition. It evaluates 100 fixed problems in two matched competitive ecologies: Solo (each evaluated LLM agent competes against fixed rule-based opponents) and Arena (six evaluated LLM agents compete in a shared market). Across six model families, 1,200 model-level trajectories spanning 7,200 decision rounds are generated. Execution-intervention analysis is supplemented.

Result: The leading model differs between ecologies: DeepSeek leads in Solo (252.29M mean valuation, mean rank 1.67) while Gemini leads in Arena (263.95M, mean rank 1.76). The two ecologies identify the same task-level winner on only 21 of 100 problems. Gemini's bottom-rank rate drops from 22% in Solo to 0% in Arena.

Conclusion: Enterprise-agent rankings do not reliably transfer across competitive market ecologies. ERPBench provides a platform for paired evaluation that controls for problem content while varying competitive market structure, enabling principled assessment of whether business-decision conclusions generalize.

Abstract: Large language model (LLM) agents are increasingly proposed for enterprise workflows, yet existing evaluations rarely test whether business-decision conclusions transfer across competitive market ecologies. We introduce ERPBench, an execution-instrumented benchmark for enterprise decision agents in a six-round Enterprise Resource Planning (ERP) simulation with coupled pricing, production, procurement, inventory, finance, and shared-market competition. ERPBench evaluates the same 100 fixed problems in two matched competitive market ecologies: Solo, where each evaluated LLM agent competes against fixed rule-based opponents, and Arena, where six evaluated LLM agents compete in a shared market. Across six model families, this yields 1,200 model-level trajectories spanning 7,200 decision rounds. Under the observed service configuration, the leading model differs between ecologies: DeepSeek leads in Solo (252.29M mean valuation; mean rank 1.67), whereas Gemini leads in Arena (263.95M; 1.76). The two ecologies identify the same task-level winner on only 21 of 100 problems, and Gemini's bottom-rank rate falls from 22 % to 0 % in Arena. ERPBench supports paired evaluation of whether enterprise-agent rankings transfer across competitive market ecologies, supplemented by aggregate execution-intervention analysis. Code and benchmark resources are available in our https://github.com/GAIR-NLP/erp-bench.

</details>


### [32] [Train What You Deploy:Token-Faithful Post-Training of a Production Coding](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.04678)
*Cheng Li,Jiexiong Liu,Yixuan Chen,Chi Hong*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper presents a fidelity-aware training coupling framework for coding and terminal agents that corrects token/control fidelity errors in existing post-training pipelines, plus Certified Divergence Proximal Policy Optimization (C-DPPO), which provides tight TV certification bounds, adaptive-K rules, budget-aware guarantees, and error-robust policy masking. Evaluated on Baize5B/Baize10B with TMax-100, C-DPPO achieves a consistent +3.0-point gain over standard DPPO.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing post-training pipelines for coding and terminal agents suffer from two key defects: (1) simplified training environments that mismatch production deployments, and (2) offline token reconstruction from agent logs that distorts original prompts and conflates policy calls with background model operations.

Method: The authors propose (1) a fidelity-aware training coupling framework that retains trainer-side sampling over original prompts, eliminates spurious model calls via a negotiated training protocol, and restricts loss computation to verifiable token spans with closed-failure guarantees; and (2) C-DPPO, which adds tight two-sided TV certification bounds, adaptive-K rules, budget-aware sequence guarantees, and error-robust policy masking atop standard DPPO.

Result: On matched Baize5B and Baize10B models with identical training and test protocols on TMax-100, C-DPPO yields a consistent +3.0-point performance gain over standard DPPO across both model scales. Certificate audits validate reliability and full operational coverage.

Conclusion: The certified training pipeline demonstrates that fidelity-aware training with certified divergence proximal policy optimization improves performance and reliability of coding/terminal agent post-training, with certificate audits confirming operational coverage.

Abstract: Existing post-training pipelines for coding and terminal agents suffer severe token and control fidelity errors: simplified training environments mismatch production deployments, and offline token reconstruction from agent logs distorts original prompts and conflates policy calls with background model operations. We present a fidelity-aware training coupling framework that retains trainer-side sampling over original prompts, eliminates spurious model calls via a negotiated training protocol, and restricts loss computation to verifiable token spans with closed-failure guarantees. We further propose Certified Divergence Proximal Policy Optimization (C-DPPO), which establishes tight two-sided TV certification bounds, adaptive-K rules, budget-aware sequence guarantees, and error-robust policy masking atop standard DPPO. Evaluated on matched Baize5B and Baize10B models with identical training and test protocols on TMax-100, C-DPPO yields a consistent +3.0-point performance gain over standard DPPO across model scales. Certificate audits validate the reliability and full operational coverage of our certified training pipeline.

</details>


### [33] [Predicting Spatiotemporal Mobile Sensing-Based PM2.5 Concentrations Using Low-Rank Adapted Spatially Attentive Graph Neural Network](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.04693)
*Om Chiddarwar,Priyanka Mandal,Praveen Kumar Chandaliya,Shriniwas Arkatkar*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper introduces a mobile-sensing PM2.5 dataset from Surat, India, and proposes SA-GNN (Spatial-Attention Graph Neural Network), a hybrid model combining cluster-specific GRUs and a Graph Attention Network, achieving R²=0.95, RMSE=6.8, MAE=4.2 µg/m³ for fine-grained short-term PM2.5 forecasting and hotspot identification, outperforming LSTM, RNN, GRU, and ANN baselines.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Urban air quality varies significantly along transit corridors, and existing forecasting models (LSTM, RNN, GRU, ANN) perform well on low-resolution data but fail to capture rapidly changing patterns in urban air quality. High-resolution monitoring and forecasting are needed for real-time, fine-grained air quality management.

Method: A novel mobile-sensing dataset was collected from Surat, Gujarat, India containing PM2.5, meteorological variables, and land-use features. Spatiotemporal data was represented as a graph using two node-definition strategies: (i) uniform segmentation at 200-400m intervals and (ii) DBSCAN clustering for adaptive grouping of dense observations. Rolling mean and standard deviation of meteorological variables were computed per node. The proposed SA-GNN combines cluster-specific GRUs to capture localized temporal dependencies with a Graph Attention Network to learn spatial heterogeneity.

Result: SA-GNN achieved R²=0.95, RMSE=6.8, and MAE=4.2 µg/m³ on the Surat dataset, outperforming all baseline models (LSTM, RNN, GRU, ANN), which struggled with rapidly changing urban air quality patterns under high resolution.

Conclusion: Combining spatial clustering with adaptive graph attention significantly improves PM2.5 forecasting accuracy, enabling real-time, fine-grained monitoring that supports personalized exposure tracking and timely alerts for healthier cities.

Abstract: Urban air quality can vary significantly along transit corridors, necessitating high-resolution monitoring. This work introduces a novel mobile-sensing dataset from Surat, Gujarat, India, comprising PM$*{2.5}$ concentrations, meteorological variables (temperature, humidity, wind speed, wind direction), and land-use features. To represent the spatiotemporal data as a graph, two node-definition strategies were used: (i) uniform segmentation (200--400~m intervals) and (ii) DBSCAN clustering to adaptively group dense observations. For each node, rolling mean and standard deviation of meteorological variables were computed. To model this high-dimensional data, we propose a SA-GNN for fine-grained, short-term PM$*{2.5}$ forecasting and hotspot identification. We compared SA-GNN with LSTM, RNN, GRU, and ANN models. These models performed well on low-resolution data but had difficulty capturing rapidly changing patterns in urban air quality. SA-GNN employs cluster-specific GRUs to capture localized temporal dependencies and a Graph Attention Network to learn spatial heterogeneity. This hybrid architecture effectively models rapid fluctuations and complex spatial interactions. On our dataset, SA-GNN achieved $R^2 = 0.95$, RMSE $= 6.8$, and MAE $= 4.2~\si{\micro\gram\per\meter\cubed}$, outperforming all baseline models. Combining spatial clustering with adaptive attention significantly improves forecasting, enabling real-time, fine-grained monitoring and supporting personalized exposure tracking and timely alerts for healthier cities.

</details>


### [34] [SQL-Zero: Self-Evolving Text-to-SQL](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.04697)
*Daniel Machado Pedrozo,Julia Soares Dollis,Bryan Lincoln Marques de Oliveira,Vinicius Alboneti Aguiar,Sávio Salvarino Teles de Oliveira,Telma Woerle de Lima Soares*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: SQL-Zero trains a competitive Text-to-SQL solver using zero human-annotated NL/SQL pairs via proposer-solver self-play, where a challenger generates SQL-paired questions calibrated to the solver's difficulty and both are alternately updated with GRPO against execution fidelity on the database. It improves over zero-shot baselines on BIRD dev and shows scale-dependent transfer to unseen databases.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Human-annotated natural-language/SQL pairs are expensive, domain-specific, and a bottleneck for scaling Text-to-SQL agents to new databases. The paper asks whether a competitive solver can be trained with zero annotated pairs, using only execution against the database itself as ground truth.

Method: Introduces SQL-Zero, a proposer-solver self-play framework. A challenger and solver start from the same base LLM. The challenger generates SQL-natural-language pairs calibrated to target the solver's current difficulty ("hard but solvable"), and both roles are updated via GRPO in alternating turns. A template-level repetition penalty is applied to the challenger to prevent diversity collapse.

Result: On BIRD databases with no labels, self-play improves over zero-shot base by 6.6 points at 3B and 7.3 points at 7B on BIRD dev. It outperforms a matched control trained on human BIRD gold pairs under the same recipe, though an exact paired test does not resolve the margin. Transfer is scale-dependent: at 3B every iteration outperforms the base on unseen Spider and under lexical perturbation (Spider-Syn), degrading less than the BIRD-gold control; at 7B only the first iteration preserves transfer.

Conclusion: A competitive Text-to-SQL solver can be trained entirely without human-annotated pairs by self-play grounded in database execution. The approach matches or exceeds a gold-trained control in-domain, but its generalization to unseen databases depends on model scale, indicating a limitation of pure self-play at larger scales.

Abstract: Training a competitive Text-to-SQL agent usually depends on human-annotated natural-language/SQL pairs, which are expensive, domain-specific, and a bottleneck for scaling to new databases. We show it is possible to train a competitive solver with zero annotated pairs. We introduce SQL-Zero, a proposer-solver self-play in which a challenger and a solver start from the same base LLM and the only ground truth is execution against the database itself. The challenger generates SQL pairs calibrated to the solver's current difficulty (targeting "hard but solvable"), and both roles are updated with GRPO in alternating turns, with a template-level repetition penalty on the challenger to prevent diversity collapse. Training on BIRD databases with no labels, self-play improves over the zero-shot base on BIRD dev by 6.6 points at 3B and 7.3 points at 7B. It also scores higher than a matched control trained under the same recipe on human BIRD gold over the same databases, although an exact paired test does not resolve that margin. Transfer depends on scale: at 3B every iteration outperforms the base on unseen Spider databases and under lexical perturbation (Spider-Syn), where it also degrades less than the matched BIRD-gold control, whereas at 7B only the first iteration preserves transfer.

</details>


### [35] [Model Retirement Creates Reproducibility Risk in Biomedical AI Publications](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.04699)
*Nathan Wolfrath,Meghan Conroy,Thomas Kosten,Dave Bell,Bhabishya Neupane,Jonah Kindel,Anjishnu Banerjee,Priya Deshpande,Bradley Taylor,Anai N. Kothari*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: A study analyzing 8,931 paper-model mentions across 5,242 biomedical publications (2022-2026) found that 42% of LLM citations involve models already retired or scheduled for retirement within two years of publication, with a median publication-to-retirement interval of 538 days, raising serious concerns about computational reproducibility in biomedical research.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Commercial LLMs are being rapidly adopted in biomedical research, but the deprecation schedules of commercial hosting services threaten scientific reproducibility. The authors aim to quantify how widespread this problem is by analyzing the lifecycle status of LLMs cited in biomedical publications.

Method: The authors searched PubMed for original research articles (2022-2026) that applied a specific LLM to a biomedical task. An extraction agent identified model names from 61,077 article abstracts with human validation on a subset. Model names were normalized to canonical identifiers, and lifecycle data (release date, retirement date, status) were compiled for the 50 most frequently used models.

Result: 8,931 paper-model mentions spanning 5,242 unique publications were identified among the 50 most used models. 77.7% of mentions cited commercial closed-weight models, and 42% involved models already retired or scheduled to retire within two years of publication. The median publication-to-retirement interval was 538 days.

Conclusion: Many biomedical publications using LLMs are on a trajectory toward computational non-reproducibility after publication. Model deprecation should be treated as a core reporting and preservation issue for biomedical research.

Abstract: Background. Large language models (LLMs) are being adopted in biomedical research at a rapid and accelerating pace, yet commercial services that host many widely used models operate under deprecation schedules that can complicate scientific reproducibility.
  Methods. We searched PubMed for original research articles from 2022 through March 2026 that applied a specific LLM to a biomedical task. An extraction agent identified model names from 61,077 article abstracts with human reviewers validating a subset for extraction accuracy. Extracted model names were normalized to canonical model identifiers. Lifecycle data (release date, retirement date, status) were compiled for the 50 most frequently used models.
  Results. We identified 8,931 paper-model mentions spanning 5,242 unique publications after restricting the analysis to the 50 most frequently used models. Among these mentions, 77.7% cited a commercial closed-weight model. Overall, 42% involved a model that was already retired by the time of official publication or is scheduled to retire within two years of publication. The median interval from publication to model retirement was 538 days.
  Conclusion. Many biomedical publications using LLMs are on a trajectory toward computational non-reproducibility after publication. Model deprecation should be treated as a core reporting and preservation issue for biomedical research.

</details>


### [36] [FinalityBench: An Effect-Level Benchmark for Agent Decisions Under Delayed and Conflicting Financial Finality](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.04706)
*Abhishek Sharma*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper introduces FinalityBench, an executable benchmark for agentic exception resolution when multiple financial systems (payment processor, ledger, ERP, bank feed) hold contradictory views due to faulted message delivery. It grades agents on terminal monetary effects and includes 321 tasks with 45 twin pairs that are observably indistinguishable yet require different dispositions. Results show accuracy ranking disagrees with paired-loss ranking, and a runtime gate on authoritative finality probes achieves the best paired loss (85.4%), with LMs matching that accuracy but losing twice as much money.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Real-world financial systems experience message delays, duplication, drops, and reordering that cause four interdependent systems to hold contradictory beliefs about the same order. An agent must make consequential, sometimes irreversible decisions—whether to ship, re-capture, refund, or wait—without knowing how pending captures will resolve. Existing benchmarks fail to capture this decision's ambiguity and monetary consequences, motivating a benchmark with executable semantics and genuine indistinguishability.

Method: FinalityBench maintains a hidden canonical event log and derives each of four system views from separately faulted delivery streams, so disagreements arise from specified fault semantics. Grading evaluates executed monetary effects—an episode's score equals the merchant's terminal economic position relative to a privileged reference that knows how the pending capture resolves. The corpus includes 45 twin pairs (90 tasks) whose four system views and authoritative probes are identical at decision instant yet require different correct dispositions; indistinguishability is checked under every evaluation seed. Nine programmatic policies plus language models are evaluated across 14,445 graded episodes.

Result: Ranking by single-task accuracy and by paired loss disagree in 7 places across 14,445 episodes from nine programmatic policies. A ship-on-first-sign policy achieves 65.7% accuracy (second-best) but worst paired loss because it cannot distinguish twin pairs. A runtime gating policy that gates irreversible actions on an authoritative finality probe reaches 85.4% and, unlike polling policies, loses nothing on pass^5. Its residual loss is concentrated in one archetype that prices finality information. Language models match the hand-written gate's accuracy on a stratified subset but lose about twice as much money, while discovering the finality-gating strategy unprompted.

Conclusion: Accuracy alone is a misleading metric for agentic financial exception resolution; paired-loss evaluation reveals systematic failures in distinguishing observable-identical tasks with divergent outcomes. Runtime finality gating is the dominant strategy, minimizing irreversible monetary loss, but even it fails on tasks where finality information must be priced directly. Language models can recover this strategy but translate it into financially weaker behavior, suggesting that future work should focus on bridging strategic understanding and monetary decision-making quality.

Abstract: A merchant's payment processor, ledger, ERP and bank feed are updated by messages that get delayed, duplicated, dropped and reordered, so for minutes at a time the four hold contradictory beliefs about the same order. An agent resolving the exception must decide whether to ship goods, re-submit a capture, refund or wait, knowing some of those cannot be undone. We present FinalityBench, an executable benchmark for that decision. It keeps a hidden canonical event log and derives each system's view from a separately faulted delivery stream, so disagreement follows from specified fault semantics rather than being authored. Grading is on executed monetary effects: an episode is scored by the merchant's terminal economic position, relative to a privileged reference told when the pending capture resolves. The corpus of 321 tasks includes 45 twin pairs (90 tasks): tasks whose four system views are identical at the decision instant, whose authoritative probes both return unknown, and whose eventual correct dispositions differ. That snapshot indistinguishability is checked under every evaluation seed rather than assumed; equivalence over all interaction traces is not claimed. Over 14,445 graded episodes from nine programmatic policies, ranking by single-task accuracy and by paired loss disagree in 7 places: a ship-on-first-sign policy is second-best by accuracy at 65.7% and worst in the suite by paired loss, because it cannot tell the two members apart. A runtime gating irreversible actions on an authoritative finality probe reaches 85.4% and, unlike every polling policy, loses nothing to pass^5; its residual loss is almost entirely one archetype, which prices finality information directly. Language models reach the same exact rate as the hand-written gate on a stratified subset, lose about twice as much money, and discover the finality-gating strategy without being told it.

</details>


### [37] [PLUME: Parameter-Efficient Personalization of Large Language Models via Low-Rank User Modulation in Shared Subspaces](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.04715)
*Xinyu Li,Hao Zhou,Jianfeng Zhu,Julina Maharjan,Ruixin Guo,Feodor Dragan,Ruoming Jin*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: PLUME is a lightweight framework for LLM personalization that trains only a small square matrix within a shared task-specific subspace per user, reducing per-user parameters by over 95% while achieving comparable or superior performance to strong baselines.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Per-user fine-tuning improves LLM personalization quality but introduces significant parameter and storage overhead, limiting scalability to large user populations.

Method: PLUME learns a global task subspace from aggregated user data, then achieves personalization by training only a lightweight small square matrix within this subspace while keeping shared components fixed. It further introduces cross-layer shared parameters and rank-1 residual terms to reduce redundancy while maintaining expressiveness.

Result: Experiments on multiple personalized text generation benchmarks show PLUME achieves comparable or superior performance to strong baselines while reducing per-user parameters by over 95%.

Conclusion: Shared-subspace modulation with minimal residuals is a scalable and semantically grounded approach to LLM personalization.

Abstract: Personalizing large language models (LLMs) is essential for delivering AI assistance that aligns with individual users' styles, intents, and preferences. While per-user fine-tuning can substantially enhance personalization quality, it introduces significant parameter and storage overhead, limiting scalability to large user populations. We propose PLUME (Personalized Low-Rank Adaptation through User Modulation and Shared Subspace), a lightweight framework that achieves efficient and expressive per-user adaptation by leveraging a shared task-specific subspace. Specifically, PLUME first learns a global task subspace from aggregated user data. Personalization is then achieved by training only a lightweight small square matrix within this subspace, enabling each user to obtain a tailored model while keeping shared components fixed. Cross-layer shared parameters and rank-1 residual terms are further introduced to significantly reduce redundancy while maintaining expressiveness. Experiments on multiple personalized text generation benchmarks demonstrate that PLUME achieves comparable or superior performance to strong baselines, while reducing per-user parameters by over 95%. These results establish shared-subspace modulation with minimal residuals as a scalable and semantically grounded approach to LLM personalization.

</details>


### [38] [Aplaud: Adaptive Personalized Low-Rank Decomposition for User-Specific LLM](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.04738)
*Xinyu Li,Ruoming Jin,Jianfeng Zhu,Ruixin Guo,Zhi Liu*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Aplaud is a lightweight, scalable framework for personalized survey response prediction using fine-tuned LLMs. It extends LoRA by decomposing user adaptation into a frozen shared low-rank basis, a compact user-specific correction, and a rank-one residual, with optional further factorization of the correction to reduce parameter cost and overfitting. It outperforms existing LoRA-based personalized LLM approaches in generalization and inference efficiency.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Personalized survey response prediction with LLMs faces unique challenges: limited per-user training data, scalability of model storage for many users, and the need to leverage shared structure across survey questions. Existing LoRA-based personalization methods lack an efficient mechanism to balance shared knowledge with individual user adaptation.

Method: Aplaud extends the LoRA paradigm by separating user adaptation into three components: (1) a frozen, shared low-rank basis capturing common structure across users/questions, (2) a compact user-specific correction matrix, which can be further factorized into an even lower-rank form to reduce parameter cost and mitigate overfitting, and (3) a rank-one residual for finer-grained personalization. This nested decomposition enables lightweight adaptive personalization while maintaining shared model efficiency.

Result: Empirical results demonstrate that Aplaud achieves efficient and scalable personalization across users, outperforming state-of-the-art LoRA-based personalized LLM approaches in both generalization performance and inference efficiency.

Conclusion: Aplaud provides an effective solution to the personalized survey prediction problem, demonstrating that a nested low-rank decomposition of LoRA-style adaptation can deliver better generalization with lower per-user parameter and inference costs compared to existing personalized LLM baselines.

Abstract: In this paper, we study the problem of personalized survey response prediction using fine-tuned large language models (LLMs). This task poses unique challenges: limited per-user training data, scalability of model storage, and the need to exploit shared structure across survey questions. To address these issues, we propose Aplaud (Adaptive Personalized Low-rank and User-specific Nested Decomposition), a lightweight and scalable framework for LLM personalization. Aplaud extends the LoRA paradigm by separating adaptation into a frozen, shared low-rank basis and a compact user-specific correction, augmented with a rank-one residual for finer personalization. To further reduce per-user parameter cost and mitigate overfitting, the correction matrix can be factorized into an even lower-rank form. Empirical results demonstrate that Aplaud achieves efficient, scalable personalization across users while outperforming state-of-the-art LoRA-based personalized LLM approaches in both generalization and inference efficiency.

</details>


### [39] [DCFA: Dual-view Causal-inspired Attribution for Failure Reasoning in LLM-based Multi-agent Systems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.04749)
*Zehao Wang,Lanjun Wang,Shilong Jin,Junjie Chen,Yanghua Xiao*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: DCFA is a training-free framework for failure attribution in LLM-based multi-agent systems. It uses structured causal-inspired dependency graphs (global module) and counterfactual-inspired local reasoning to identify the earliest decisive error causing system failure, improving step-level accuracy by up to 8.27% over baselines on the Who&When benchmark.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: LLM-based multi-agent systems are fragile and prone to reasoning and coordination errors causing system-level failures. Existing failure attribution methods suffer from shallow attribution (only catching minor deviations like formatting or retrieval errors) and contextual degradation (model reasoning deteriorates as trace length grows), making it hard to identify the decisive cause of failure.

Method: DCFA consists of two modules: (1) a global module that constructs structured causal-inspired dependency graphs from system traces to identify the initial decisive error, and (2) a local module that applies counterfactual-inspired reasoning to refine the causal attribution. The framework is training-free.

Result: Experiments on the Who&When benchmark across six LLMs show DCFA improves step-level accuracy by up to 8.27% over state-of-the-art baselines.

Conclusion: DCFA effectively addresses both shallow attribution and contextual degradation challenges in failure attribution of multi-agent systems, achieving superior performance over existing methods.

Abstract: Large language model (LLM)-based multi-agent systems have experienced rapid growth in recent years. Despite their promise, such systems remain fragile, frequently exhibiting reasoning and coordination errors that can lead to system-level failures. Failure attribution in such systems relies on tracing natural language interactions among agents to identify the decisive error, which refers to the earliest action whose correction can reverse system failure. There are two key challenges: 1) Shallow attribution: Existing methods often capture only minor deviations, such as incomplete retrievals or formatting errors, which verification mechanisms can correct, while missing the decisive cause of system failure. 2) Contextual degradation: As the length of the system traces increases, the model's reasoning ability rapidly deteriorates. To address these challenges, we propose DCFA, a training-free framework for failure attribution. DCFA integrates a global module that constructs structured causal-inspired dependency graphs from system traces to identify the initial decisive error, and a local module that applies local counterfactual-inspired reasoning to refine causal-inspired attribution. Experiments on the Who&When benchmark across six LLMs show that DCFA improves step-level accuracy by up to 8.27% over state-of-the-art baselines.

</details>


### [40] [Shadow Queries for Private Retrieval in Vector Databases](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.04767)
*Xinguo Feng,Zhongkui Ma,Zihan Wang,Chuan Yan,Guowei Yang,Alsharif Abuadbba,Guangdong Bai*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: SHAQ is a defense against embedding inversion attacks (EIAs) in retrieval-augmented generation systems. Instead of storing original document embeddings, it uses a generative language model to create diverse shadow queries that capture different semantic aspects of documents, then stores these shadow-query embeddings to decompose document semantics and decouple them from source text, achieving substantially improved privacy while preserving or even improving retrieval utility.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: LLMs increasingly use IR systems like RAG that store document embeddings in cloud vector databases. These embeddings are vulnerable to embedding inversion attacks (EIAs) that can reconstruct the original text. Existing defenses (adding noise, scaling embeddings) either provide limited privacy or significantly harm retrieval utility.

Method: SHAQ works by: (1) using a generative language model to create diverse shadow queries capturing different semantic aspects of each document, (2) encoding these shadow queries and storing them in place of the original document embeddings. This semantically decomposes documents and decouples stored embeddings from the source text, breaking the strong coupling between embedding and original text that EIAs exploit.

Result: Across diverse IR datasets: recovery rate as low as 0.2104, defends up to 19.50% more tokens than baseline defenses, achieves up to 0.7967 MAP@10 with up to 5.53% utility improvement.

Conclusion: Semantic decomposition and embedding decoupling provide an effective alternative to directly modifying embeddings for defending against EIAs, substantially improving privacy while preserving and even improving retrieval utility.

Abstract: Large language models (LLMs) increasingly rely on information retrieval (IR) systems, such as Retrieval-Augmented Generation (RAG), to incorporate domain-specific knowledge without costly re-training. These systems often store pre-computed document embeddings in cloud-based vector databases. However, such embeddings are vulnerable to embedding inversion attacks (EIAs), which can reconstruct their underlying text. Existing defenses, such as adding noise or scaling embeddings, often provide limited privacy or significantly reduce retrieval utility.
  We propose SHAQ (shadow query generation), a semantic-decomposition and embedding-decoupling defense against EIAs. SHAQ is based on the insight that EIAs rely on the strong coupling between an embedding and its original text. Instead of storing document embeddings directly, SHAQ uses a generative language model to create diverse shadow queries that capture different semantic aspects of each document. These queries are then encoded and stored in place of the original document embeddings, thereby decomposing document semantics and decoupling stored embeddings from the source text.
  Experiments across diverse IR datasets show that SHAQ substantially improves privacy while preserving retrieval utility, achieving a recovery rate as low as 0.2104, defending up to 19.50% more tokens than baseline defenses, and reaching up to 0.7967 MAP@10 with up to 5.53% utility improvement. These results demonstrate that semantic decomposition and embedding decoupling provide an effective alternative to directly modifying embeddings for defending against EIAs.

</details>


### [41] [Diffusion Language Models for Mobile Edge Agentic AI: Foundations, Applications, and Challenges](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.04778)
*Chenqi Li,Minghui Min,Dusit Niyato,Wei Ni*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This survey paper examines diffusion language models (DLMs) as a non-autoregressive alternative to transformer-based LLMs for mobile edge agentic AI, analyzing their suitability under latency, memory, energy, bandwidth, privacy, and reliability constraints across edge, IoT, and wireless deployments.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Autoregressive LLMs suffer from rigid sequential decoding, which is suboptimal for edge agents that need flexible quality-latency trade-offs, robustness under noisy/incomplete contexts, and low communication overhead. DLMs offer parallel token refinement and bidirectional context exploitation, but their system-level advantages for edge deployment have not been systematically surveyed.

Method: The paper conducts a comprehensive survey that covers DLM foundations, resource-efficient architectures, training/inference acceleration, compression techniques, edge/cloud deployment strategies, communication-aware serving, IoT/wireless applications, and evaluation methodologies for DLM-based agents. It maps DLM modeling properties (bidirectionality, parallel refinement, controllability, quality-latency elasticity) to system-level edge intelligence requirements.

Result: The survey identifies that DLMs enable partial refinement, early exit, and constraint-guided correction—features uniquely suited for edge environments—and reviews the landscape of techniques across architecture, deployment, and communication dimensions, while highlighting open challenges.

Conclusion: Open issues remain in long-context state management, split inference, trustworthy execution, multimodal grounding, and reproducible benchmarking. The paper aims to bridge DLM modeling capabilities with practical system demands for future mobile edge intelligence.

Abstract: Diffusion language models (DLMs) offer a non-autoregressive alternative for mobile edge agentic artificial intelligence (AI) by refining tokens through iterative denoising rather than left-to-right decoding. Compared with autoregressive Transformer-based large language models (LLMs), DLMs can update multiple uncertain tokens in parallel and exploit bidirectional context throughout the generation process, enabling more flexible quality-latency trade-offs beyond fixed sequential decoding. These properties are particularly attractive for edge agents, where partial refinement, early exit, and constraint-guided correction can reduce response delay and communication overhead while improving robustness under noisy, incomplete, or dynamic contexts. This survey reviews DLM foundations and analyzes their suitability for edge settings under latency, memory, energy, bandwidth, privacy, and reliability constraints. We cover resource-efficient architectures, training and inference acceleration, compression, edge/cloud deployment, communication-aware serving, Internet of Things (IoT)/wireless applications, and evaluation of DLM-based agents. We further discuss open issues in long-context state management, split inference, trustworthy execution, multimodal grounding, and reproducible benchmarking. The goal is to connect DLM modeling properties, including bidirectionality, parallel refinement, controllability, and quality-latency elasticity, with system-level requirements of future mobile edge intelligence.

</details>


### [42] [DODR: Deterministic Operator-Driven Reasoning in Latent Space](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.04782)
*Weicai Huang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: DODR replaces token-level autoregressive sampling in LLMs with deterministic matrix operator reasoning in latent space. Reasoning states are snapshot vectors of semantic units, and Peirce's three inference types (deduction, induction, abduction) are formalized as three distinct matrix operators. The paper proves the operator set is minimal/complete, that no single super-operator can unify all three types, and that reasoning graphs are Turing-complete with convergence guaranteed via Banach's fixed-point theorem. Experiments on 503 samples show strong performance across all three reasoning modes with structural zero-hallucination guarantees.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Autoregressive LLMs treat reasoning as token-level probabilistic sampling, which introduces three fundamental defects in complex logical reasoning: (1) error accumulation across generated tokens, (2) probability substituting logical necessity, and (3) the linear-chain information bottleneck that limits reasoning capacity. These issues motivate a fundamentally different reasoning paradigm.

Method: DODR reconstructs reasoning as graph computation in a high-dimensional linear-algebraic space. Reasoning states are snapshot vectors composed of semantic units (phrases/sentences) rather than tokens, and each inference step is a deterministic matrix operation with no sampling. Peirce's three inference types are formalized as: a rank-deficient deduction operator (information collapse), a full-rank induction operator (information expansion), and an abduction operator as the Moore-Penrose pseudo-inverse of deduction (information hypothesizing). Theoretical proofs establish operator minimality/completeness, a rank obstruction preventing a unified super-operator, and Turing-completeness with fixpoint convergence.

Result: On 503 sample records (420 deduplicated), across dedicated and end-to-end settings: deduction loss converges to 1.40e-05; induction achieves 0.9996 generalization coverage with 20/20 hard vetoes on counterexamples; abduction solutions exceed random baseline by 28x with judgment accuracies of 72.5% (58/80, Wilson 95% CI [61.9%, 81.1%]) and 81.7% (49/60, CI [70.1%, 89.4%]); frozen operators achieve 100% (60/60) on unseen cross-domain deduction. The architecture provides structural zero-hallucination guarantees and a three-layer continual-learning mechanism.

Conclusion: DODR demonstrates that logical reasoning can be reformulated away from probabilistic token sampling into deterministic linear-algebraic operator computation in latent space. The framework provides theoretical guarantees (minimal/complete operator set, rank obstruction for unification, Turing-completeness, Banach convergence) alongside strong empirical results, including zero-hallucination guarantees, cross-domain generalization, and continual learning capability.
