Abstract: Autoregressive (AR) large language models formulate reasoning as token-level probabilistic sampling, which induces three fundamental defects in complex logical reasoning: error accumulation, probability substituting necessity, and the linear-chain information bottleneck. This paper proposes the Deterministic Operator-Driven Reasoning in Latent Space architecture (DODR), which reconstructs reasoning as reasoning-graph computation in a high-dimensional linear-algebraic space. Reasoning states are represented as snapshot vectors whose primitives are semantic units (phrases or sentences) rather than tokens, and each inference step is a deterministic matrix operation with no token sampling. Peirce's three inference types are formalized as three trainable matrix operators: a rank-deficient deduction operator (information collapse), a full-rank induction operator (information expansion), and an abduction operator defined as the Moore-Penrose pseudo-inverse of deduction (information hypothesizing). We prove that the operator set is minimal and complete given Peirce's trichotomy, that no single "super-operator" can realize all three types (a rank obstruction), and that reasoning graphs are Turing-complete with contractive backflow converging by Banach's fixed-point theorem. Experiments on 503 sample records (420 deduplicated samples) across dedicated and end-to-end settings show: deduction loss converges to 1.40e-05; induction achieves 0.9996 generalization coverage with 20/20 hard vetoes on counterexamples; abduction solutions exceed the random baseline by 28x with judgment accuracies of 72.5% (58/80, Wilson 95% CI [61.9%, 81.1%]) and 81.7% (49/60, CI [70.1%, 89.4%]); frozen operators attain 100% (60/60) on unseen cross-domain deduction. The architecture provides a structural zero-hallucination guarantee and a three-layer continual-learning mechanism. All data and code are released.

</details>


### [43] [ProtLingo: Efficient Protein Language Modeling via Conditional Memory and Expert Routing](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.04793)
*Mingrui Li,Sixian Shen,Minzhang Li,Ruiyi Zhang,Kexin Zhang,Jiakai Zhang,Jingyi Yu*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: ProtLingo is an efficient protein language model framework that augments a pretrained single-sequence backbone with conditional local memory and sparse expert routing. It maps contextual residue representations into route-specific discrete codes and composes local windows into latent N-gram addresses to retrieve reusable residual signals, while upcycling selected feed-forward blocks into sparse Mixture-of-Experts layers. ProtLingo achieves competitive performance with a 150M-scale backbone across protein fitness prediction, FLIP benchmarks, and supervised contact prediction, with strong parameter efficiency on mutation-effect prediction.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: While protein language models are scalable for modeling sequence-function relationships, increasing the size of dense Transformer backbones brings substantial computational cost without consistently improving mutation-sensitive prediction performance. There is a need for an efficient PLM that balances computational efficiency with predictive accuracy.

Method: ProtLingo augments a pretrained single-sequence backbone with two mechanisms: (1) conditional local memory that maps contextual residue representations into route-specific discrete codes, composes centered local windows into latent N-gram addresses, and retrieves reusable residual signals for recurring local sequence contexts; and (2) sparse expert routing that upcycles selected feed-forward blocks into sparse Mixture-of-Experts layers with shared and routed experts, enabling residue-dependent computation while activating only a subset of parameters.

Result: ProtLingo achieves competitive performance on protein fitness prediction, FLIP benchmarks, and supervised contact prediction using only a 150M-scale backbone, demonstrating strong parameter efficiency on mutation-effect prediction while preserving long-range structural representations.

Conclusion: ProtLingo demonstrates that efficient architecture design—combining conditional local memory with sparse expert routing—can achieve competitive protein modeling performance at a modest scale, offering a computationally efficient alternative to simply scaling dense Transformer backbones for mutation-sensitive prediction tasks.

Abstract: Proteins perform diverse cellular functions, and even single amino-acid substitutions can alter stability, activity, or molecular interactions. Protein language models (PLMs) provide a scalable approach for modeling such sequence--function relationships from unlabeled sequences, but increasing the size of dense Transformer backbones often brings substantial computational cost without consistently improving mutation-sensitive prediction. We introduce ProtLingo, an efficient PLM framework that augments a pretrained single-sequence backbone with conditional local memory and sparse expert routing. ProtLingo maps contextual residue representations into route-specific discrete codes, composes centered local windows into latent $N$-gram addresses, and retrieves reusable residual signals associated with recurring local sequence contexts. In parallel, selected feed-forward blocks are upcycled into sparse Mixture-of-Experts layers with shared and routed experts, enabling residue-dependent computation while activating only a subset of parameters. Experiments on protein fitness prediction, FLIP benchmarks, and supervised contact prediction show that ProtLingo achieves competitive performance with a 150M-scale backbone, including strong parameter efficiency on mutation-effect prediction and preserved long-range structural representations.

</details>


### [44] [Whose record is this? Diagnosing and authorizing record use in personalized multimodal models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.04801)
*Xinyu Mao,Junsi Li,Chenyang Liu,Haoji Zhang,Ming Sun*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper formalizes record authorization in contextualized visual personalization as three conditions (subject presence P, record-edge validity E, answer support S) and identifies visual memory misbinding (VMM) when these fail. It introduces RecordAuth-Diag, a 3,690-case diagnostic suite, evaluates interface failures across Qwen/Phi/Gemma families, and shows typed pre-generation authorization dramatically reduces unsafe exposure at some cost to recall.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Contextualized visual personalization systems can retrieve the correct record but apply it to the wrong visual subject—a failure mode not adequately formalized or diagnosed. The authors aim to define when record-conditioning is legitimate and to localize why models violate this.

Method: The authors define record authorization as P∧E∧S (subject presence, record-edge validity, answer support). They build RecordAuth-Diag, a matched 3,690-case suite that changes one image–record edge while holding other elements fixed. Card-removal and nonce relabeling ablations attribute failures to supplied records. They test raw-bank and typed pre-generation authorization across multiple model families, and use 560 localized DAVIS cases for full P∧E∧S validation plus appearance-supplied E evidence conditional on P using authenticated subject tokens.

Result: Gemma-3-4B-IT shows 63.69% local unauthorized use at 25.75% clean recall. CoViP reaches 26.02% vs 22.49% for its Qwen backbone. Typed authorization reduces Qwen card exposure on RecordAuth-Diag from 43.63% to 3.06%, while positive recall drops from 86.26% to 60.90%. On DAVIS, relevance and typed authorization have similar release rates (28.93% vs 28.39%) but differ in unsafe release (6.79% vs 0.89%). The 33 additional unsafe cases removed decompose as 27 support, 4 edge, 2 clean, 0 boundary—indicating the gain is an E∧S decision dominated by support.

Conclusion: Visual memory misbinding is a real, measurable failure dominated by answer-support violations rather than edge checks alone. Appearance provides edge evidence only conditional on subject presence, and typed pre-generation authorization is an effective intervention. Findings are specific to the evaluated contracts, not claims about natural prevalence, consent, or visual identity.

Abstract: Contextualized visual personalization can retrieve a true record yet apply it to the wrong visual subject. We formalize when a record may condition an answer as \emph{record authorization}: subject presence ($P$), record-edge validity ($E$), and answer support ($S$) must all hold. We call violations visual memory misbinding (VMM). We construct RecordAuth-Diag, a 3,690-case matched diagnostic suite that changes one image--record edge while holding the query, question, record text, and image multiset fixed. Card removal and nonce relabeling attribute these failures to supplied records. Raw-bank failures span Qwen-, Phi-, and Gemma-family interfaces: Gemma-3-4B-IT reaches 63.69\% local unauthorized use at 25.75\% clean recall. CoViP remains at 26.02\%, versus 22.49\% for its Qwen backbone at similar clean recall. Typed pre-generation authorization reduces Qwen card exposure on RecordAuth-Diag from 43.63\% to 3.06\%, while positive recall changes from 86.26\% to 60.90\%. Full $P\wedge E\wedge S$ validation uses 560 localized DAVIS cases: top-1 relevance and typed authorization have comparable release (28.93\% and 28.39\%) but 6.79\% and 0.89\% unsafe release, respectively. Of the 33 additional unsafe cases removed, 27 are support, 4 edge, 2 clean, and 0 boundary cases. Thus the observed increment is an $E\wedge S$ decision dominated by support, not an edge check alone. Appearance supplies $E$ evidence only conditional on $P$; authenticated subject tokens instantiate the missing presence witness as a sufficiency control. The claims concern the evaluated contracts, not natural prevalence, consent, or visual identity

</details>


### [45] [Hierarchical Possession-Aware Graph Pointer Network for Pass Receiver Selection](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.04803)
*Jingyi Wang,Da Li,Kaixin Wang,Zhangqin Huang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper proposes HPGPN, a Hierarchical Possession-aware Graph Pointer Network, for predicting the intended pass receiver in football using event-centered freeze-frame observations with partial player visibility and anonymous candidates, outperforming baselines on public data.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Pass receiver selection is challenging in broadcast-like freeze-frame settings where only partial and variable player visibility is available, without complete trajectories or stable identities, requiring reasoning over anonymous candidates, opponent pressure, and recent context.

Method: HPGPN formulates pass receiver selection as variable-size candidate prediction over visible teammates, jointly modeling current player interactions via graph representation, fixed event context, and possession-level temporal dynamics using a dual-branch possession history, with hierarchical candidate representation refinement and a glimpse pointer head for receiver scoring.

Result: Experiments on public football event and freeze-frame data show HPGPN improves pass receiver selection performance; ablation studies validate the contributions of graph-based interaction modeling, fixed event context, and dual-branch dynamic possession-history modeling.

Conclusion: HPGPN effectively addresses partial-observation pass receiver prediction by hierarchically integrating spatial, contextual, and historical evidence, demonstrating that possession-aware temporal modeling and graph-based interaction are key to accurate receiver selection.

Abstract: Pass receiver selection is a fundamental task in football analytics, aiming to predict the intended receiver under a given game state. This task is challenging with event-centered freeze-frame observations, a broadcast-like setting that provides only partial and variable player visibility without complete trajectories or stable player identities. The model must therefore reason over anonymous visible candidates, opponent pressure, and recent context under partial observation. To address this setting, we propose a Hierarchical Possession-aware Graph Pointer Network (HPGPN), which formulates pass receiver selection as variable-size candidate prediction over visible teammates. HPGPN jointly models current player interactions, local event context, and possession-level temporal dynamics. It represents the current pass situation with a graph, incorporates fixed event context, and uses dynamic possession history to capture how the attacking sequence evolves. Candidate representations are refined hierarchically by integrating spatial, contextual, and historical evidence, and a glimpse pointer head scores the receiver candidates. Experiments on public football event and freeze-frame data show that HPGPN improves pass receiver selection performance. Ablation studies demonstrate the effectiveness of graph-based interaction modeling, fixed event context, and dual-branch dynamic possession-history modeling.

</details>


### [46] [MedFlow: Class-Aware Multi-Scale Generation for Medical Time-Series Synthesis](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.04804)
*Yanhao Huang,Shibo Feng,Wanjin Feng,Peilin Zhao,Chunyan Miao*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: MedFlow is a class-aware, multi-scale flow matching framework for synthetic medical time-series generation that addresses data scarcity and imbalance by capturing heterogeneous temporal patterns and strengthening minority-class signals, outperforming diffusion-based baselines on downstream prediction tasks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing synthetic medical time-series methods focus on matching overall distribution and temporal dynamics of real data, which does not ensure strong downstream utility on imbalanced datasets. Rare minority-class characteristics are often obscured by dominant population patterns, and clinically informative patterns occur at heterogeneous temporal scales.

Method: MedFlow uses (1) a vector-quantized multi-scale tokenizer that represents medical sequences at complementary temporal resolutions to capture both coarse clinical trends and fine-grained dynamics, and (2) Token Marginal Guidance, which incorporates class-conditional token statistics into the flow matching process to steer generation toward class-specific regions of learned tokens, strengthening minority-class patterns while preserving global and tail distributions.

Result: On four public datasets (EHR, EEG, ECG), MedFlow consistently outperforms state-of-the-art diffusion-based baselines across downstream prediction tasks, improving AUPRC by 5.8%, reducing Context-FID by 88.6%, and achieving 3.8x higher sampling throughput.

Conclusion: MedFlow effectively addresses data scarcity and class imbalance in synthetic medical time-series generation by jointly modeling multi-scale temporal dynamics and injecting class-conditional guidance, yielding both higher fidelity and better downstream utility at improved computational efficiency.

Abstract: Synthetic medical time-series generation can alleviate data scarcity and support the development of reliable clinical prediction models. However, existing methods mainly focus on matching the overall distribution and temporal dynamics of real data, which does not necessarily ensure strong downstream utility on imbalanced medical datasets. Clinically informative patterns often occur at heterogeneous temporal scales, while rare minority-class characteristics can be obscured by dominant population patterns. To address these challenges, we propose MedFlow, a class-aware multi-scale flow matching framework for medical time-series synthesis. MedFlow employs a vector-quantized multi-scale tokenizer to represent medical sequences at complementary temporal resolutions, capturing both coarse clinical trends and fine-grained dynamics. We further introduce Token Marginal Guidance, which incorporates class-conditional token statistics directly into the flow matching process to steer generation toward class-specific regions of the learned tokens. This mechanism strengthens minority-class patterns, while preserving the global and tail distributions of real data. Experiments on four public datasets covering electronic health records, EEG, and ECG signals demonstrate that MedFlow consistently outperforms recent state-of-the-art diffusion-based baselines across downstream prediction tasks. On average, it improves AUPRC by 5.8%, reduces Context-FID by 88.6%, and achieves 3.8$\times$ higher sampling throughput.

</details>


### [47] [When Financial Fine-tuning Fails: A Three-Level Detectability Analysis of Numerical Hallucination in Domain-Adapted Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.04806)
*Xiaodong Li,Peiwei Liu*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper investigates numerical hallucination in financial LLMs under controlled fine-tuning, showing that domain adaptation—not insufficient numerical reasoning—drives hallucination. Fine-tuned domain models exhibit 82.5-98% hallucination rates versus 5.4% for the base model, with numeracy supervision paradoxically amplifying the problem via "template injection" of memorized canonical values.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Financial LLMs are deployed for summarization where numerical hallucination poses serious practical risks, yet the common assumption that hallucination stems from insufficient numerical reasoning has never been systematically tested under controlled fine-tuning conditions.

Method: A cost-effective, controlled study across three model variants (base instruction-tuned, domain language-adapted FT-A, and numeracy-enhanced FT-A+B+C). Introduces a three-level detectability taxonomy: overt (currency-denominated fabrication), covert-explicit (professional-convention numbers), and covert-implicit (ungrounded quantitative claims) hallucination.

Result: Domain fine-tuning severely degrades numerical restraint at all detectability levels. Base model maintains 5.4% hallucination, FT-A reaches 82.5% overt hallucination, and FT-A+B+C reaches 98%. Numeracy supervision amplifies rather than mitigates hallucination. Template injection (memorized canonical values inserted regardless of input) is identified as the primary mechanism.

Conclusion: Numerical hallucination in financial summarization is driven by degradation of numerical restraint through domain adaptation, not insufficient numerical reasoning. Recommendations include evaluating hallucination across all detectability levels and implementing grounding-aware generation or abstention mechanisms in deployment.

Abstract: Financial large language models are increasingly deployed for summarization of reports and disclosures, where numerical hallucination poses significant practical risks. While prior work often attributes such hallucination to insufficient numerical reasoning, this assumption has not been systematically tested under controlled fine-tuning settings. In this paper, we conduct a cost-effective, controlled study of numerical hallucination in financial summarization across three model variants: a base instruction-tuned model, a domain language-adapted model (FT-A), and a numeracy-enhanced domain model (FT-A+B+C). We introduce a three-level detectability taxonomy distinguishing between overt hallucination (currency-denominated fabrication), covert-explicit hallucination (professional-convention numbers), and covert-implicit hallucination (ungrounded quantitative claims). Our results reveal that domain fine-tuning substantially degrades numerical restraint at all detectability levels. While the Base model maintains near-zero hallucination rates (5.4\%), FT-A exhibits 82.5\% overt hallucination and FT-A+B+C reaches 98\%. Contrary to intuition, numeracy supervision amplifies rather than mitigates hallucination across all levels. We identify template injection---the insertion of memorized canonical values regardless of input content---as a primary hallucination mechanism in fine-tuned models. These findings demonstrate that numerical hallucination in financial summarization is driven by the degradation of numerical restraint through domain adaptation, not by insufficient numerical reasoning. We recommend that evaluation protocols assess hallucination across all detectability levels and that deployment practices include explicit mechanisms for grounding-aware generation or abstention.

</details>


### [48] [CPR-IE:A Compression-Prediction-Resource Intelligence Efficiency Metric](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.04809)
*Xiantao Jiang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper introduces Compression-Prediction-Resource Intelligence Efficiency (CPR-IE), a protocol-relative framework for ordering intelligent systems under deployment constraints based on representational economy, predictive quality, and resource burden. The framework uniquely derives logarithmic cumulative burden via proportional-increment composition and power responses via context-independent ratio response, yielding the representation I(C,P,T). Extensive theoretical properties are proven, with connections to MDL, algorithmic complexity, and related concepts serving as motivation rather than derivation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Traditional evaluation of intelligent systems focuses on predictive accuracy alone, which is insufficient when systems must operate under real-world deployment constraints such as computational resources, memory, and energy. A more comprehensive ordering framework is needed that jointly accounts for predictive quality, resource burden, and how efficiently the system represents information.

Method: The paper develops CPR-IE as an axiomatic efficiency measure by separating two questions: (1) how raw resource consumption is represented, and (2) how resulting attributes are aggregated. Proportional-increment composition uniquely yields logarithmic cumulative burden, and context-independent ratio response yields power responses. Reference normalization produces the representation I(C,P,T). The framework is analyzed via a translog parent model and foundational theories (MDL, algorithmic complexity, proper scoring rules, variational inference, Landauer's principle) are used to motivate measurement choices.

Result: The paper proves Pareto consistency, unit invariance, boundary behavior, trade-off identities, ranking-stability regions, and cross-task aggregation. Additional results establish cardinal and ordinal identification, sub-Gaussian finite-sample ranking guarantees, robust selection under exponent uncertainty, and deterministic regret bounds. The authors clarify that CPR-IE is a constructed efficiency representation, not a universal law or definition of intelligence.

Conclusion: CPR-IE provides a principled, axiomatically grounded framework for comparing intelligent systems under deployment constraints, offering a protocol-relative efficiency index with strong theoretical guarantees. The framework is explicitly positioned as a practical evaluation tool rather than a claim about the fundamental nature of intelligence.

Abstract: Comparing intelligent systems under deployment constraints requires more than predictiveaccuracy.This paper develops Compression-Prediction-Resource Intelligence Efficiency (CPR-IE) as a protocol-relative ordering by representational economy, predictive quality, and resourceburden. The analysis separates two questions-how raw resource consumption is represented, andhow the resulting attributes are aggregated. Proportional-increment composition uniquely yieldslogarithmic cumulative burden, and context-independent ratio response yields power responsesto compression, prediction, and burden; with reference normalization the representation is I(C,P,T).We prove Pareto consistency, unit invariance, boundary behavior, trade-off identities, ranking-stability regions, and cross-task aggregation. A translog parent model makes interaction restrictions explicit, and further results establish cardinal and ordinal identification, sub-Gaussianfinite-sample ranking guarantees, robust selection under exponent uncertainty, and deterministicregret bounds. Minimum description length, algorithmic complexity, proper scoring rules, varia-tional inference, and Landauer's principle motivate measurement choices but do not entail theformula. CPR-IE is a constructed efficiency representation, not a universal law or a definition ofintelligence itself.

</details>


### [49] [Long Horizon Transformer Quantile Fault Prediction for Multi Site Industrial Predictive Maintenance](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.04840)
*David J Poland,Daniele Ravi,Na Helian*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: TQRNN30d is a long-horizon predictive maintenance framework using a dual-stage quantile regression neural network to map 81-channel hourly machine data into quantile-state representations, fused via a multi-stream temporal classifier with dynamic covariates, static metadata, and latent-history streams. It achieves 79.97% F1 at 30 days and outperforms 18 baselines at 7/14/30-day fixed thresholds on a machine-disjoint 43/14/15 split across 72 machines in nine facilities, though generalisation to unseen sites and equipment is not established.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Long-horizon predictive maintenance requires distinguishing slowly evolving degradation from normal operating-regime variation over day-scale planning windows. The paper seeks to evaluate whether an explicit conditional-quantile representation offers an informative classifier interface for this challenging temporal discrimination problem.

Method: A dual-stage QRNN feature extractor maps each hourly word of 81-channel behaviour into a 324-dimensional quantile-state representation. 720 ordered hourly words form a 30-day document. A multi-stream temporal fusion classifier integrates quantile states with dynamic covariates, channel-level static metadata, and a 168-hour latent-history stream via gated residual processing, causal recurrent encoding, and metadata-conditioned cross-modal attention. An instability-aware signal from sustained one-word-ahead prediction-error divergence modulates auxiliary memory at the longest horizon. Evaluation uses a machine-disjoint 43/14/15 split over 72 machines in nine facilities.

Result: At 30 days, TQRNN30d achieves 79.97% F1, 80.18% recall, 81.82% precision, 82.39% accuracy, and 0.820 ROC-AUC. It outperforms all 18 baselines at 7-, 14-, and 30-day fixed-threshold comparisons, with the largest F1 advantage at 14 days.

Conclusion: The results support held-out-machine performance within the observed homogeneous nine-facility fleet, but do not establish unseen-site, cross-equipment, or cross-sector generalisation, leaving those as important limitations and directions for future work.

Abstract: Long-horizon predictive maintenance requires models to distinguish slowly evolving degradation from normal operating-regime variation over planning windows measured in days rather than hours. This paper evaluates whether an explicit conditional-quantile representation provides an informative classifier interface for this problem. The proposed TQRNN30d framework combines a dual-stage quantile regression neural network (QRNN) feature extractor with a multi-stream temporal fusion classifier. Each hourly word of 81-channel machine behaviour is mapped to a 324-dimensional quantile-state representation, and 720 ordered hourly words form the 30-day document supplied to the long-horizon model. The classifier fuses quantile states with dynamic covariates, channel-level static metadata, and a 168-hour latent-history stream using gated residual processing, causal recurrent encoding, and metadata-conditioned cross-modal attention. A bounded instability-aware signal derived from sustained one-word-ahead prediction-error divergence provides auxiliary memory modulation at the longest horizon. Evaluation uses a machine-disjoint 43/14/15 train/validation/test allocation across 72 machines in nine manufacturing facilities. At 30 days, TQRNN30d achieves 79.97% F1, 80.18% recall, 81.82% precision, 82.39% accuracy, and 0.820 ROC-AUC. It leads all 18 evaluated baselines at the 7-, 14-, and 30-day fixed-threshold comparisons, with the largest F1 advantage at 14 days. The results support held-out-machine performance within the observed homogeneous nine-facility fleet, but do not establish unseen-site, cross-equipment, or cross-sector generalisation.

</details>


### [50] [ElderBench: Benchmarking Autonomous Mobile Agents for Older Adults](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.04850)
*Weide Zhan,Qumu Shaqu,Yuanqing Liu,Peng Zhang,Jiahao Liu,Kam Him Lam,Ning Gu,Zhan Hu,Tun Lu*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper introduces ElderBench, the first benchmark for evaluating mobile GUI agents in authentic elderly-oriented scenarios, built from 249 naturally elicited smartphone tasks across 20 applications. It characterizes linguistic divergence between elderly instructions and existing benchmarks, reveals substantial performance degradation of mainstream GUI agents on these instructions, and identifies how elderly-specific language patterns (indirect speech, referential ambiguity, under-specified requests) contribute to agent failures.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing GUI benchmarks rely on explicit, goal-oriented instructions and fail to capture natural language patterns of older adults (e.g., indirect speech, referential ambiguity, under-specified requests), creating a mismatch that may hinder reliable deployment of mobile agents for this population.

Method: Constructs ElderBench from 249 naturally elicited smartphone tasks from older adults across 20 applications. Analyzes linguistic divergence between elderly instructions and existing GUI benchmark instructions from syntactic, semantic, and pragmatic perspectives. Evaluates mainstream GUI agents and Vision-Language Models under online and offline settings, followed by controlled instruction normalization, failure analysis, and fine-grained linguistic feature analysis.

Result: Substantial performance degradation is observed across mainstream GUI agents when handling elderly-oriented instructions. The analyses identify how elderly-specific language patterns (indirect speech, referential ambiguity, under-specified requests) contribute to agent failures.

Conclusion: The findings provide actionable design insights toward more adaptive, interpretable, and age-inclusive GUI agents for older adults.

Abstract: While autonomous mobile agents hold great potential for assisting older adults with smartphone usage, existing GUI benchmarks mainly rely on explicit, goal-oriented instructions and rarely capture the naturally occurring language patterns of older users, such as indirect speech, referential ambiguity, and under-specified requests. This mismatch between benchmark instructions and real-world elderly interactions may hinder reliable agent deployment. To address this gap, we present ElderBench, the first benchmark for evaluating mobile GUI agents in authentic elderly-oriented scenarios. ElderBench is constructed from 249 naturally elicited smartphone tasks collected from older adults across 20 applications. We first characterize the linguistic divergence between elderly instructions and existing GUI benchmark instructions from syntactic, semantic, and pragmatic perspectives. We then evaluate mainstream GUI agents and Vision-Language Models under both online and offline settings, revealing substantial performance degradation when handling elderly-oriented instructions. Through controlled instruction normalization, failure analysis, and fine-grained linguistic feature analysis, we further identify how elderly-specific language patterns contribute to agent failures. Our findings provide actionable design insights toward more adaptive, interpretable, and age-inclusive GUI agents for older adults.

</details>


### [51] [MM-IFEval-Pro: A Multilingual and Attack-Resistant Benchmark for Instruction-Following in Vision-Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.04859)
*Changming Xiao,Zhenliang Ni,Jinhui He,Han Shu,Jie Hu*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper introduces MM-IFEval-Pro, a bilingual (Chinese and English) multimodal instruction-following benchmark encompassing 4 major task categories, 24 subcategories, 8 instruction categories with 52 subcategories, and diverse instruction hijacking cases. It also constructs a reinforcement-learning training set with Chinese and adversarial instructions that improves model performance on the benchmark and generalizes to other multimodal benchmarks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing multimodal instruction-following benchmarks suffer from limited language coverage and insufficient adversarial safety scenarios, making them inadequate for evaluating real-world multilingual and safety-sensitive applications.

Method: The authors construct MM-IFEval-Pro, a benchmark covering Chinese and English tasks with diverse instruction hijacking cases, organized into 4 major task categories, 24 subcategories, and 8 instruction categories with 52 subcategories, where each sample averages 3.0 constraints. They further build a reinforcement-learning training set enriched with Chinese and adversarial instructions.

Result: The reinforcement-learning training set significantly improves model performance on MM-IFEval-Pro and transfers effectively to other mainstream multimodal benchmarks, demonstrating strong cross-task and cross-language generalization.

Conclusion: MM-IFEval-Pro addresses gaps in language coverage and safety evaluation for multimodal instruction following, and the proposed RL training approach yields robust gains that generalize across tasks and languages.

Abstract: As vision-language models (VLMs) rapidly advance in image understanding, cross-modal reasoning, and complex instruction execution, instruction-following capability has become a key indicator of their reliability and practicality. However, existing multimodal instruction-following benchmarks still suffer from limited language coverage and insufficient adversarial safety scenarios, making them inadequate for evaluating real-world multilingual and safety-sensitive settings. To address these gaps, we present MM-IFEval-Pro, a multimodal instruction-following benchmark covering Chinese and English tasks as well as diverse instruction hijacking cases. MM-IFEval-Pro includes 4 major task categories and 24 subcategories and 8 instruction categories with 52 subcategories, with each sample containing an average of 3.0 constraints to realistically simulate complex instruction scenarios. We further construct a reinforcement-learning training set enriched with Chinese and adversarial instructions, which significantly improves model performance on MM-IFEval-Pro and transfers effectively to other mainstream multimodal benchmarks, demonstrating strong cross-task and cross-language generalization.

</details>


### [52] [MZ-Rain: Moisture-Budget-Guided Zero-Inflated Model for Station-Level Precipitation Nowcasting](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.04864)
*Yifang Zhang,Shengwu Xiong,Henan Wang,Wenjie Yin,Yuqiang Zhang,Chen Zhou,Hua Chen,Qile Zhao,Pengfei Duan*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: MZ-Rain is a moisture-budget-guided zero-inflated sLSTM framework for station-level precipitation nowcasting that decomposes precipitation formation into physical process-specific pathways and employs an adaptive Tweedie modeling strategy to handle zero-inflated rainfall data, consistently outperforming baselines on CSI, FAR, MSE, and MAE metrics.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Conventional time-series models for station-level precipitation nowcasting fail due to (1) treating meteorological variables homogeneously without physics-guided modeling of precipitation formation, and (2) severe zero inflation in precipitation data where dry intervals overwhelm meaningful rainfall patterns.

Method: MZ-Rain decomposes precipitation formation into process-specific pathways (moisture storage, moisture transport, surface evaporation, precipitation persistence) guided by the moisture budget equation, each captured by dedicated sLSTM branches. It introduces an adaptive Tweedie modeling strategy that adaptively modulates rainfall mean while jointly learning precipitation occurrence as an auxiliary task.

Result: Extensive experiments across diverse geographical and climatic regimes show MZ-Rain consistently outperforms strong baselines on CSI, FAR, MSE, and MAE, with superior skill in forecasting heavy precipitation events.

Conclusion: The physically grounded process modeling combined with zero-inflation handling enables better dry-wet discrimination and quantitative precipitation estimation, making MZ-Rain effective for precipitation nowcasting across varied climates.

Abstract: Accurate station-level precipitation nowcasting is critical for agriculture, water resource management, and disaster prevention, which typically is formulated as a time series forecasting problem. However, conventional time-series modeling techniques face two major challenges in addressing station-level precipitation nowcasting: (1) Lack of Physics-Guided Modeling}, where meteorological variables are treated as a homogeneous set without accounting for their distinct roles in precipitation formation, leads to predictions that deviate from the physical processes governing precipitation. (2) Severe zero inflation in precipitation, where dry intervals dominate the dataset, obscuring meaningful precipitation patterns and complicating the predictive modeling. To address these challenges, we propose \textbf{MZ-Rain}, a moisture-budget-guided zero-inflated sLSTM framework for station-level precipitation nowcasting. Guided by the moisture budget equation, MZ-Rain decomposes the precipitation formation process into process-specific pathways corresponding to moisture storage, moisture transport, surface evaporation, and precipitation persistence, and captures their temporal evolution through dedicated sLSTM branches. To account for the zero-inflated nature of precipitation, MZ-Rain introduces an adaptive Tweedie modeling strategy that adaptively modulates the rainfall mean while jointly learning precipitation occurrence as an auxiliary task, enabling the model to better balance dry-wet discrimination and quantitative precipitation estimation. Extensive experiments across diverse geographical and climatic regimes demonstrate that MZ-Rain consistently outperforms strong baselines on multiple evaluation metrics, including CSI, FAR, MSE, and MAE. In particular, the model exhibits superior skill in forecasting heavy precipitation events, while benefiting from physically grounded process modeling.

</details>


### [53] [CoSkill: Joint Reinforcement Learning of Reasoning and Meta-Skill Agents for Hierarchical Skill Evolution](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.04865)
*Jinyuan Feng,Dongmin Li,Yiqun Chen,Yang Gao,Xing Chen,Huimu Wang,Zhiqiang Pu*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: CoSkill is a unified multi-agent RL framework that recasts static meta-skill workflows as a learnable Meta-Skill Agent, jointly trained with a Reasoning Agent over a hierarchical skill library for end-to-end co-adaptation, achieving 98.4% success on ALFWorld and 90.6% on WebShop.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing skill library paradigms for LLM agents have structural shortcomings: they either decouple skill evolution from policy optimization or treat meta-skills as fixed workflows, making skills passive objects that limit flexible evolution and co-adaptation with the reasoning agent.

Method: CoSkill formulates a unified multi-agent RL framework where the static meta-skill workflow becomes a learnable Meta-Skill Agent, jointly trained with a Reasoning Agent over a hierarchical skill library. Both agents share a single backbone and cooperate as a team: the Reasoning Agent conditions actions on retrieved task-level and step-level skills, while the Reasoning Agent's task performance guides the Meta-Skill Agent to refine step skills. This enables end-to-end co-adaptation.

Result: Experiments on ALFWorld and WebShop show CoSkill substantially outperforms prior skill-based and RL baselines, achieving success rates of 98.4% and 90.6% respectively, with improvements of +3.5 and +6.2 percentage points. It also demonstrates superior early-stage sample efficiency, asymptotic performance, and wall-clock efficiency.

Conclusion: CoSkill demonstrates that jointly training the meta-skill and reasoning agents in a unified RL framework enables effective co-adaptation and significantly improves agent performance, sample efficiency, and convergence speed compared to prior static or decoupled skill management paradigms.

Abstract: Skill libraries improve the sample efficiency of agentic reinforcement learning (RL) by enabling large language model (LLM) agents to reuse procedural knowledge. Yet existing paradigms exhibit structural shortcomings: they either decouple skill evolution from policy optimization or instantiate meta-skills as fixed workflows. Both treat skills as passive objects to be managed, limiting the flexible evolution of skills and their co-adaptation with the reasoning agent. To address the limitations, we propose CoSkill, a unified multi-agent RL framework that recasts the static meta-skill workflow as a learnable Meta-Skill Agent and jointly trains it with a Reasoning Agent over a hierarchical skill library. By modeling the Reasoning and Meta-Skill Agents as a cooperative team sharing a single backbone, CoSkill enables end-to-end co-adaptation: the Reasoning Agent conditions its actions on a retrieved task skill and step skills selected from its child set, while its task performance guides the Meta-Skill Agent in refining those step skills. Experiments on ALFWorld and WebShop show that CoSkill substantially outperforms prior skill-based and RL baselines, achieving success rates of 98.4% and 90.6%, respectively (+3.5 and +6.2 pp). As shown in Figure 1, CoSkill achieves superior early-stage sample efficiency, asymptotic performance, and wall-clock efficiency. Our code is available at https://github.com/jinyuan-cookie/CoSkill.

</details>


### [54] [LLM-Assisted Behavioural and Scenario Augmentation for Agent-Based Energy Adoption Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.04866)
*Iias Faiud,Hossein Khaleghy,Michael Schukat,Karl Mason*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: A hybrid framework integrating bounded behavioural rubrics generated via LLM assistance into a calibrated agent-based model of solar PV adoption by Irish dairy farms, preserving techno-economic mechanisms while enabling scenario-driven uncertainty analysis. Results show stable, monotonic adoption behaviour with up to ~13% behavioural uplift relative to logistic baselines.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: LLMs offer potential for enriching simulation-based energy policy analysis through structured behavioural assumptions and exploratory scenarios, but directly replacing adoption models with LLM reasoning risks compromising interpretability, reproducibility, and behavioural validity. A controlled hybrid approach is needed.

Method: The paper proposes a hybrid framework for LLM-assisted specification design that integrates bounded behavioural rubrics (conservative, balanced, optimistic) and structured, rule-validated scenario specifications into a calibrated agent-based model of solar PV adoption by Irish dairy farms, augmenting the original techno-economic adoption mechanism.

Result: Across multiple policy settings, Monte Carlo worlds, and random seeds, the framework produced stable and economically plausible behaviour, with adoption outcomes bounded and monotonic across behavioural regimes. LLM-assisted specifications achieved up to approximately 13% behavioural adoption increase over the logistic case without unstable saturation dynamics.

Conclusion: LLM-assisted specifications can be integrated into calibrated energy ABMs in a controlled, reproducible, and policy-relevant manner, demonstrating the viability of hybrid human-LLM design for behavioural energy adoption modelling.

Abstract: Recent advances in large language models (LLMs) create opportunities to enrich simulation-based energy policy analysis, particularly by supporting structured behavioural assumptions and exploratory techno-economic scenarios. However, directly replacing adoption models with LLM reasoning raises concerns regarding interpretability, reproducibility, and behavioural validity. This paper proposes a hybrid framework for LLM-assisted specification design, integrating bounded behavioural rubrics and structured scenario specifications into a calibrated agent-based model (ABM) of solar photovoltaic (PV) adoption by Irish dairy farms. The proposed approach preserves the original techno-economic adoption mechanism while augmenting it with bounded behavioural modulation and scenario-driven uncertainty analysis. Behavioural effects are represented through interpretable conservative, balanced, and optimistic rubrics, while future policy and market conditions are explored through fixed, rule-validated scenario specifications. Experimental results across multiple policy settings, Monte Carlo worlds, and random seeds demonstrate stable and economically plausible behaviour, with adoption outcomes remaining bounded and monotonic across behavioural regimes. The framework achieves up to approximately 13% behavioural adoption increase relative to the corresponding logistic case without producing unstable or unrealistic saturation dynamics. The results demonstrate that LLM-assisted specifications can be integrated into calibrated energy ABMs in a controlled, reproducible, and policy-relevant manner.

</details>


### [55] [From Interaction Traces to Persistent Skills: Online Evolution for Computer-Use Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.04869)
*Longtao Hu,Xiao Liang,Linchao Zhu*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper presents an online skill-evolution framework for computer-use agents that converts interaction trajectories and evaluator feedback into a persistent, versioned library of reusable GUI procedures. Compared to an empty-library control across four OSWorld domains, the full system achieves higher post-warm-up evaluator scores (5.7–18.6 percentage point improvements), though benefits are conditional and repeated revision does not guarantee task recovery.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Computer-use agents do not systematically retain, refine, and reuse procedural knowledge from one interaction rollout to another, making each task interaction transient. Existing skill libraries provide external procedural knowledge but their incremental value over agents without skills, and their longitudinal dynamics under repeated interaction, remain insufficiently characterized.

Method: The authors propose an online skill-evolution framework that converts interaction trajectories and evaluator feedback into a persistent, versioned library of reusable procedures. Each iteration executes against a frozen library snapshot, and evidence-guided skill updates become available in subsequent iterations without modifying model parameters. They compare the full evolving-library system against a configuration-matched empty-library control across four OSWorld domains under identical action-generation, GUI-grounding stack, task sets, and iteration horizons.

Result: Following a five-iteration empty-library warm-up, the Full system attained higher post-warm-up mean evaluator scores in all four domain runs, with mean differences ranging from 5.7 to 18.6 percentage points, along with domain-dependent temporal stability. In GIMP, provenance-aware analysis revealed retrieval across task-of-origin boundaries and revision churn, where repeated accepted edits failed to recover the originating task.

Conclusion: Evolving skill libraries serve as auditable, shared procedural memory that can improve a fixed computer-use stack. However, their benefits are conditional, and repeated revision does not guarantee recovery of the original task.

Abstract: Computer-use agents can execute increasingly complex tasks in graphical interfaces, but their interaction experience is typically transient: procedural knowledge acquired from one rollout is not systematically retained, refined, and reused in later tasks. Existing skill libraries provide external procedural knowledge, yet their incremental value over the same agent operating without skills, as well as their longitudinal dynamics under repeated interaction, remain insufficiently characterized. We present an online skill-evolution framework that converts interaction trajectories and evaluator feedback into a persistent, versioned library of reusable procedures. Each iteration executes against a frozen library snapshot, and evidence-guided skill updates become available in subsequent iterations without changing model parameters. We compare the full evolving-library system with a configuration-matched empty-library control across four OSWorld application domains under the same fixed action-generation and GUI-grounding stack, task sets, and iteration horizons. Following a five-iteration empty-library warm-up, Full attains a higher post-warm-up mean evaluator score in all four observed domain runs, with mean differences ranging from 5.7 to 18.6 percentage points and domain-dependent temporal stability. In GIMP, provenance-aware analysis reveals retrieval across task-of-origin boundaries and revision churn, where repeated accepted edits fail to recover the originating task. These findings characterize evolving skill libraries as auditable, shared procedural memory that can improve a fixed computer-use stack, while showing that their benefits are conditional and repeated revision does not guarantee recovery. Code is released at https://github.com/LongtaoHu/Skill-Evo4GUI.

</details>


### [56] [CHAMP: Cross-domain Hybrid Architecture for Matchmaking and Prediction in Online Multi-Player Games](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.04870)
*Kai Wang,Ge Fan,Chaoyun Zhang,Yuyang Jiang,Yuze Liu*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Summary generation failed


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Motivation analysis unavailable

Method: Method extraction failed

Result: Result analysis unavailable

Conclusion: Conclusion extraction failed

Abstract: Multiplayer Online Battle Arena (MOBA) games rely on matchmaking to maintain competitive balance. Our prior work, CUPID, framed matchmaking as an assignment re-optimization problem and showed that a single-mode win-rate predictor can meaningfully rebalance teams. However, deploying such a system across diverse player populations exposes three practical bottlenecks: most queueing players lack sufficient in-mode match history (cold start), skill distributions shift drastically across rank tiers (distribution inconsistency), and extreme skill segments are severely data-starved.
  We present CHAMP, a cross-domain matchmaking framework that resolves these deployment bottlenecks. To address data sparsity and cold starts, CHAMP replaces the target-mode-only player profile with a hybrid domain feature collection: a timestamp-ordered cross-mode short-term sequence whose slices are annotated with target-domain features, plus per-mode breakdowns of long-term, real-time and team statistics. We further propose the Domain-Aware Win-rate Network (DAWN): a Domain-aware Knowledge Extractor (DAKE) compiles target-mode attributes into learnable representations that feed Domain-Aware Temporal/Spatial/Permutation OmniNet Encoders (DATOE/DASOE/DAPOE), so that mode-conditioned representations and per-mode debiasing are learned jointly inside a single shared network. Online, one trained DAWN serves every supported mode, with per-mode position-satisfaction thresholds as the only mode-specific knob.
  Offline, DAWN achieves 67.73% win-rate prediction accuracy, outperforming all evaluated attention and sequence baselines. Online A/B tests across the entire League ladder of a large-scale MOBA game, from novice players up to the top-expert players served by Elite Mode, demonstrate consistent drops in imbalanced matches. For lower-tier players, CHAMP reduces the 5-minute kill crushing rate by up to 20.73%.

</details>


### [57] [AutoLR: Automating the Path from Research to Launch Review in Industrial Recommender Systems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.04871)
*Qi Zhang,Yanlin Chen,Wenchao Xiao*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: AutoLR is an autonomous research-to-launch harness built for NetEase's DASHEN gaming-community app that automates the end-to-end iterative recommender improvement workflow—from research idea identification through code implementation, offline evaluation, online A/B testing, and Launch Review—by combining a multi-expert council for adversarial review, a deterministic evidence-weighted exploration-exploitation selector for trial budget allocation, and a layered knowledge system integrating external research, production knowledge, and DASHEN-specific domain knowledge with posterior evidence.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Improving industrial recommenders is a long, iterative, human-dependent process spanning research ideation, reproduction, implementation, offline/online evaluation, and launch review—often across multi-day experimental cycles. While LLMs can assist individual stages, there is no harness that reliably coordinates them across the full long-running workflow, motivating the need for an autonomous system that can drive the entire research-to-launch pipeline.

Method: AutoLR combines three system mechanisms: (1) a multi-expert council that debates and adversarially reviews proposals; (2) a deterministic evidence-weighted exploration-exploitation selector that allocates a limited trial budget across candidate directions with Council reranking; and (3) a layered knowledge system integrating external research, production-system knowledge, and DASHEN-specific domain knowledge (game communities, player characteristics, content-interaction patterns) alongside posterior evidence from configurations, patches, logs, failures, and offline outcomes. LLM agents handle semantic reasoning and code generation, while deterministic controllers retain authority over execution, metric extraction, guardrails, and persistent state transitions.

Result: The paper presents the system architecture and design of AutoLR, initially built as Auto Launch Review and later extended upstream into a full autonomous research-to-launch harness for the DASHEN recommender pipeline.

Conclusion: AutoLR demonstrates a hybrid human-machine architecture where LLM agents provide semantic reasoning and code generation capabilities while deterministic controllers maintain authority over critical operations such as execution, metrics, guardrails, and state management—enabling automation of the complete research-to-launch recommender improvement cycle.

Abstract: Improving an industrial recommender is an iterative research-and-engineering process rather than a direct path from idea to deployment. In \textbf{DASHEN, NetEase's gaming-community app}, algorithm engineers typically identify promising directions from research papers, technical reports, and prior production experiments; reproduce or adapt the underlying methods; implement them in the production codebase; and evaluate the resulting models through training and offline experiments. Promising candidates are then advanced to online A/B tests, and those demonstrating robust gains are submitted to Launch Review---the internal gate for full-traffic rollout. Large language models (LLMs) can assist with individual stages of this workflow, but the overall process remains human-dependent without a harness that can reliably coordinate them across long-running, often multi-day experimental cycles. We present \textbf{AutoLR}, initially built as \textbf{Auto Launch Review} and later extended upstream into an autonomous research-to-launch harness. AutoLR combines three system mechanisms: a \textbf{multi-expert council} that debates and adversarially reviews proposals; a \textbf{deterministic evidence-weighted exploration--exploitation selector} that allocates a limited trial budget across candidate directions and uses Council reranking; and a layered knowledge system that combines external research, production-system knowledge, and DASHEN-specific domain knowledge---such as game communities, player characteristics, and content-interaction patterns---with posterior evidence from configurations, patches, logs, failures, and offline outcomes. LLM agents perform semantic reasoning and code generation, while deterministic controllers retain authority over execution, metric extraction, guardrails, and persistent state transitions.

</details>


### [58] [MARLA: A Conceptual Scaffold for Regulatory Learning under the EU AI Act](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.04877)
*Alessio Buscemi,Tom Deckenbrunnen,Imane Hmiddou,Marco Billi,Livio Rubino,Silvia Rizzuto Ferruzza,Daniele Pagani,Antonino Rotolo*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper proposes MARLA, a conceptual scaffold for organizing regulatory learning under the EU AI Act as a five-stage cycle (Map, Assess, Report, Learn, Adapt), providing a shared vocabulary for technical and legal stakeholders across Local, National, and European governance levels.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The EU AI Act requires regulatory learning whereby evidence generated during implementation is translated into governance and legal knowledge, but the actors producing this evidence and those relying on it operate in different professional worlds, creating a gap in knowledge transfer and consistent interpretation.

Method: The paper proposes MARLA (Map, Assess, Report, Learn, Adapt), a five-stage conceptual scaffold situating regulatory learning at Local, National, and European levels of the AI Act's governance architecture. Each of the first three stages generates documentable forms of regulatory learning, illustrated with two piloted case studies and a prospective National-to-European example.

Result: The MARLA scaffold is deliberately non-prescriptive and provides technical and legal stakeholders with a shared vocabulary for regulatory learning. It is illustrated and validated through two piloted case studies and a prospective National-to-European illustration.

Conclusion: Regulatory learning under the EU AI Act can be effectively organized as a structured, multi-level cycle—with MARLA serving as a conceptual foundation—enabling evidence from implementation to inform governance, consistent interpretation, and adaptation as technologies evolve.

Abstract: The EU AI Act positions regulation as part of the infrastructure for safe, trustworthy and market-ready innovation. Realising this ambition requires regulatory learning: the evidence generated during implementation must be translated into governance and legal knowledge that supports consistent interpretation, effective oversight, and adaptation as technologies evolve. Yet the actors who produce this evidence and those who rely on it operate in different professional worlds. This paper proposes MARLA (Map, Assess, Report, Learn, Adapt), a conceptual scaffold organising regulatory learning as a five-stage cycle centred on the implementation of legal requirements into socio-technical practices, situated at the Local, National and European levels of the AI Act's governance architecture. Deliberately non-prescriptive, MARLA gives technical and legal stakeholders a shared vocabulary in which each of the first three stages generates its own documentable form of regulatory learning. We illustrate the scaffold with two piloted case studies and a prospective National-to-European illustration.

</details>


### [59] [Reinforcement Learning for Sequential Solar PV Policy Design under Uncertainty: An Agent-Based Approach](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.04880)
*Iias Faiud,Jonaid Shianifar,Michael Schukat,Karl Mason*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This study combines reinforcement learning (PPO, SAC, TD3) with a stochastic agent-based model to design adaptive solar PV adoption policies over a 16-year horizon, balancing adoption gains against public expenditure. Results show a clear trade-off frontier (e.g., ~4,145 adopters at EUR 41.73M vs. 2,682 adopters at EUR 7.27M), with consistent patterns across algorithms and broader exploration than static baselines.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Designing effective and fiscally sustainable solar PV adoption policies requires balancing adoption gains against public expenditure under uncertainty and heterogeneous decision-making—a complex challenge not well addressed by static or purely analytical approaches.

Method: The study formulates PV policy design as a sequential decision problem, integrating reinforcement learning (PPO, SAC, TD3) with a stochastic agent-based model simulating yearly solar PV adoption. A policymaker agent selects annual incentives (capital grants, subsidised loan rates, feed-in tariffs) over a 16-year horizon, with adoption-cost trade-offs handled via a scalarised reward framework where policy weights (w_cost) vary preferences.

Result: The highest-adoption policy (TD3, w_cost=0.5) achieves ~4,145 adopters at EUR 41.73M; the lowest-cost policy (PPO, w_cost=2.0) reduces expenditure to EUR 7.27M with 2,682 adopters; the balanced policy (PPO, w_cost=1.6) achieves 3,495 adopters at EUR 22.47M. Consistent trade-off patterns across algorithms indicate robustness, and the RL framework explores a broader policy range than static baselines.

Conclusion: Reinforcement learning demonstrates strong potential as a flexible tool for adaptive PV policy design under uncertainty, enabling systematic exploration of the adoption-cost trade-off frontier and yielding robust, scenario-tunable policy options.

Abstract: Designing effective and fiscally sustainable policies for solar photovoltaic (PV) adoption requires balancing adoption gains against public expenditure under uncertainty and heterogeneous decision-making. This study formulates PV policy design as a sequential decision problem and integrates reinforcement learning (RL) with a stochastic agent-based model (ABM) that simulates yearly solar PV adoption under uncertainty. A policymaker agent selects annual incentives, including capital grants, subsidised loan rates, and feed-in tariffs, over a 16-year horizon. Adoption--cost trade-offs are explored by varying policy preferences within a scalarised reward framework. Policies are learned using PPO, SAC, and TD3 and evaluated under stochastic simulation. The results show that this approach produces a clear trade-off structure: the highest-adoption policy (TD3, $w_{\text{cost}}=0.5$) achieves approximately 4,145 adopters at a cost of EUR 41.73 million, while the lowest-cost policy (PPO, $w_{\text{cost}}=2.0$) reduces expenditure to EUR 7.27 million with 2,682 adopters. The balanced policy (PPO, $w_{\text{cost}}=1.6$) achieves 3,495 adopters at a cost of EUR 22.47 million. Across algorithms, consistent trade-off patterns are observed, indicating robustness of the adoption--cost relationship. Compared with static baseline policies, the RL framework explores a broader range of policy configurations. These findings demonstrate the potential of RL as a flexible tool for adaptive policy design under uncertainty.

</details>


### [60] [From Language Models to World-Acting Systems: Progress and Limits of Agentic AI across Digital, Social, Virtual, and Physical Environments](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.04894)
*Linsen Zhu,Mengqing Cai*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: A critical review of LLM agency literature up to Aug 2026 that challenges the narrative of an autonomous march, organizing evidence across delegated authority, temporal persistence, and environmental coupling while separating model, harness, and environment. It finds that expanding action interfaces is well-documented, but robust task completion, recovery, authorization, and independent verification are not. It proposes "justified delegation" as a guiding heuristic and a research agenda centered on coupled evaluation, capability-based permissions, durable state, cross-agent accountability, and staged physical validation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Consequential LLM-agent deployments are often narrated as inevitable progress toward autonomy, conflating model competence, system integration, persistence, and safe authority. The authors aim to disentangle these dimensions and provide a rigorous, evidence-based assessment of what is actually documented versus what remains aspirational.

Method: A critical literature review synthesizing primary research and official technical specifications available by 31 August 2026, systematically organized along three axes: delegated authority, temporal persistence, and environmental coupling, with explicit separation of the model, the harness, and the environment.

Result: Action-interface expansion is well-documented across protocols (MCP, Agent2Agent), multi-agent systems, simulations, robotics, and scientific automation. However, robust completion, recovery, authorization, and independent verification are not convincingly established. Interoperability protocols improve connectivity but fail to establish trustworthy delegation; multi-agent systems introduce specialization but also cost and correlated failure; persistent simulations and world models support training/planning but do not demonstrate agency; robotics and self-driving labs show bounded feasibility, not unattended open-world reliability.

Conclusion: The authors propose "justified delegation" as an analytical and normative heuristic rather than a law or score: expand action scope only where evidence supports provenance, bounded authority, failure detection, safe recovery, and calibrated human control. This motivates a research agenda for coupled model-harness evaluation, capability-based permissions, durable state, cross-agent accountability, and staged physical validation.

Abstract: Large language models become consequential agents when surrounding systems let outputs change external state. Models now call tools, operate interfaces, delegate work, retain state, inhabit generated worlds, and control robots or laboratory equipment. Such advances are often narrated as one march toward autonomy, conflating model competence, system integration, persistence, and safe authority. This critical review synthesizes primary research and official technical specifications available by 31 August 2026. We organize the evidence along delegated authority, temporal persistence, and environmental coupling, while separating model, harness, and environment. Within the evidence examined, action-interface expansion is documented more convincingly than robust completion, recovery, authorization, or independent verification. Model Context Protocol and Agent2Agent improve interoperability but do not establish trustworthy delegation; multi-agent organization adds specialization alongside cost and correlated failure. Persistent simulations and world models support training and planning but do not themselves demonstrate agency; robotics and self-driving laboratories establish bounded feasibility rather than unattended open-world reliability. We propose justified delegation as an analytical and normative heuristic, not an observed law or certified score: expand action scope only where evidence supports provenance, bounded authority, failure detection, safe recovery, and calibrated human control. This framing yields a research agenda for coupled model-harness evaluation, capability-based permissions, durable state, cross-agent accountability, and staged physical validation.

</details>


### [61] [Compact-Memory LLM Agents via Online Max-Member Clustering and Atom-Aware Packing](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.04915)
*Jiahe Geng,Jinpeng Wang,Kun Yuan*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: RSM-full is an online clustered-memory pipeline combining a cosine-gated max-member merge write rule with an atom-aware grouped context packer. It achieves 83% of Full-Context quality at 32% of the token cost at a 4k budget, significantly outperforming streaming-clustered baselines across compact-memory benchmarks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Long-horizon LLM deployments face tight prompt budgets due to latency, cost, and context limits. The key question is not raw recall alone but which memory design yields the best quality-token trade-off in the compact-memory regime, where full-context prompting becomes impractical.

Method: RSM-full uses two key design choices: (1) a cosine-gated max-member merge write rule for streaming memory clustering, and (2) an atom-aware grouped context packer for assembling retrieved content. It is evaluated against streaming-clustered baselines (Online K-Means, matched-tau DP-means), retrieval baselines (Budget-RAG, BM25-RAG), and agentic-memory baselines (Streaming-Proto, A-MEM).

Result: On AMA-Bench, RSM-full reaches 83% of Full-Context quality at 32% of token cost at a 4k budget, and beats Online K-Means by +3.5-6.0pp (p<.001) across the ~2.6k-5k regime. Ablations attribute +5.7pp to the merge rule and +5.0pp to the grouped packer. On RealMem, it outperforms Budget-RAG (+0.69pp) and Streaming-Proto (+2.97pp) and A-MEM (+1.65pp), while being on par with BM25-RAG.

Conclusion: Under tight prompt budgets (~2k-5k tokens), compact-memory quality is driven mainly by how streaming memories are merged and how retrieved content is assembled. RSM-full defines a strong compact-memory Pareto point in this regime, though higher-token baselines remain stronger outside it.

Abstract: Many long-horizon LLM deployments face tight prompt budgets: latency, cost, and context limits make full-context prompting impractical as interaction length grows. The key question is then not raw recall alone, but which memory design gives the best quality--token trade-off in the compact-memory regime. We present \textbf{RSM-full}, an online clustered-memory pipeline designed for a strong quality--token Pareto point.
  RSM-full combines two design choices: a cosine-gated \emph{max-member merge} write rule and an atom-aware grouped context packer. On AMA-Bench, our primary compact-memory benchmark, it reaches $83%$ of Full-Context quality at $32%$ of the token cost at a $4$k budget; under four-seed averaging it beats the closest streaming-clustered baseline (Online K-Means) by $+3.5$--$6.0$,pp ($p{<}.001$) across the whole ${\sim}2.6$k--${\sim}5$k regime. Three-seed ablations show most of this gain comes from the merge rule ($+5.7$,pp over Online K-Means and matched-$τ$ DP-means) and the grouped packer ($+5.0$,pp over flat concatenation).
  The pattern reproduces on RealMem, an independent long-horizon persona-memory benchmark: RSM-full improves on Budget-RAG ($+0.69$,pp, $p{=}.006$), is on par with BM25-RAG (paired $Δ{=}{+}0.27$,pp, $p{=}.47$; we do \emph{not} claim BM25 equivalence in the equivalence-test sense), and significantly outperforms Streaming-Proto ($+2.97$,pp) and the closest reproduced 2025 agentic-memory baseline A-MEM ($+1.65$,pp, $p{<}.001$). Across benchmarks the message is consistent: under tight budgets, compact-memory performance is driven mainly by how streaming memories are merged and how retrieved content is assembled.
  Overall, RSM-full is most useful when answeroughly $2k$--$5k$ prompt tokens, where itdefines a strong compact-memory Pareto point; higher-token baselines remain stronger outside this regime.

</details>


### [62] [Artificial Intelligence in Equity and Crypto Markets: Progress, Profitability Evidence, and the Limits of Automated Investing](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.04917)
*Linsen Zhu,Mengqing Cai*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: A critical state-of-the-art review (through Aug 2026) examining whether AI investment workflows across equities, ETFs, crypto spot, perpetual futures, and on-chain markets translate technical capability into durable, risk-adjusted net alpha. Using an "alpha-translation chain" framework, the review finds real but mainly upstream progress in prediction, text processing, portfolio design, and workflow integration, while evidence for persistent net performance is thin due to temporal contamination, selection bias, weak benchmarks, implementation costs, and capacity constraints. No general AI architecture demonstrates persistent, cross-regime, capacity-aware net alpha in public evidence.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Technical AI capability in investment workflows does not imply investment profitability. The paper aims to critically assess whether public research evidence supports claims that AI can deliver durable net alpha, by systematically examining the gap between demonstrated upstream technical progress and downstream economic viability.

Method: A critical state-of-the-art review of public research available through 31 August 2026 across listed equities, exchange-traded funds, centralized crypto spot, perpetual futures, and on-chain markets. Evidence is organized via an "alpha-translation chain" requiring that point-in-time information yields (1) a stable signal, (2) feasible positions, (3) executable orders, and (4) risk-adjusted returns after costs. The review spans machine learning, time-series foundation models, financial language models, reinforcement learning, and agent-based systems, with separate treatment of crypto asset classes.

Result: The examined record shows real but mostly upstream progress in prediction, text processing, portfolio design, and workflow integration. Evidence is thinner for durable net performance. Identified failure modes include temporal contamination, repeated selection, survivorship bias, weak benchmarks, implementation costs, venue mechanics, and capacity constraints. Strong historical results coexist with predictor decay, corrected look-ahead failures, mixed prospective evidence, and few audited live-capital records. In crypto, spot, perpetual, and decentralized cash flows/execution require separate treatment. No general AI architecture in public evidence demonstrates persistent, cross-regime, capacity-aware net alpha.

Conclusion: More credible claims require point-in-time data and models, decision-aligned objectives, joint portfolio-execution evaluation, controlled adaptation, prospective tests, and authority-matched governance. These conditions can improve evidence quality and implementation likelihood but do not guarantee profit.

Abstract: Artificial intelligence (AI) now supports investment workflows from data and prediction through research, portfolios, execution, and tool use. Technical capability, however, is not evidence of investment profitability. This critical state-of-the-art review examines public research available through 31 August 2026 on listed equities, exchange-traded funds, centralized crypto spot, perpetual futures, and on-chain markets. We organize evidence with an alpha-translation chain: point-in-time information must yield a stable signal, feasible positions, executable orders, and risk-adjusted returns after costs. Across machine learning, time-series foundation models, financial language models, reinforcement learning, and agents, the examined record shows real but mainly upstream progress in prediction, text processing, portfolio design, and workflow integration. Evidence is thinner for durable net performance. Temporal contamination, repeated selection, survivorship, weak benchmarks, implementation costs, venue mechanics, and capacity can break translation to net alpha. Strong historical results coexist with predictor decay, corrected look-ahead failures, mixed prospective evidence, and few audited live-capital records. Crypto adds informative state but requires separate treatment of spot, perpetual, and decentralized cash flows and execution. Within the public evidence examined here, no general AI architecture is shown to deliver persistent, cross-regime, capacity-aware net alpha. More credible claims require point-in-time data and models, decision-aligned objectives, joint portfolio--execution evaluation, controlled adaptation, prospective tests, and authority-matched governance. These conditions can improve evidence and implementation; they do not guarantee profit.

</details>


### [63] [Solving Hard XAI Queries Based on a Compiled Dual-Rail Encoding](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.04931)
*Arthur Ledaguenel,Florent Capelli,Jean-Marie Lagniez*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper proves that computing certain classes of abductive explanations (including shorter ones and those incorporating user preferences) remains computationally hard even for Ordered Binary Decision Diagrams (OBDDs), and proposes that a dual-rail encoding representation of the classifier enables efficient computation of these explanation classes.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: While XAI provides explanations for boolean classifiers via abductive and contrastive explanations, computing explanations is generally hard. Compiled representations like OBDDs promise tractability, but it is unclear whether all desired classes of explanations (e.g., shortest, preference-aware) can be efficiently computed with such representations.

Method: The authors formally analyze the computational complexity of computing several classes of abductive explanations over OBDDs, demonstrating intractability for classes such as shorter abductive explanations and preference-aware abductive explanations. They then show that representing the dual-rail encoding of the classifier in a suitable compiled form allows these explanation classes to be computed efficiently.

Result: It is proven that several classes of abductive explanations—including shorter abductive explanations and those respecting the explainee's preferences—remain hard to compute even for OBDDs, a highly tractable subset of the knowledge compilation map. However, exploiting a proper representation of the dual-rail encoding restores tractability for computing these explanation classes.

Conclusion: Standard OBDD representations are insufficient for efficiently computing certain explanation classes; instead, compiled representations of the dual-rail encoding of the classifier provide the necessary structure to compute these explanations efficiently, recovering the benefits of knowledge compilation for XAI.

Abstract: The widespread adoption of artificial intelligence (AI) within real-world applications has raised a lot of concerns regarding their trustworthiness, especially in critical applications. The field of eXplainable AI (XAI) has emerged with the objective of providing explanations to the users about the decisions made by AI systems. Several explanations for boolean classifiers have been introduced in the literature, including abductive and contrastive explanations, each giving a different insight on the decision of the classifier. However, computing an explanation for a decision of a boolean classifier is a hard problem in general. One way to deal with this complexity is to rely on a compiled representation of the classifier for which each explanation can be computed efficiently.
