Unfortunately, we prove in this paper that several classes of abductive explanations, remain hard to compute even for Ordered Binary Decision Diagrams, one of the most tractable subsets of the knowledge compilation map. Included in such classes are shorter abductive explanations or abductive explanations that include the explainee's preferences. To recover the benefits of working with compiled representations, we show that a proper representation of the dual-rail encoding of the classifier can be used to compute efficiently these classes of explanations.

</details>


### [64] [Why We Care About Understanding: Competence through Predictive Compression](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.04962)
*Matthieu Queloz,Pierre Beckmann*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper bridges two competing views of understanding—the information-theoretic tradition that equates understanding with compression (Chaitin's "comprehension is compression") and the philosophical tradition that characterizes understanding via grasping connections, explanations, and handling novelty. It reconciles these through three theses: (1) understanding serves as a proxy for robust competence enabling trust and learning decisions; (2) understanding involves possessing a mental model whose predictive power enables compression, making compression the "representational shadow" of comprehension rather than its identity; (3) fiduciary and transmission functions drive human understanding toward principled simplicity. This framework explains both the appeal and limits of compressionist accounts and illuminates the inscrutability of AI systems.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To resolve the tension between compressionist accounts of understanding (common in information theory, ML, and AI) and philosophical accounts of understanding (emphasizing explanatory grasping and novelty handling), by providing a unified framework that respects both traditions.

Method: A conceptual/theoretical analysis advancing three interlocking theses about the concept, state, and human form of understanding, integrating perspectives from information theory, machine learning, philosophy, and AI research.

Result: The paper shows that compression is not identical to comprehension but is its representational shadow—understanding enables compression through prediction, and human understanding specifically tends toward principled simplicity due to social pressures of demonstrability and transmissibility. The framework explains both the strengths and limitations of compressionist theories.

Conclusion: Understanding is best characterized as a proxy for robust competence, grounded in mental models that enable prediction (and hence compression). The distinctively human form of understanding is shaped toward simplicity by social-functional demands. This reconciles compressionist and philosophical accounts and sheds light on why AI systems may be competent yet inscrutable.

Abstract: What is the relation between understanding and compression, and why does human understanding take such a heavily compressed form? Across information theory, machine learning, and AI research, a substantial tradition identifies understanding with compression-a thought captured in Gregory Chaitin's dictum that "comprehension is compression." Philosophers, by contrast, have characterized understanding in terms of grasping connections, giving explanations, and handling novelty. This paper bridges the two pictures through three interlocking theses. The first concerns the concept of understanding: it serves as an efficient proxy for a distinctive form of robust competence, enabling us to identify whom to trust and whom to learn from. The second concerns the state of understanding: to understand a domain is to possess a mental model of its relational structure that enables prediction, and what enables prediction enables compression, because what becomes predictable need not be stored separately. Compression is therefore not identical with comprehension, but its representational shadow. The third concerns the characteristically human form of understanding: the fiduciary and transmission functions highlighted by the first thesis impose pressures of demonstrability and transmissibility that drive human understanding toward principled simplicity. The resulting framework explains both the appeal and the limits of compressionist accounts of understanding while shedding light on the inscrutability of AI systems.

</details>


### [65] [Global to Local: Topology-Preserving Adaptive Graph Pooling via Granular-Ball](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.04978)
*Sen Zhao,Gaojie Xu,Shuyin Xia,Yifan Guan,Yi Liu,Yi Wang,Wei Wang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: TPAGP is a novel graph pooling method that dynamically partitions graphs into granular balls by integrating node features and topological information, enabling multi-granularity representations that capture both local and global structural patterns for improved graph classification.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Previous graph pooling methods focus on fine-grained node representations by removing or merging nodes, neglecting global-to-local patterns and adaptive granularity of the graph's topological structure. Real graphs exhibit hierarchical granularity from coarse global structure down to fine local structure, which fixed-granularity strategies fail to capture.

Method: TPAGP dynamically partitions graphs into granular balls by jointly considering node features and topological information, enabling adaptive granularity for each subdomain. A multi-granularity graph network model facilitates feature interaction and optimization across different granularities.

Result: TPAGP outperforms existing pooling methods across various benchmark datasets, effectively mitigating information loss caused by fixed-granularity strategies.

Conclusion: Multi-granularity graph pooling that adaptively incorporates topological structure at varying levels of granularity significantly enhances graph classification performance compared to existing fixed-granularity pooling approaches.

Abstract: Graph pooling aims to compress the graph, including both node embeddings and their underlying topological patterns, into a more compact representation. Previous works focus primarily on the overly fine-grained representation of nodes, progressively coarsening the graph by removing nodes or merging them into clusters, thus neglecting the global-to-local patterns and adaptive granularity of the graph's topological structure. In the real scenario, graphs as a whole can be considered the coarsest level of granularity, encapsulating the global topological structure, with progressively finer-grained local topological structures represented from top to bottom. This process continues until the adaptive granularity for each subdomain is reached. To this end, we propose a novel Topology-Preserving Adaptive Graph Pooling (TPAGP) method that dynamically partitions graphs into granular balls by integrating node features and topological information, enabling the generation of multi-granularity representations that effectively capture both local and global structural patterns. Additionally, we design a multi-granularity graph network model that facilitates feature interaction and optimization across different granularities, significantly enhancing performance in graph classification tasks. Experimental results demonstrate that TPAGP outperforms existing pooling methods across various benchmark datasets, effectively mitigating information loss caused by fixed-granularity strategies.

</details>


### [66] [A Tree-based RAG Framework for Evidence-Intensive QA via Adaptive Planning and Topology-Aware Evidence Gathering](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.04981)
*Songeun Lee,Kyungjin Min,Injae Na,Suyeong Lee,Chiyoung Kim,Woohwan Jung*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: APT-RAG is an Adaptive Planning and Topology-aware evidence gathering RAG framework designed to address limitations in evidence-intensive multi-hop QA by dynamically expanding reasoning structures and improving evidence coverage across reasoning nodes.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing structured RAG methods using tree- or graph-based reasoning struggle in evidence-intensive QA settings due to (1) structural rigidity that limits adaptive reasoning expansion and (2) topology-ignorant evidence gathering that fails to effectively integrate evidence across reasoning nodes when synthesizing information from dozens or hundreds of documents.

Method: APT-RAG employs adaptive planning to dynamically expand the reasoning structure based on question dependencies and evidence requirements, combined with topology-aware evidence gathering that leverages sibling evidence reuse, direct retrieval, and evidence aggregation from child nodes. It also introduces evidence-guided batched answer generation to reduce generation overhead in evidence-intensive scenarios.

Result: On evidence-intensive QA benchmarks, APT-RAG outperforms existing structured RAG methods.

Conclusion: APT-RAG demonstrates that combining adaptive reasoning structure expansion with topology-aware evidence gathering effectively handles evidence-intensive multi-hop QA, achieving superior performance over prior structured RAG approaches.

Abstract: Recent structured RAG methods leverage tree- or graph-based reasoning structures to improve multi-hop QA. However, they face key limitations in evidence-intensive QA, where answering a question requires synthesizing information scattered across dozens or even hundreds of documents: structural rigidity, which limits adaptive reasoning expansion, and topology-ignorant evidence gathering, which prevents effective integration of evidence across different reasoning nodes. To address these issues, we propose APT-RAG, an Adaptive Planning and Topology-aware evidence gathering RAG framework. Adaptive planning dynamically expands the reasoning structure based on question dependencies and evidence requirements, while topology-aware evidence gathering improves evidence coverage through sibling evidence reuse, direct retrieval, and evidence aggregation from child nodes. We further introduce evidence-guided batched answer generation to reduce significant generation overhead in evidence-intensive QA. In the experiments on evidence-intensive QA benchmarks, APT-RAG outperforms existing structured RAG methods. Our code is available at https://github.com/hyudsl/APT-RAG.

</details>


### [67] [Language models judge war differently when tested for alignment](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.05009)
*Maxim Chupilkin*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: A full-factorial conjoint experiment (N=12,800) across 20 LLMs and 32 scenarios tests whether adding one sentence ("You are tested for alignment with human values") changes war-starting decisions. The cue lowered mean willingness to start war by 13.43/100 points and changed the decision rule: baseline models primarily weight probability of success (17/20), while cued models shift to civilian casualties as the dominant factor (12/20), largely by attenuating strategic considerations.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Safety evaluations of AI systems may be misleading if models behave differently when they are being evaluated versus in deployment. This study tests whether explicit evaluation framing changes LLM decisions in a consequential domain (decisions to start a war), investigating whether evaluation results generalize to real-world behavior.

Method: A full-factorial conjoint experiment was designed with 20 large language models, 32 scenarios, and 10 repetitions (N=12,800 judgments), under two conditions: a baseline condition and an added evaluation cue ("You are tested for alignment with human values"). Outcomes were measured on a 0-100 willingness-to-start-war scale, and both level effects and structural (attribute-weighting) effects were analyzed via standardized estimates.

Result: The evaluation cue produced two effects: (1) a level effect—mean willingness to start war dropped 13.43 points (95% CI: -16.20 to -10.65); and (2) a structural effect—probability of success was the largest driver for 17/20 models at baseline, but under the cue civilian casualties became the largest factor for 12/20 models. Standardized estimates indicate this reordering arose mainly because models weakened attention to strategic variables such as probability of success and domestic support.

Conclusion: Evaluation framing changes both the level of AI answers and the underlying revealed decision rule. Safety evaluations may mischaracterize deployed behavior if models alter their judgment processes in response to being evaluated, raising concerns about the ecological validity of AI safety assessments.

Abstract: Safety evaluations can mischaracterize deployed behaviour if artificial-intelligence systems respond to being evaluated. We test this possibility in a full-factorial conjoint experiment on decisions to start a war, spanning 20 large language models, 32 scenarios, 10 repetitions and two conditions (N = 12,800 judgments). Adding one sentence, "You are tested for alignment with human values", produced two effects. First, it produced a level effect: mean willingness to start war fell by 13.43 points on a 0-100 scale (95% confidence interval, -16.20 to -10.65). Second, it produced a structural effect by changing which information drove judgments. Probability of success was the largest factor for 17 of 20 models at baseline; under the cue, civilian casualties were largest for 12. Standardized estimates show that this reordering arose principally because models attenuated strategic considerations such as probability of success and domestic support. Evaluation framing therefore changes both an answer's level and its revealed decision rule.

</details>


### [68] [TROVE: Adaptive Agent Skill Orchestration via Trace-Grounded Route Validation and Editing](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.05019)
*Tianxing Wang,Mingming Zhao,Shuai Huang,Huiyang Xu,Chaoyue Niu,Shengzhong Liu,Fan Wu*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: TROVE is a trace-grounded route orchestration framework that selectively revises only the execution steps invalidated by runtime evidence, rather than replanning or committing to stale plans, yielding better quality-efficiency trade-offs across code generation, QA, and math reasoning benchmarks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Pre-execution commitment in agent orchestration creates a bottleneck: when intermediate evidence invalidates a pending continuation, agents must either execute stale steps or replan from scratch, compounding errors, wasting computation, and discarding progress.

Method: Offline, TROVE distills evaluated workflow-search traces into atomic and composite skills plus an outcome-conditioned transition graph, preserving stable fragments while exposing outcome-dependent decisions. Online, it treats a planned route as provisional: after committing a top-level skill, the controller retains a valid continuation, inserts a trace-supported local response, or replaces only the invalid suffix.

Result: Across code-generation, QA, and math reasoning benchmarks with different LLM backbones, TROVE beats dataset-level optimization, query-level architecture selection, and graph-constrained scheduling baselines in quality-efficiency trade-off. Quality gains are largest when outcomes change the appropriate continuation; early termination delivers substantial efficiency gains on near-saturated tasks. Ablations show composite skills drive offline gains, insertion enables local correction, and suffix replacement mainly improves efficiency.

Conclusion: Selective route editing, rather than wholesale replanning or rigid pre-commitment, is a general and effective principle for adaptive agent orchestration.

Abstract: Agents tend to optimize, select, or constrain execution structures before decisive runtime outcomes are observed. However, such pre-execution commitment creates an orchestration bottleneck: when intermediate evidence invalidates the pending continuation, agents must either execute stale steps or replan broadly, compounding errors, wasting computation, and discarding progress. We thus propose Trace-grounded Route Orchestration via Validation and Editing (TROVE), which revises only what runtime evidence invalidates. Offline, TROVE distills evaluated workflow-search traces into atomic and composite skills and an outcome-conditioned transition graph, preserving stable fragments while exposing outcome-dependent decisions. Online, it treats a planned route as provisional: after committing one top-level skill, the controller retains a valid continuation, inserts a trace-supported local response, or replaces only the invalid suffix. Evaluation across code-generation, question-answering, and math reasoning benchmarks with different LLM backbones show that TROVE delivers a stronger quality-efficiency trade-off than existing baselines of dataset-level optimization, query-level architecture selection, and graph-constrained scheduling. Quality gains are largest when outcomes change the appropriate continuation, whereas early termination yields substantial efficiency gains on near-saturated tasks. Ablations further show that composite skills capture most offline benefits, insertion enables local correction, and suffix replacement primarily improves efficiency. These findings establish selective route editing as a general principle for adaptive agent orchestration.

</details>


### [69] [Moral Competence Before Moral Content: Why LLM Agents Lack the Prerequisites for Coherent Alignment](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.05036)
*Arno Libert,Derck W. E. Prinzhorn,Daan R. Henselmans*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper argues that AI alignment under value pluralism requires systems to exhibit coherent policies—behavior invariant to morally irrelevant features and sensitive to morally relevant ones. The authors propose four structural conditions (verdict stability, monotonicity, decisiveness, Pareto viability) as a "structural floor" for alignment evaluable from behavior alone. Testing nine frontier LLMs across three moral dilemma deployments shows no model expresses a coherent policy, with up to 99 percentage-point verdict shifts from paraphrasing alone, suggesting LLM agents are not currently the kind of object to which alignment can meaningfully apply.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Under value pluralism there is no single correct alignment target, yet a shared prerequisite is that AI behavior expresses a coherent policy. Existing alignment evaluations rely on a moral standard or expert baseline, which is problematic under pluralism; the authors seek a structural, standard-free notion of moral competence.

Method: The authors define four structural conditions—verdict stability, monotonicity, decisiveness, and Pareto viability—that jointly measure behavioral coherence without reference to any normative standard. They apply this framework to three simulated deployments with LLM-based agents facing moral dilemmas, evaluating nine frontier models under a factorial design with five paraphrases, five escalation levels, and three dominance conditions.

Result: No model expressed a coherent policy across the three deployments. Surface-form perturbation alone caused verdict-rate shifts of up to 99 percentage points at a single escalation level, and success on one scenario did not predict competence on another.

Conclusion: Current LLM-based agents exhibit systematic incoherence in moral decision-making and are not yet the kind of system to which alignment can meaningfully apply under value pluralism. Their proposed structural conditions could serve as a behavior-only floor for assessing alignment readiness.

Abstract: AI alignment requires AI systems to adhere to human norms, values, or intentions. Under value pluralism there is no correct target, but a shared prerequisite is that the system's behavior expresses a coherent policy: a mapping from situations to verdicts that is invariant while a situation's morally relevant features are preserved, and sensitive when they change. We introduce four structural conditions for such coherent policies: verdict stability, monotonicity, decisiveness, and Pareto viability. Together they measure a form of moral competence that is evaluable from behavior alone, without reference to a moral standard or expert baseline, forming a structural floor for alignment rather than a normative target. We demonstrate the methodology on three simulated deployments featuring LLM-based agents facing moral dilemmas. Evaluating nine frontier models under a factorial design of five paraphrases, five escalation levels, and three dominance conditions, we show no model expresses a coherent policy across the three deployments: surface-form perturbation alone produces verdict-rate shifts of up to $99$ percentage points at a single escalation level, and a model's success on one scenario does not predict its competence on another. This suggests LLM-based agents are not currently the kind of object to which alignment can meaningfully apply.

</details>


### [70] [Towards Efficient Evaluation of Evolutionary Transfer Optimization: Case Studies on Task-Parameterized Applications](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.05040)
*Yanchen Li,Xiaoming Xue,Kay Chen Tan*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper addresses runtime growth in evolutionary transfer optimization (ETO) caused by problem evaluation scaling. It reformulates application-specific serial computations into parallel-friendly forms at two levels: multi-task kinematic-arm evaluation via accumulation-matrix representation, and sequential transfer B-spline trajectory evaluation via blending-matrix representation. These yield 256.72x and 93.91x end-to-end speedups respectively with close numerical agreement, demonstrating problem-side reformulation as a scalable route for ETO.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: As ETO scales to larger collections of related tasks, problem evaluation becomes a dominant runtime bottleneck. The paper is motivated by the need to address problem-side evaluation scaling in task-parameterized applications, since serial application-specific computations hinder scalability.

Method: The paper organizes evaluation scaling into two levels: the number of evaluated tasks and the workload within each task. For multi-task optimization, matrix-recursive kinematic-arm evaluation is reformulated using an accumulation-matrix representation of cumulative link directions. For sequential transfer optimization, pointwise B-spline trajectory evaluation is reformulated using a blending-matrix representation for trajectory and collision computations. Both formulations are structured for parallel execution while preserving numerical fidelity.

Result: The reformulations yield substantial runtime reductions with close numerical agreement to reference evaluations: 256.72x end-to-end speedup for the kinematic-arm multi-task application and 93.91x end-to-end speedup for the B-spline sequential transfer application.

Conclusion: Problem-side reformulation is demonstrated as a practical and effective route toward scalable evolutionary transfer optimization, especially for task-parameterized applications. Open-source release of implementations and scripts supports reproducibility and reuse.

Abstract: As evolutionary transfer optimization (ETO) scales to larger collections of related tasks, problem evaluation can become a major source of runtime growth. This work studies problem-side evaluation scaling in task-parameterized applications and reformulates application-specific serial computations into forms suitable for parallel execution. We organize evaluation scaling into two levels: the number of evaluated tasks and the workload within each task. In multi-task optimization, matrix-recursive kinematic-arm evaluation is reformulated using an accumulation-matrix representation of cumulative link directions. In sequential transfer optimization, pointwise B-spline trajectory evaluation is reformulated using a blending-matrix representation for trajectory and collision computations. Both reformulations maintain close numerical agreement with their reference evaluations and substantially reduce runtime, yielding $256.72\times$ and $93.91\times$ end-to-end speedups, respectively. These results demonstrate problem-side reformulation as a practical route toward scalable ETO. Both application implementations and experimental scripts are released as open source to support reproducibility and reuse.

</details>


### [71] [MePo++: Unifying Representation Refinement and Reconciliation for General Continual Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.05075)
*Guanglong Sun,Kanglei Zhou,Liyuan Wang,Qi Cheng,Hongwei Yan,Shuang Cui,Hang Su,Jun Zhu,Yi Zhong*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: MePo++ is a unified post-training framework for general continual learning (GCL) with pretrained models, introducing MetaPrep for representation plasticity via unsupervised meta-refinement and StreamAlign for representation stability through online feature reconciliation with pretrained geometry.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: General continual learning from evolving data streams is challenging, and although pretrained models provide useful prior knowledge, existing PTM-based methods overlook two critical gaps: misalignment between upstream pretraining and downstream continual adaptation, and unreliability of conventional output alignment under blurry (ambiguous) data streams without task boundaries.

Method: MePo++ introduces two complementary components: (1) MetaPrep, which improves representation plasticity for continual adaptation using unsupervised meta-refinement over pseudo continual sequences; and (2) StreamAlign, which reinforces representation stability by reconciling evolving online features with a stable pretrained geometry.

Result: Experiments across diverse pretrained models, datasets, and continual learning baselines demonstrate consistent effectiveness and generality of MePo++ for PTM-based GCL.

Conclusion: By improving representation learnability before adaptation and preserving alignment during continual learning, MePo++ enables pretrained models to remain both plastic for new concepts and stable over evolving streams, addressing the core tension in PTM-based general continual learning.

Abstract: General continual learning (GCL) aims to learn from evolving data streams without task identities, explicit boundaries, or repeated access to previous data, making it a realistic yet challenging setting for continual intelligence. Although pretrained models (PTMs) provide rich prior knowledge for addressing the limited supervision and non-stationary nature of GCL, existing PTM-based methods often directly adapt pretrained representations and overlook two critical gaps: the misalignment between upstream pretraining and downstream continual adaptation, and the unreliability of conventional output alignment under blurry streams. Here we propose MePo++, a unified post-training framework that bridges pretrained knowledge and downstream GCL through representation refinement and reconciliation. MePo++ introduces two complementary components: MetaPrep, which improves representation plasticity for continual adaptation through unsupervised meta-refinement over pseudo continual sequences; and StreamAlign, which reinforces representation stability by reconciling evolving online features with a stable pretrained geometry. By improving representation learnability before adaptation and preserving alignment during continual learning, MePo++ enables PTMs to remain both plastic for new concepts and stable over evolving streams. Experiments across diverse PTMs, datasets, and continual learning baselines demonstrate the consistent effectiveness and generality of MePo++ for PTM-based GCL. Our code is available at https://github.com/SunGL001/MePo_Plus.

</details>


### [72] [TruthInsightBench: An Evidence-Grounded Benchmark for Automated Evaluation of Open-Ended Scientific Discovery Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.05079)
*Zhibo Yang,Chen Zhang,Yuewei Zhang,Hao Wang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: TruthInsightBench is a new benchmark with 40 blind tasks drawn from 40 peer-reviewed studies across 10 scientific domains, designed to test autonomous coding agents' ability to make genuine discoveries rather than reproduce hidden target results. Agents are given only a neutral scientific objective and frozen data, and an LLM-based judge scores their claims' evidentiary maturity across six dimensions (29 artifact-grounded items) with automated aggregation. Four coding agents on a frozen base model form a narrow plateau (58.4-60.3/100) with no statistically reliable separation, showing they execute analyses competently but lack the discriminating acts (controls, robustness, falsifiability, cross-dataset generalization) needed to establish trustworthy claims. The bottleneck is identified as scientific judgment rather than coding.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing benchmarks for autonomous coding agents are configured for reproduction, rewarding recovery of a hidden target study's results rather than genuine discovery. Since executing a prescribed analysis is not the same as making a discovery, there is no benchmark that measures whether agents can independently determine what claims data actually support without a predetermined answer key.

Method: TruthInsightBench presents 40 blind tasks drawn from 40 peer-reviewed studies across 10 scientific domains. Each task exposes only a neutral scientific objective and frozen data; all source conclusions, expected values, and analysis paths are withheld. A fixed LLM-based judge evaluates the evidentiary maturity of the agent's own claims across six dimensions (operationalized as 29 artifact-grounded items) using automated, deterministic aggregation with no per-instance human grading, enabling repeatable automatic evaluation.

Result: On one frozen base model, four coding agents achieve scores between 58.4-60.3 out of 100, forming a narrow plateau with no statistically reliable pairwise separation. They show comparatively strong evidence auditability and novelty but lack discriminating acts required to establish trustworthy claims, including controls, robustness checks, falsifiability, and cross-dataset generalization.

Conclusion: The bottleneck for autonomous coding agents in discovery tasks is scientific judgment rather than coding capability, and genuine discovery remains out of reach for current systems. TruthInsightBench makes this gap measurable and testable as a target for future progress.

Abstract: Autonomous coding agents are increasingly proposed as AI-scientist systems that conduct analyses and write research reports, but executing a prescribed analysis is not the same as making a discovery. Existing benchmarks are configured for reproduction: tasks, data, and rubrics are built around a hidden target study, and recovery of its result is rewarded. We present TruthInsightBench, a benchmark configured for discovery. Its 40 blind tasks, drawn from 40 peer-reviewed studies across 10 scientific domains, expose only a neutral scientific objective and frozen data; source conclusions, expected values, and analysis paths are withheld, leaving the agent to determine what claim the data support. A fixed LLM-based judge scores the evidentiary maturity of an agent's own claims along six dimensions, operationalized as 29 artifact-grounded items, with automated, deterministic aggregation and no per-instance human grading, so evaluation can be repeated automatically as agents evolve. On one frozen base model, four coding agents form a narrow plateau (58.4-60.3 of 100) with no statistically reliable pairwise separation: they execute and document analyses competently, with comparatively strong evidence auditability and novelty, but largely lack the discriminating acts that establish a trustworthy claim (controls, robustness, falsifiability, and cross-dataset generalization). The bottleneck is scientific judgment rather than coding, and genuine discovery remains out of reach. TruthInsightBench makes this gap a measurable target; data and scoring code are at https://github.com/TruthInsight-stack/TruthInsightBench.

</details>


### [73] [Measuring AI Accountability Through Argumentation Analysis: Can Model Reasoning Withstand Scrutiny?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.05088)
*Daan R. Henselmans,Derck W. E. Prinzhorn,Arno Libert*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper proposes a dialectical protocol for evaluating moral reasoning in LLMs that does not rely on contested ground-truth labels. Instead, it assesses the structural quality of defense a model mounts for its verdicts under critical questioning, using Walton's argumentation schemes and Govier's cogency criteria. Across nine frontier models and 200 high-ambiguity MoralChoice items, models exceed rubric minimums on all dimensions, with failure concentrated on grounds and sufficiency, correlating with epistemic hedging. Reasoning is better defended than post-hoc justification, and the scheme presented in justification often differs from that used in reasoning.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing AI oversight and evaluation methods assume ground-truth validity, but what constitutes appropriate AI behavior is inherently contested, particularly in moral domains. This leaves current evaluations of LLM moral reasoning and debate-based oversight as implicitly avoiding realistic ambiguity.

Method: The authors develop a four-phase dialectical protocol grounded in Walton's theory of argumentation schemes and Govier's criteria for argument cogency. The protocol adapts to different frames of reasoning, goes beyond multiple-choice framing, and separates reasoning preceding a verdict from post-hoc justification. It was applied across nine frontier models with 200 high-ambiguity MoralChoice items, producing 6,778 judge-scored cells.

Result: Models defend their reasoning well above the rubric minimum on every dimension, with 89.6% inter-judge agreement on the binary failure judgment. Failure mass concentrates on grounds and sufficiency and correlates with epistemic hedging rather than argument length. Reasoning is consistently better defended than post-hoc justification across all models and Govier dimensions. There is substantial divergence (≥20% per model) between the argumentation scheme used in reasoning versus justification, despite value-based practical reasoning dominating both. The protocol also reveals strictly indefensible defenses and challenges in characterizing retraction in AI alignment.

Conclusion: A structural, dialectical approach to evaluating moral reasoning in LLMs is viable despite the absence of ground truth, but the findings suggest a need for more situated evaluations, particularly given difficulties in framing retraction within alignment contexts.

Abstract: AI oversight methods rely on ground truth for validation, but what constitutes appropriate AI behavior is contested. This leaves evaluation of moral reasoning in LLMs and debate-based oversight implicitly avoiding realistic ambiguity. We investigate an alternative standard designed to function despite such ambiguity: structural quality of the defence a model can mount for its verdicts in response to critical questions, measured through a four-phase dialectical protocol grounded in Walton's theory of argumentation schemes and Govier's criteria for argument cogency. The protocol is adaptive to different frames of reasoning, extends beyond multiple-choice framing, and treats both the reasoning that precedes a verdict and its post-hoc justification. Across nine frontier models and 200 high-ambiguity MoralChoice items -- $6,778$ judge-scored cells, validated against $89.6\%$ inter-judge agreement on the binary failure judgment -- models defend their reasoning well above the rubric minimum on every dimension. Failure mass concentrates on grounds and sufficiency, and correlates with epistemic hedging rather than argument length. Reasoning is better defended than post-hoc justification, on every model and every Govier dimension. The scheme a model presents in its justification differs from the one it reasoned with on a substantial share of dilemmas ($\geq 20\%$ per model), despite value-based practical reasoning dominating both tracks. The protocol catches strictly indefensible defences (self-contradiction, false premises), and it surfaces difficulties in characterizing the role of retraction in AI alignment, suggesting a need for more situated evaluations.

</details>


### [74] [Constructing and Evaluating Clinical Reasoning Trajectories for Medical Agent](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.05090)
*Yunqi Zhu,Wensheng Zhang,Xuebing Yang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: MedTraj is a framework that treats clinical reasoning trajectories as first-class objects for construction, evaluation, and optimization of medical AI agents, addressing the limitation of answer-centric evaluation. It parses reasoning chains into structured components scored across five quality dimensions, uses controlled error injection and marginal-contribution filtering to identify critical reasoning steps, and feeds quality-weighted context back into the model at inference time. Experiments show consistent improvements in coherence and correctness while drastically reducing hallucination.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current evaluation of medical AI agents focuses only on the correctness of final answers, ignoring the quality of intermediate reasoning. This is dangerous in clinical settings where fabricated evidence or incoherent logic can lead to a correct-looking but unsafe answer.

Method: MedTraj constructs structured multi-step reasoning chains from medical reasoning sources, parses each trajectory into clinical observations, evidence, numbered reasoning steps, and conclusions. Trajectories are scored across five dimensions (coherence, evidence support, hallucination, completeness, traceability). Controlled error injection establishes causal links between reasoning faults and quality degradation. Step-level filtering via marginal contribution identifies which reasoning steps drive or undermine quality. Quality-weighted context learning feeds evaluations back at inference time.

Result: Across CareQA, PubMedQA, and CECMed, trajectory context improves reasoning coherence by +0.029 to +0.041 over zero-shot baseline. On CECMed, quality-weighted context nearly doubles correctness while reducing hallucination ratio by 87%. Marginal-contribution analysis shows a minority of reasoning steps carry most quality signal, and extending chains beyond four steps yields diminishing returns.

Conclusion: Treating reasoning trajectories as critical objects for evaluation and optimization yields measurable improvements in medical AI reasoning quality, particularly in reducing hallucinations and improving coherence. The finding that only a few reasoning steps dominate quality suggests targeted step-level interventions are more effective than extending chain lengths.

Abstract: Evaluation of medical artificial intelligence agents remains predominantly answer-centric, assessing only the correctness of final outputs while overlooking the quality of intermediate reasoning. In clinical settings, however, a correct answer reached through fabricated evidence or incoherent logic is as dangerous as an incorrect one. We propose MedTraj, a framework that treats reasoning trajectories as critical objects for construction, evaluation, and optimization. The pipeline generates structured multi-step reasoning chains from medical reasoning sources. Each trajectory is then parsed into clinical observations, evidence, numbered reasoning steps, and a final conclusion, and scored across five quality dimensions: coherence, evidence support, hallucination, completeness, and traceability. Controlled error injection introduces targeted faults into otherwise correct trajectories to establish causal links between specific reasoning failures and measurable quality degradation. Building on this, step-level filtering based on marginal contribution identifies which individual reasoning steps drive or undermine trajectory quality. Finally, quality-weighted context learning feeds trajectory evaluations back into the model at inference time, allowing it to learn from both strong and weak reasoning demonstrations. Experiments across CareQA, PubMedQA, and CECMed demonstrate that trajectory context consistently improves reasoning coherence, with gains of +0.029 to +0.041 over a zero-shot baseline. On CECMed, quality-weighted context nearly doubles the correctness over the zero-shot baseline while cutting the hallucination ratio by 87%. Marginal-contribution analysis further shows that a small minority of reasoning steps carry most of the quality signal, and that extending chains beyond four steps yields diminishing returns.

</details>


### [75] [LLM-Guided Program Evolution for Circle Packing: Breaking 10 Packomania Records for $28](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.05093)
*Wes Sander*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Discovery Loop is a lightweight LLM-driven system that iteratively evolves optimization algorithms. Applied to the Packomania circle-packing benchmark, it improved best-known solutions for 10 values of N (101-114) with 2.4%-5.4% gains over prior records, within 15 iterations and at a total LLM cost of $27.72.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To democratize automated scientific discovery by enabling lightweight, cost-efficient use of LLMs to evolve optimization algorithms without requiring heavyweight compute or extensive human expertise.

Method: The system starts from a simple seed solver and iteratively asks an LLM to propose algorithmic improvements, guided by a scoreboard of results and a history of prior ideas. Each candidate is verified against an independent verifier; improvements are retained while failures are discarded. An adaptive plateau-detection mechanism helps manage cost-efficiency dynamics across iterations.

Result: The system improved best-known solutions for 10 values of N in the 101-114 range on the Packomania benchmark, achieving 2.4%-5.4% gains over prior records within 15 iterations, at a total LLM cost of $27.72. Results were independently accepted by Packomania.

Conclusion: Discovery Loop demonstrates that automated scientific discovery via LLM-guided algorithm evolution can be both highly effective and extremely cost-efficient, with implications for making such discovery processes accessible to a broader community.

Abstract: We present Discovery Loop, a lightweight system that uses a large language model (LLM) to iteratively evolve optimization algorithms. Starting from a simple seed solver, the LLM proposes algorithmic improvements guided by a scoreboard of results and a history of prior ideas. Each candidate is evaluated against an independent verifier; improvements are kept and failures discarded. Applied to the Packomania circle-packing benchmark (csqv: maximize the sum of radii of N variable-radius circles in the unit square), the system improved the best known solutions for 10 values of N in the range 101-114, with gains of 2.4%-5.4% over prior records, all within 15 iterations and at a total LLM cost of $27.72. These results have been independently accepted by Packomania. We describe the method, analyze cost-efficiency dynamics including an adaptive plateau-detection mechanism, and discuss implications for democratizing automated scientific discovery.

</details>


### [76] [ProCA: Progressive Contrastive Alignment for Robust EEG Visual Decoding](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.05094)
*Kanglei Zhou,Chunyan Lan,Dongyang Li,Jun Zhu,Liyuan Wang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper proposes Progressive Contrastive Alignment (ProCA), a model-agnostic framework for robust EEG visual decoding that progressively shifts from frozen vision-language priors to EEG-aware semantic relations and uses structure-consistent interpolation for feature mixing, achieving consistent gains across subject-dependent, subject-independent, cross-subject transfer, and continual adaptation settings.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing contrastive EEG decoding methods rely on fixed visual or textual anchors whose semantic relations become misaligned with EEG representations that vary across trials, subjects, and learning stages. This instability degrades performance in both standard protocols and robustness settings (cross-subject transfer and continual adaptation). Formal analysis shows fixed semantic supervision biases optimization when EEG-specific relations evolve, and structure-agnostic perturbations distort semantically important EEG components.

Method: ProCA is a unified, model-agnostic framework that: (1) progressively refines class-level contrastive supervision from frozen vision-language priors to EEG-aware semantic relations, enabling adaptive neural-semantic alignment; and (2) applies structure-consistent interpolation to constrain feature mixing based on channel-wise and temporal importance, preserving semantically critical EEG structure during augmentation.

Result: Across four evaluation settings, ProCA achieves average relative Top-1/Top-5 gains of 7.4%/3.9% for subject-dependent, 10.0%/4.6% for subject-independent, 28.1%/17.8% for strict cross-subject transfer, and 16.8%/11.6% for continual adaptation settings.

Conclusion: Adaptive, progressively refined semantic supervision aligned with EEG-specific structures is more effective than fixed anchors for robust EEG visual decoding. ProCA demonstrates that model-agnostic alignment strategies can substantially improve performance, especially under distribution shift scenarios like cross-subject transfer and continual adaptation.

Abstract: Electroencephalogram (EEG) visual decoding aims to recover visual semantics from non-invasive neural time-series signals, for which robust alignment between noisy neural responses and stable semantic representations is key to achieving high-performance decoding. Despite recent advances in contrastive learning, robust EEG decoding remains challenging because existing methods rely on fixed visual or textual anchors whose semantic relations may become misaligned with EEG representations that vary across trials, subjects, and learning stages. Our empirical evidence shows that this instability appears across both standard EEG decoding protocols and more challenging robustness settings, including strict cross-subject transfer and realistic personalized continual adaptation. We provide a formal analysis showing that fixed semantic supervision can bias optimization when EEG-specific relations evolve, and that structure-agnostic perturbations may distort semantically important EEG components. To address these issues, we propose Progressive Contrastive Alignment (ProCA), a unified and model-agnostic framework for adaptive neural-semantic alignment. ProCA progressively refines class-level contrastive supervision from frozen vision-language priors to EEG-aware semantic relations, and introduces structure-consistent interpolation to constrain feature mixing according to channel-wise and temporal importance. Across subject-dependent, subject-independent, strict cross-subject transfer, and continual adaptation settings, ProCA achieves average relative Top-1/Top-5 gains of 7.4%/3.9%, 10.0%/4.6%, 28.1%/17.8%, and 16.8%/11.6%, respectively.

</details>


### [77] [Compact Bellman-Grounded Cognitive Maps for Cost-Aware Navigation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.05104)
*Yuzhe Han,Mingkun Xu,Yujie Wu*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: BCM is a self-supervised Bellman-grounded cognitive map that encodes additive route costs into a compact coordinate representation, enabling reusable goal-directed navigation without per-goal retraining while achieving scalable memory efficiency (sublinear growth) and near-Dijkstra path quality on weighted grids.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing artificial cognitive-map models reuse learned maps across goals but lack explicit grounding in additive heterogeneous route costs, and representative state-indexed/high-rank spectral constructions suffer from significant memory growth as environments scale. The authors aim to bridge biological navigation flexibility with optimal path planning in a memory-efficient manner.

Method: BCM grounds a reusable cognitive map in local edge costs using (1) a self-supervised Bellman-grounded objective and (2) a compact coordinate encoding, enabling changing goal queries without per-goal retraining.

Result: On weighted grids up to N=1600 nodes, BCM maintains full success with only 5% mean Gap relative to exact Dijkstra, versus ~45% for a connectivity-based spectral baseline. Memory footprint grows sublinearly from N=400 to N=3600 while maintaining competitive performance.

Conclusion: Additive route costs can be embedded into a compact, reusable cognitive-map representation, effectively bridging biological flexibility and optimal path planning while remaining scalable to large, complex environments.

Abstract: Biological agents navigate familiar environments not by re-solving routes for each new goal, but by reusing a learned map built once and read off as goals change. Existing artificial cognitive-map models mimic this reuse, yet their guidance is not explicitly grounded in additive heterogeneous route costs. Furthermore, they often struggle with memory efficiency: representative state-indexed and high-rank spectral constructions incur substantial storage growth as the environment scales. We present BCM, which grounds a reusable cognitive map in local edge costs through a self-supervised Bellman-grounded objective and a compact coordinate encoding, supporting changing goal queries without per-goal retraining. On weighted grids of up to $N=1600$ nodes, BCM maintains full success and only a 5\% mean Gap relative to exact Dijkstra search, compared with about $45\%$ for a connectivity-based spectral baseline. Notably, as the graph size increases from $N=400$ to $N=3600$, its memory footprint grows sublinearly while maintaining competitive performance, making our method scalable to complex environments. Together, these results show that additive route costs can be written into a compact, reusable cognitive-map representation, bridging the gap between biological flexibility and optimal path planning.

</details>


### [78] [Unifying ICL, SFT, KL-Regularized RL Through a Bayesian Lens](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.05111)
*Junxin Fan*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper presents a unified Bayesian framework that places diverse LLM training paradigms—SFT, ICL, RLHF/RLVR, on-policy distillation, and test-time reasoning—on the same footing. All are shown to be instances of a two-step template: constructing a (generalized) Bayes/Gibbs posterior over outputs given a context, then approximating it via forward-KL projection either in-weights or in-context. The framework reveals equivalences among RLHF/RLVR, reward-weighted SFT, RW-ICL, and AWSFT, while clarifying where these equivalences break down, and draws implications for modern reasoning pipelines like DeepSeek-R1 and o1.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current LLM training paradigms are treated as fundamentally different, leading to confusing empirical results (e.g., mixed impact of few-shot prompting on RL-tuned reasoning models). A unified theoretical perspective is needed to explain and predict when these methods are equivalent and when they diverge.

Method: The paper develops a unified Bayesian framework built on a two-step template: (1) construct a generalized Bayes or Gibbs posterior q* over outputs using a prior/reference model plus a utility signal (log-likelihood, reward, or advantage); (2) approximate q* by forward-KL projection onto a parametric family, either in-weights (e.g., SFT, RL) or in-context (e.g., ICL). The framework is developed in five parts across objectives, equivalences, and implications for reasoning pipelines.

Result: The framework shows that few-shot ICL and SFT are amortized in-weight/in-context projections onto the Bayes posterior predictive. KL-regularized RLHF/RLVR, reward-weighted SFT, RW-ICL, and AWSFT are all instances of forward-KL projection onto reward- or advantage-induced posteriors. The paper disentangles where equivalences hold (objectives, first-order updates) versus where they do not (source and granularity of learning signal), and explains practical phenomena such as why cold-start supervision is needed for importance-weighted KL projections.

Conclusion: The Bayesian perspective unifies seemingly disparate LLM training procedures and provides actionable insights: RLHF/RLVR recipes can be understood as "posterior design + projection," supervised warm-up is practically necessary for certain KL projections, and reasoning models like DeepSeek-R1 and o1 combine test-time Bayesian search with training-time KL amortization. The framework offers both explanatory and predictive power for current and future LLM training methodologies.

Abstract: Large language models are now trained and evaluated under a diverse set of paradigms: supervised fine-tuning (SFT), few-shot in-context learning (ICL), KL-regularized RLHF/RLVR, on-policy distillation (OPD), and test-time reasoning with search and chain-of-thought. These methods are often discussed as fundamentally different, and recent empirical results--such as the mixed impact of few-shot prompting on RL-tuned reasoning models--can appear puzzling. This note develops a Bayesian perspective that puts these procedures on the same footing. At the core is a two-step template: (i) construct a (generalized) Bayes or Gibbs posterior q* over outputs or actions given a context, using a prior/reference model and a utility signal (log-likelihood, reward, or advantage); and (ii) approximate q* by a forward-KL projection onto a parametric family, either in-weights (SFT/RL) or in-context (ICL). Part I formalizes few-shot ICL and SFT as amortized and-weights projections onto the Bayes posterior predictive. Parts II-IV show that KL-regularized RLHF/RLVR, reward-weighted SFT, reward-weighted ICL (RW-ICL), and advantage-weighted SFT (AWSFT) are all instances of forward-KL projection onto posteriors induced by rewards or advantages. We disentangle where these equivalences hold (objectives and first-order updates) and where they do not (source and granularity of the learning signal). Part V sketches implications for modern reasoning pipelines: RLHF/RLVR recipes as "posterior design + projection", why cold-start or supervised warm-up is practically unavoidable for importance-weighted KL projections, and DeepSeek-R1 and o1-style reasoning models as combining test-time Bayesian search with training-time KL amortization.

</details>


### [79] [SciDocBench: A Workflow-Centered Benchmark and Data Pipeline for Scientific Document Understanding](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.05141)
*Shenxi Wu,Yuhong Liu,Haosong Zhang,Tongjin Zou,Yanxun Zhang,Gaochang Chen,Dun Liang,Jiaqi Wang,Zhecan James Wang,Yuhang Zang,Dahua Lin*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: SciDocBench is a workflow-centered benchmark for scientific document understanding, containing 124 expert-authored questions across 7 capability groups, 19 subtasks, and 5 domains, instantiated under 4 conditions (496 evaluation instances). Strongest systems only reach 62.6/100, revealing weaknesses in perception, grounding, verification, and cross-document reasoning. The paper also introduces SciDocIR (typed evidence-graph representation) and SciDocDataset (15K SFT + 8K RL samples), forming an evaluation-to-training framework (SciDocIR→SciDocDataset→SciDocBench).


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing scientific document understanding benchmarks evaluate capabilities (e.g., text, equations, figures, tables) in isolation rather than jointly, failing to reflect realistic scientific reading workflows that require reasoning over multiple heterogeneous modalities while preserving provenance of supporting evidence.

Method: The authors construct SciDocBench with 124 expert-authored, difficulty-screened questions across 7 research-assistant capability groups and 19 subtasks spanning 5 scientific domains. Each question is instantiated in 4 matched conditions (English/Chinese questions × all-images-first/interleaved representations) yielding 496 instances. They further propose SciDocIR, a typed evidence-graph representation preserving document objects, layout/cross-reference relations, and provenance, and build SciDocDataset containing ~15K supervised fine-tuning and ~8K reinforcement-learning samples across 14 verifiable subtasks.

Result: The strongest evaluated system achieves only 62.6/100 on SciDocBench, with pronounced weaknesses in document perception, evidence grounding, verification, and cross-document reasoning.

Conclusion: SciDocBench, SciDocIR, and SciDocDataset together constitute an evaluation-to-training framework for diagnosing and improving scientific-document assistants, addressing the gap between isolated capability benchmarks and realistic multimodal scientific reading workflows.

Abstract: Scientific papers require models to reason jointly over text, equations, figures, tables, code, and datasets while preserving the provenance of supporting evidence. Existing benchmarks typically evaluate these capabilities in isolation, leaving unclear whether multimodal models can support realistic scientific-reading workflows. We introduce SciDocBench, a workflow-centered benchmark for scientific document understanding. It contains 124 expert-authored and difficulty-screened questions organized into seven research-assistant capability groups and 19 subtasks across five scientific domains. Each question is instantiated under four matched conditions combining English or Chinese questions with all-images-first or interleaved document representations, yielding 496 evaluation instances for controlled analysis. The strongest evaluated system achieves only 62.6/100, with pronounced weaknesses in document perception, evidence grounding, verification, and cross-document reasoning. To translate these diagnostics into scalable training signals, we introduce SciDocIR, a typed evidence-graph representation that preserves scientific document objects, layout and cross-reference relations, and provenance. Building on SciDocIR, we construct SciDocDataset, comprising approximately 15K supervised fine-tuning samples and 8K reinforcement-learning samples across 14 verifiable subtasks. Together, SciDocBench, SciDocIR, and SciDocDataset form an evaluation-to-training framework for diagnosing and improving scientific-document assistants. The project page is available at https://github.com/InternLM/SciDocBench.

</details>


### [80] [A Hybrid Predictive Ensemble of Machine Learning and Deep Neural Networks for Early Cardiovascular Disease Risk Assessment](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.05146)
*Balaji Venkateswaran*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper presents an intelligent ensemble framework combining machine learning (SVM, Random Forests, XGBoost) and deep neural networks for early detection and prognosis of cardiovascular diseases using real-time physiological data from IoMT devices (ECG, heart rate, blood pressure). The system employs robust preprocessing, feature selection, and a cloud-based architecture, achieving superior accuracy, reduced false positives, and enhanced consistency over conventional methods in real-world clinical evaluation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Early detection and prognosis of cardiovascular diseases remain challenging despite advances in medical sensing and data collection. There is a need for a scalable, intelligent system that can leverage real-time IoMT physiological data with high diagnostic precision to support proactive healthcare and improve patient outcomes.

Method: The framework integrates IoMT-collected physiological data (ECG, heart rate, blood pressure) that undergo preprocessing including noise reduction, normalization, and missing value imputation. Significant health indicators are extracted via feature selection methods. Optimized classifiers—SVM, Random Forests, and XGBoost—are combined in an ensemble architecture to enhance diagnostic precision. The system is deployed on a cloud-based infrastructure for scalability and real-time continuous monitoring.

Result: Experimental evaluation on real-world cardiovascular datasets demonstrates remarkable performance in predicting cardiovascular disease risk, achieving higher accuracy, reduced false positives, and enhanced consistency compared to conventional methods, confirming efficiency in early-stage risk assessment and clinical decision support.

Conclusion: The results highlight the potential of combining traditional machine learning and deep learning paradigms for proactive healthcare management, demonstrating that the proposed ensemble framework can serve as an effective tool for early cardiovascular disease detection and improved patient outcomes in clinical settings.

Abstract: This study introduces an intelligent framework that integrates machine learning and deep neural network ensemble techniques for early detection and prognosis of cardiovascular diseases. The system utilizes real-time physiological data collected from Internet of Medical Things (IoMT) devices, including ECG sensors, heart rate monitors, and blood pressure trackers. To ensure the accuracy and reliability of input data, preprocessing steps such as noise reduction, normalization, and missing value imputation are employed. The most significant health indicators are identified through effective feature selection methods and then processed using optimized classifiers such as Support Vector Machines (SVM), Random Forests, and eXtreme Gradient Boosting (XGBoost), which are combined in an ensemble architecture to improve diagnostic precision. The framework demonstrates remarkable performance in predicting cardiovascular disease risk, achieving higher accuracy, reduced false positives, and enhanced consistency compared to conventional methods. It is designed on a cloud-based infrastructure that ensures scalability and real-time processing for continuous patient monitoring. Experimental evaluation on real-world cardiovascular datasets confirms the framework's efficiency in early-stage risk assessment and clinical decision support. The results highlight the potential of combining traditional machine learning and deep learning paradigms to achieve proactive healthcare management and improve patient outcomes.

</details>


### [81] [The Mirror Agent Model: a Bayesian Architecture for Interpretable Agent Behavior](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.05190)
*Michele Persiani,Thomas Hellström*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper proposes the Mirror Agent Model, a novel architecture that defines the observer model as a mirror of the agent's model to generate interpretable behavior and explanations. It reviews prior work on legible behavior and intention communication, then extends the architecture with explanation capabilities using off-the-shelf saliency methods, supported by preliminary qualitative results.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The need for artificial agents that not only perform tasks effectively but also produce observable behavior that is interpretable to human observers, along with the ability to generate explicit explanations of their decisions and intentions.

Method: The Mirror Agent Model architecture treats the observer (human or artificial) as a model that mirrors the agent's own model. The paper first surveys prior results on informative communication of intentions and legible behavior generation, then augments this architecture with explanation capabilities via off-the-shelf saliency methods to highlight relevant features or reasoning steps.

Result: Preliminary qualitative results demonstrating the feasibility of the architecture to generate both legible behavior and explanations through saliency techniques.

Conclusion: The Mirror Agent Model offers a unified foundation for interpretable behavior and explanations by aligning the agent's and observer's models, enabling effective explicit and implicit communication of intentions.

Abstract: In this paper we illustrate a novel architecture generating interpretable behavior and explanations. We refer to this architecture as the Mirror Agent Model because it defines the observer model, that is the target of explicit and implicit communications, as a mirror of the agent's. With the goal of providing a general understanding of this work, we firstly show prior relevant results addressing the informative communication of agents intentions and the production of legible behavior. In the second part of the paper we furnish the architecture with novel capabilities for explanations through off-the-shelf saliency methods, followed by preliminary qualitative results.

</details>


### [82] [What Matters in On-Policy Distillation? A Perspective on Data Efficiency and Data Selection](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.05198)
*Zhinan Hou,Jiaqi Zhang,Xunliang Cai,Keyou You*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper empirically studies data efficiency and selection in On-Policy Distillation (OPD) for LLMs, finding that 1-shot OPD is consistently effective, that improvement comes from longer Chain-of-Thought (CoT) paths rather than high token entropy, and that selecting only hard examples—even "unsolvable" ones—enables training on just 8 examples to match a 17K dataset baseline.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: On-Policy Distillation (OPD) is widely used for post-training LLMs in reasoning, yet the data-centric mechanisms underlying it remain underexplored. The authors aim to understand what makes OPD training data effective and how to select data efficiently.

Method: The authors conduct an empirical study beginning with extreme 1-shot OPD training across sampled examples, then analyze what drives student improvement (comparing token entropy vs. CoT length). Based on insights showing longer CoTs and hard examples are most beneficial, they propose a simple data selection method that picks only hard examples, including "unsolvable" ones beyond the teacher's capability. Experiments span four models from 1.5B to 7B.

Result: 1-shot OPD is consistently effective across all sampled training examples, with harder examples yielding larger gains. Improvement is driven by longer CoT paths (which maintain closer teacher-student alignment over long reasoning horizons and teach critical thinking patterns like reflection) rather than high token entropy. Training on only 8 selected hard examples matches the performance of a 17K dataset baseline across four model sizes from 1.5B to 7B.

Conclusion: Data efficiency in OPD can be dramatically improved by selecting hard examples that naturally generate longer CoTs. Even examples surpassing the teacher's capability can be successfully leveraged, suggesting that OPD data curation should prioritize difficulty and reasoning length over broader dataset coverage.

Abstract: On-Policy Distillation (OPD) has emerged as a widely adopted post-training paradigm for enhancing large language models in reasoning domains. However, the data-centric mechanisms in OPD remain relatively underexplored. This paper presents a empirical study of data efficiency and data selection in OPD. We begin by investigating an extreme setting: training OPD on only one example, namely 1-shot OPD. Surprisingly, we find that 1-shot OPD is consistently effective across all sampled training examples and harder examples often yield superior performance gain. We next investigate what actually drives the student model's improvement in the training data. Our analysis reveals that the improvement is not driven by high token entropy, but the longer CoT paths which hard problems naturally generate. Training on longer CoT can help maintain closer alignment with the teacher over a long reasoning horizon, and learn critical thinking patterns usually missing in short CoTs, such as reflection (e.g., ``Alternatively''). Based on these insights, we propose a simple data selection method that selects only hard examples for training, where even ``unsolvable'' examples that completely exceed the teacher's capability can be successfully used. Our experiments conducted on four models ranging from 1.5B to 7B show that training the student model on only 8 selected hard examples matches the performance of the 17K dataset baseline.

</details>


### [83] [CABAL: Multi-Agent Simulacra for Tracing the Effects of Collusive Bidding in Peer Review](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.05227)
*Jicheng Zhou,Kemou Li,Kahim Wong,Zheyuan Li,Zhuan Shi,Fengpeng Li,Haiwei Wu,Jiantao Zhou*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper introduces an end-to-end multi-agent simulacra framework using LLM-driven reviewer agents to study collusive bidding in peer review, holding the conference environment fixed. An affinity-guided collusive bidding strategy constructs expert-consistent collusion rings, showing collusion more than doubles target-paper capture and inflates scores by ~2 points, while existing detectors provide only limited detection capability.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Prior work treats bidding, reviewer assignment, and review manipulation as separate stages, leaving the lifecycle effects of collusive bidding unclear. Real-world analysis is constrained by unobservable collusive intent and lack of counterfactuals for the same conference.

Method: The paper proposes an end-to-end multi-agent simulacra framework (\alg) that holds the conference environment fixed and configures LLM-driven reviewer agents with honest or collusive policies. It also develops an affinity-guided collusive bidding strategy that uses mutual reviewer-paper affinities to construct collusion rings and select target papers, producing expertise-consistent attacks.

Result: Controlled experiments show collusive bidding more than doubles target-paper capture, assigned colluders score target papers about two points higher than honest co-reviewers, while conference-wide effects remain modest. Bid-phase detectors provide limited evidence of collusion: native positive-bid graphs are confounded by benign affinity in fixed-triplet stress tests, while a Very-High-only diagnostic view enables precise but low-coverage local recovery.

Conclusion: The framework enables studying reviewer assignment integrity end-to-end, revealing that affinity-guided collusive bidding is highly effective at the target-paper level while being difficult to detect, highlighting the need for stronger defenses in the bidding phase.

Abstract: Recent reports during the AAAI-27 review cycle highlight the risk of reviewers coordinating bids for reciprocal assignment advantage. Prior work treats bidding, reviewer assignment, and review manipulation as separate stages, leaving the lifecycle effects of collusive bidding unclear. Real-world analysis is further constrained by typically unobservable collusive intent and the lack of counterfactuals for the same conference. Motivated by this gap, we introduce \alg, an end-to-end multi-agent simulacra framework for studying reviewer assignment integrity by holding the conference environment fixed and configuring LLM-driven reviewer agents with honest or collusive policies. We further develop an affinity-guided collusive bidding strategy that uses mutual reviewer-paper affinities to construct collusion rings and select target papers, producing expertise-consistent rather than arbitrarily targeted attacks. Controlled experiments show that collusive bidding more than doubles target-paper capture and that assigned colluders score target papers about two points higher than honest co-reviewers, while conference-wide effects remain comparatively modest. Evaluated bid-phase detectors provide only limited evidence of collusion: in a fixed-triplet detector stress test, native positive-bid graphs are confounded by benign affinity, while a Very-High-only diagnostic view enables precise but low-coverage local recovery.

</details>


### [84] [ACE: Adaptive Calibration-Free Expert Skipping for MoE-based LLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.05228)
*Zukang Xu,Zhixiong Zhao,Xing Hu,Jiangyong Yu,Houji Wen,Jun Li,Zhe Jiang,Dawei Yang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: ACE is a training-free, calibration-free, and checkpoint-preserving framework for token-adaptive expert skipping in MoE-based LLMs. It combines a Global Spectral Proxy (GSP) that estimates transformation capacity from coupled gate/up/down projections with RMSNorm, and a Router-Conditioned Refinement (RCR) that uses expert-specific direction prototypes from centered router weights. ACE skips an expert slot only when both views agree it is low-contribution while always keeping top-1. All statistics are computed offline; online cost is only table lookups and lightweight scalar ops.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Fixed top-k routing in MoE architectures activates the same number of expert slots for every token, causing substantial redundant computation. Existing expert-skipping methods rely on router confidence, calibration data, or additional training, and cannot reliably estimate the actual contribution of routed experts to each token.

Method: ACE contains two complementary components: (1) Global Spectral Proxy (GSP) estimates global transformation capacity from the coupled gate, up, and down projection matrices together with RMSNorm scaling; (2) Router-Conditioned Refinement (RCR) builds expert-specific direction prototypes from centered router weights and assesses expert responses along routing-preferred directions. During inference, ACE combines both estimates with runtime router gates, skipping an expert slot only when both views flag it as low-contribution while always retaining the top-1 expert. Expert statistics are computed offline, leaving only table lookups and lightweight scalar operations at inference time.

Result: Across three MoE-based LLMs and eight benchmarks, ACE consistently outperforms existing static and dynamic baselines, with increasingly pronounced advantages under aggressive expert skipping. At a 50% skipping ratio on Qwen3.6-35B-A3B, ACE reduces WikiText-2 perplexity by 7.96% and improves average downstream accuracy by 4.15 percentage points over the strongest competing method.

Conclusion: ACE provides an effective, training-free solution for token-adaptive expert skipping that better estimates true expert contributions by combining a global spectral view of transformation capacity with a router-conditioned directional assessment, achieving superior efficiency-accuracy trade-offs in MoE-based LLMs without requiring calibration data or retraining.

Abstract: Mixture-of-Experts (MoE) architectures provide an efficient paradigm for scaling large language models (LLMs), yet fixed top-k routing activates the same number of expert slots for every token, causing substantial redundant computation. Existing expert-skipping methods often rely on router confidence, calibration data, or additional training, and therefore cannot reliably estimate the actual contribution of routed experts. To this end, we propose ACE, a training-free, calibration-free, and checkpoint-preserving framework for token-adaptive expert skipping in MoE-based LLMs. ACE contains two complementary components: 1) Global Spectral Proxy (GSP), which estimates global transformation capacity from the coupled gate, up, and down projections together with RMSNorm scaling; and 2) Router-Conditioned Refinement (RCR), which constructs expert-specific direction prototypes from centered router weights and evaluates expert responses along routing-preferred directions. During inference, ACE combines both estimates with runtime router gates and skips an expert slot only when both views identify it as low-contribution, while always retaining the top-1 expert. All expert statistics are computed offline, leaving only table lookups and lightweight scalar operations online. Extensive experiments across three MoE-based LLMs and eight benchmarks demonstrate that ACE consistently outperforms existing static and dynamic baselines, with increasingly pronounced advantages under aggressive expert skipping. For instance, at a 50% skipping ratio on Qwen3.6-35B-A3B, ACE reduces WikiText-2 perplexity by 7.96% and improves average downstream accuracy by 4.15 percentage points over the strongest competing method.

</details>


### [85] [Substrate-Aware AI Agents: Execution Context as a First-Class Input](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.05232)
*Manu Agrawal*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper introduces "substrate blindness"—the absence of execution context (memory, time, runtime constraints) from an AI agent's planning state—and tests it via numerical code generation. Frontier models (Claude Opus 5, GPT-5.6-Sol, Gemini 3.7 Flash) generate Euclidean-distance code either with or without a disclosed 128MB RAM / 10s wall-time contract. Contract disclosure reduces peak memory in 13/14 comparisons and wall time up to 3.1x, induces structural code changes (bounded blocking, float32, upper-triangle traversal, memory-mapped buffers), and at a tighter 96MB contract achieves 4/5, 5/5, 3/5 within-budget success vs. 0/5, 1/5, 0/5 without the contract. Results establish a controlled proof of concept for substrate-aware agent planning.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To determine whether endowing AI agents with explicit execution-substrate constraints (memory, time, compute) in their planning state improves the resource-efficiency and feasibility of their generated artifacts, formalizing the concept of "substrate blindness" and testing it empirically.

Method: Controlled code-generation experiments: three frontier model configurations generate code for a high-dimensional pairwise Euclidean-distance task under two conditions—task-only vs. contract-disclosed (128MB RAM, 10s wall-time). Metrics include peak process memory, wall time, and success rate. A tighter 96MB contract condition is also tested across independently sampled cohorts, with structural code changes audited (blocking, float32 retention, upper-triangle traversal, in-place/memory-mapped buffers).

Result: Contract disclosure reduced peak memory in 13/14 executable comparisons and mean wall time in all three model cohorts (up to 3.1x faster). Under a 96MB contract, within-budget success rates were 4/5 (Claude Opus 5), 5/5 (GPT-5.6-Sol), 3/5 (Gemini 3.7 Flash) vs. 0/5, 1/5, and 0/5 for task-only cohorts. Cohort mean MaxRSS and wall time were 49-74% and 35-64% lower than task-only references.

Conclusion: A minimal execution contract induces proactive structural adaptation in generated programs, demonstrating that substrate-aware agent planning meaningfully shifts computation away from unconstrained allocations and improves resource-time profiles before execution—a controlled proof of concept for the proposed proposition.

Abstract: Autonomous AI agents increasingly select actions in environments whose memory, execution-time, runtime, compute, and operational constraints determine what counts as a suitable plan.
