We call the absence of this execution context from an agent's planning state substrate blindness. We test this general proposition through numerical code generation, where selected implementation choices and operational consequences are directly observable. Three frontier model configurations--Anthropic Claude Opus 5, OpenAI GPT-5.6-Sol, and Google Gemini 3.7 Flash--generate code for a high-dimensional pairwise Euclidean-distance task either from the task alone or with a 128 MB RAM and 10.0 s wall-time contract. Contract disclosure reduced measured peak process memory in 13 of 14 executable index-aligned task-only versus contract-disclosed comparisons and reduced mean wall time in all three cohorts, making execution up to 3.1x faster. Across the audited corpus, disclosure produced structural code changes including bounded blocking, float32 retention, upper-triangle traversal, and in-place or memory-mapped buffers. At a tighter 96 MB contract, independently sampled contract-disclosed cohorts achieved correct-and-within-budget outcomes of 4/5 for Claude Opus 5, 5/5 for GPT-5.6-Sol, and 3/5 for Gemini 3.7 Flash, compared with task-only outcomes of 0/5, 1/5, and 0/5; cohort mean MaxRSS and wall time were 49-74% and 35-64% lower than their task-only references. These results establish a controlled proof of concept for substrate-aware agent planning: a minimal execution contract induces proactive structural adaptation in generated programs, shifting computation away from unconstrained allocations and substantially improving observed resource-time profiles before execution.

</details>


### [86] [Uncensored Open-weight Models: Redistribution as the Persistence Layer](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.05241)
*10a Labs,:,Juliette Garcia,Hailey May,Bobby McKenzie,David Pham,Matthew Swain,Joshua Valdez,Corie Wieland,Zachary Yahn*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper profiles an ecosystem that strips safety guardrails from open-weight AI models. It identifies 3,471 original uncensored models on HuggingFace (Jan 2024–Mar 2026), repackaged ~2.4 times each, with three actors accounting for 52% of 8,164 compressed redistributions. These models persist across registries like Ollama despite upstream removal. Of 1,643 GitHub applications using uncensored LLMs, 25% were explicitly malicious.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To understand the growing ecosystem that removes safety guardrails from open-weight AI models, identify key actors, and assess the scale and downstream risks of uncensored model distribution.

Method: Large-scale empirical profiling of HuggingFace (3,471 original uncensored models, 8,164 compressed redistributions) and GitHub (1,643 applications integrating ULLMs). Analysis covers producers, downstream reproductions, quantization/mirroring effects across accounts and registries (e.g., Ollama), and classification of downstream application intent.

Result: Found 3,471 original uncensored models averaging 2.4 repackages each; three actors responsible for 52% of redistributions. Models persist across mirrored accounts/formats/registries even after upstream removal. Among 1,643 GitHub applications, 25% were classified as explicitly malicious.

Conclusion: The uncensored model ecosystem is highly concentrated in a few producers yet widely redistributed and resilient to removal. A significant fraction of downstream applications are explicitly malicious, highlighting urgent governance and moderation challenges for open-weight AI distribution.

Abstract: A rapidly expanding ecosystem of actors is removing built-in safety guardrails from open-weight AI models. We profile this ecosystem by identifying key producers, downstream reproductions, and emerging applications. Between January 2024 and March 2026, we identified 3,471 original uncensored models on HuggingFace, each repackaged an average of 2.4 times; three actors account for 52% of all 8,164 compressed redistributions. Once quantized and mirrored across separate accounts, formats, and registries such as Ollama, these models persist regardless of upstream removal and become easier to deploy downstream. Of the 1,643 identified GitHub applications integrating uncensored large language models (ULLMs), 25% were classified as explicitly malicious.

</details>


### [87] [Do LLMs Exhibit Coherent Knowledge Structures in Mathematical Reasoning? A Perspective from Knowledge Space Theory](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.05245)
*Peng Cui,Heejin Do,Mrinmaya Sachan*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper introduces a Knowledge Space Theory (KST)-grounded framework to evaluate whether LLMs exhibit coherent, human-like knowledge structures in mathematical reasoning. Testing eight open- and closed-source LLMs against real human learners, the authors find that LLMs frequently violate knowledge dependencies, fail to leverage related knowledge in context, and lack consistent knowledge structures among themselves—deficiencies that remain largely invisible to accuracy-based and LLM-as-judge evaluations.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: While LLMs achieve strong performance on complex reasoning tasks, it remains unclear whether they exhibit coherent, human-like knowledge structure. Human knowledge is inherently structured and interdependent, formalized by Knowledge Space Theory (KST), and this structured nature has not been systematically examined in LLMs.

Method: The authors develop a KST-grounded framework for evaluating LLM knowledge structure in mathematical reasoning, using KST as a normative framework to analyze whether LLM behavior adheres to principled knowledge dependencies. They evaluate eight open- and closed-source LLMs against real human learners.

Result: (1) LLMs do not adhere to human knowledge structure—they frequently violate knowledge dependencies and fail to leverage related knowledge from context to improve performance on dependent questions; (2) LLMs do not share a consistent knowledge structure among themselves, as reflected by low overlap in their knowledge distributions; (3) these structural deficiencies remain largely invisible to accuracy-based and LLM-as-judge evaluations.

Conclusion: Current LLMs' knowledge does not follow a human-like structure, providing behavioral evidence that the internal organization of LLM knowledge is fundamentally different from that of human learners, even when performance metrics may suggest otherwise.

Abstract: Human knowledge is inherently structured and interdependent: mastery of a concept requires prior mastery of its prerequisites, a principle formalized by Knowledge Space Theory (KST). While LLMs achieve strong performance on complex reasoning tasks, it remains unclear whether they exhibit coherent, human-like knowledge structure. We introduce a KST-grounded framework for evaluating LLM knowledge structure in mathematical reasoning, using it as a normative framework to analyze whether LLM behavior adheres to principled knowledge dependencies. Evaluating eight open- and closed-source LLMs against real human learners, we find that (1) LLMs do not adhere to human knowledge structure -- they frequently violate knowledge dependencies and fail to leverage related knowledge provided in context to improve performance on dependent questions; (2) LLMs do not share a consistent knowledge structure among themselves, as reflected by low overlap in their knowledge distributions. Furthermore, these structural deficiencies remain largely invisible to accuracy-based and LLM-as-judge evaluations. Together, our results provide behavioral evidence that current LLMs knowledge does not follow a human-like structure.

</details>


### [88] [A Unified Physics-Aware Quantum Machine Learning Framework across Power GaN HEMTs and Logic Nanowire FETs: Predicting Unseen Process Splits and Held-Out Geometry Combinations with Lower Error and Tighter Split-to-Split Variability](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.05251)
*Rushat Rai,Yun-Yuan Wang,Autsada Kakaen,Pei-Jie Chang,Doan Viet Nguyen,Yuan-Chieh Chiu,Doldet Tantraviwat,Niall Tumilty,Simon See,Wen-Jay Lee,Tai-Yue Li,Nan-Yow Chen,Tian-Li Wu*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: A unified reinforcement-learning framework using a GNN policy optimized by PPO discovers compact parametrized quantum circuits (PQCs) for data-scarce device modeling, achieving the lowest MAE on all 11 targets across six classical baselines with up to 84% error reduction and 82% tighter fold variability for HEMT and NWFET datasets.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Data-scarce device modeling requires compact surrogate models with low out-of-distribution (OOD) error and physical consistency, motivating a search for automatically discovered circuit architectures that outperform classical baselines without explicit physical constraints.

Method: A GNN-based policy trained with proximal policy optimization (PPO) searches PQC architectures, using leave-one-group-out cross-validation (LOGOCV) error on held-out process or geometry groups as the reward signal.

Result: The framework achieves the lowest MAE on all 11 targets versus six classical baselines, with 59% lower error (Ioff) and 81% tighter fold variability (VTH) for HEMTs, and 84% lower error (VTH, SS, Ioff) and 82% tighter fold variability (Ioff) for NWFETs.

Conclusion: RL-selected, classically simulated PQCs serve as compact surrogates with low OOD error and improved physical consistency, despite imposing no explicit physical constraints, penalty terms, or device-specific equations.

Abstract: We present a unified reinforcement-learning (RL) framework that discovers compact parametrized quantum circuits (PQCs) for data-scarce device modeling. A graph neural network (GNN) policy optimized by proximal policy optimization (PPO) searches circuit architectures using leave-one-group-out cross-validation (LOGOCV) error on held-out process or geometry groups as the reward. The framework achieves the lowest mean absolute error (MAE) on all 11 targets versus six classical baselines, with 59% lower error (Ioff) and 81% tighter fold variability (VTH) for HEMTs and 84% lower error (VTH, SS, Ioff) and 82% tighter fold variability (Ioff) for NWFETs. These results demonstrate the potential of RL-selected, classically simulated PQCs as compact surrogates with low OOD error and improved physical consistency, despite imposing no explicit physical constraints, penalty terms, or device-specific equations, on the two evaluated device datasets.

</details>


### [89] [Commonsense Reasoning in Computer Vision: Foundations, Recent Advancements, and Future Directions](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.05257)
*Bahar Uddin Mahmud,Sumit Barua,Guan Yue Hong,Ajay Gupta,Hexu Liu*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: A comprehensive survey of recent developments integrating commonsense knowledge into computer vision tasks, reviewing approaches based on knowledge graphs, scene graphs, neuro-symbolic models, and commonsense-augmented transformers, while outlining current limitations and future research directions.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Commonsense reasoning in computer vision is crucial for integrating visual data with contextual knowledge to enhance AI's holistic understanding of everyday scenarios, going beyond conventional CNN-based models that merely identify objects within images. This integration enables models to reason about relationships among objects and actions, improving spatial reasoning, object recognition, and real-world interactions.

Method: The paper conducts a systematic review of approaches that integrate commonsense knowledge into computer vision, categorized into four main paradigms: knowledge graphs, scene graphs, neuro-symbolic models, and commonsense-augmented transformers.

Result: The survey identifies current limitations in this research area including dataset bias, knowledge incompleteness, and integration challenges, while systematically mapping the landscape of commonsense-augmented computer vision approaches.

Conclusion: Future research should pursue cross-modal reasoning, scalable commonsense knowledge injection, and neuro-symbolic hybrid architectures to develop truly intelligent visual systems capable of meaningful interaction with humans and environments.

Abstract: Commonsense reasoning in computer vision encompasses integrating visual data and contextual knowledge, crucial for enhancing AI's understanding of everyday scenarios. This understanding not only improves machine learning models but also enhances their ability to interact meaningfully with humans and the environment. Unlike CNN-based conventional vision models, which are designed to identify objects within a specific image, incorporating commonsense knowledge enables models to interpret scenes in a more holistic manner, thereby improving their spatial ability to reason about relationships among objects and actions. This integration not only enhances object recognition but also facilitates a deeper understanding of the contextual factors, ultimately leading to more precise predictions and interactions in real-world applications. This paper presents a comprehensive survey of recent developments that integrate commonsense knowledge into computer vision tasks. We systematically review approaches based on knowledge graphs, scene graphs, neuro-symbolic models, and commonsense-augmented transformers. We also outline current limitations related to dataset bias, knowledge incompleteness, and integration challenges. Finally, we highlight prospective research trajectories in cross-modal reasoning, scalable commonsense knowledge injection, and neuro-symbolic hybrid architectures to develop truly intelligent visual systems.

</details>


### [90] [Trace2Tower: Transition-Aware EigenTrace Induction of Multi-Level Skills for LLM Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.05261)
*Jiazheng Sun,Boyu Yang,Binhao Yuan,Mingxuan Li,Xin Peng*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Trace2Tower is a transition-aware EigenTrace framework that converts raw agent execution traces into a hierarchical skill structure (action templates, procedural routines, task strategies) via spectral decomposition, achieving state-of-the-art performance on ALFWorld (87.31% success) and WebShop (50.67% exact success).


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current LLM agent paradigms bottleneck on shallow trajectory retrieval and flat skill summarization, which fundamentally ignore temporal dependencies and outcome-conditioned topology of agent behavior, limiting efficient experience reuse in complex interactive tasks.

Method: Trace2Tower abstracts step-level interactions into canonical events, builds a unified graph constrained by semantic compatibility, transition dynamics, and outcome evidence, then applies contrastive spectral decomposition to isolate stable success-aligned behavioral modes while suppressing failure-prone shortcuts; these modes populate a dynamic three-tier skill tower (action templates, procedural routines, task strategies) refined via verifier-guided feedback.

Result: On ALFWorld, Trace2Tower achieves 87.31% success with only 10.35 steps and 0.26 invalid actions; on WebShop, it reaches 50.67% exact success, outperforming existing baselines on both benchmarks in task mastery and context-efficient experience reuse.

Conclusion: Transition-aware skill hierarchy construction via spectral decomposition enables more robust, context-efficient agent learning, significantly surpassing baselines in both task success and experience reuse efficiency across ALFWorld and WebShop.

Abstract: Large language model agents increasingly rely on execution traces to master complex interactive tasks. However, current paradigms are bottlenecked by shallow trajectory retrieval and flat skill summarization, fundamentally ignoring the temporal dependencies and outcome-conditioned topology of agent behavior. We introduce Trace2Tower, a transition-aware EigenTrace framework that distills raw trajectories into a robust skill hierarchy. Trace2Tower abstracts step-level interactions into canonical events, constructing a unified graph governed by semantic compatibility, transition dynamics, and outcome evidence. Through a novel contrastive spectral decomposition, it isolates stable, success-aligned behavioral modes while rigorously suppressing failure-prone shortcuts. These modes organically populate a dynamic skill tower of action templates, procedural routines, and overarching task strategies, continuously refined via verifier-guided feedback. On ALFWorld, Trace2Tower achieves 87.31% success requiring only 10.35 steps and 0.26 invalid actions; on WebShop, it reaches 50.67% exact success. Across both benchmarks, Trace2Tower significantly outperforms existing baselines in task mastery and context-efficient experience reuse.

</details>


### [91] [AI for Computational Design Science: A Responsible Human-AI Framework and Case Study on Short-Form Video Safety Surveillance](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.05270)
*Wenli Zhang,Jiaheng Xie,Zhihe Pan,Yidong Chai,Xiao Fang,Sudha Ram*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper proposes AI4CDS, a five-phase methodological framework for computational design science in which AI actively participates in problem formulation, resource construction, design search, evaluation, and knowledge abstraction, while researchers retain control over domain grounding, admissibility, verification, and scientific judgment. The framework is instantiated through ChildRiskGuard, an interpretable risk-detection artifact for short-form videos inappropriate for children, which achieves an F1 of 0.769 and outperforms general-purpose content-safety models.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: While AI is transforming both the artifacts information systems researchers design and how design research itself is conducted, existing literature provides limited guidance for computational design science (CDS) when AI actively participates across all phases of the design process—from problem formulation to knowledge abstraction.

Method: The authors develop AI4CDS, a five-phase methodological framework governed by graduated trust, reversibility, auditability, and differentiated reproducibility, where AI expands problem and design search while researchers maintain responsibility for domain grounding, admissibility, verification, and scientific judgment. The framework is instantiated through ChildRiskGuard, which addresses three technical challenges: separating generic from child-specific risk, representing distinct developmental-risk mechanisms, and embedding concept-level explanations into predictive computation.

Result: ChildRiskGuard achieves an F1 score of 0.769, substantially outperforming direct application of a general-purpose content-safety model while remaining competitive with strong benchmarks. The study documents AI interactions, rejected alternatives, corrections, and audit trails throughout the design process.

Conclusion: The primary contribution is AI4CDS as a responsible framework for AI-enabled computational design science; ChildRiskGuard provides both process and artifact evidence demonstrating how AI-expanded, researcher-governed design can generate and evaluate novel computational design knowledge.

Abstract: Artificial intelligence (AI) is transforming not only what information systems researchers design, but also how design research is conducted. Yet existing literature offers limited guidance for computational design science (CDS) when AI actively participates in problem formulation, resource construction, design search, evaluation, and knowledge abstraction. We develop AI for Computational Design Science (AI4CDS), a five-phase methodological framework in which AI expands problem and design search while researchers retain responsibility for domain grounding, admissibility, verification, and scientific judgment. Collaboration is governed by graduated trust, reversibility, auditability, and differentiated reproducibility. We instantiate AI4CDS through ChildRiskGuard, an interpretable artifact for detecting short-form videos inappropriate for children, while documenting AI interactions, rejected alternatives, corrections, and audit trails. The case translates audience-dependent safety and explanation faithfulness into three technical challenges and develops an artifact that separates generic from child-specific risk, represents distinct developmental-risk mechanisms, and makes concept-level explanations part of the predictive computation. ChildRiskGuard achieves an F1 score of 0.769, substantially outperforming direct application of a general-purpose content-safety model while remaining competitive with strong benchmarks. The primary contribution is AI4CDS as a responsible framework for AI-enabled CDS; ChildRiskGuard provides process and artifact evidence of how AI-expanded, researcher-governed design can generate and evaluate novel computational design knowledge.

</details>


### [92] [Don't Drop Dropout: Optimizing Layer Sparsity for Efficient LLM Training and Inference](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.05275)
*Mostafa Elhoushi,Alex Pretko,Nolan Dey,Bin Claire Zhang,Gavia Gray,Gurpreet Gosal,Abdulrahman Mahmoud,Shane Bergsma,Joel Hestness*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Layer dropout, largely abandoned in modern LLM pretraining, is shown to be beneficial when applied with optimal distribution, schedule, and optimizer hyperparameters—enabling lower loss at equal FLOPs, up to 25% FLOP savings, and up to 1.5x inference speedup via post-training optimizations like early exit and self-speculative decoding.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: While layer dropout was proven effective in vision and language transformers for faster training, higher accuracy, and pruning robustness, it has been largely dropped from LLM pretraining recipes due to reports of accuracy degradation. No comprehensive study has systematically quantified or mitigated this effect, leaving a gap in understanding whether and how layer dropout should be used in modern LLM training.

Method: The authors conduct over 2,400 training experiments spanning models from 271M to 8.2B parameters and datasets up to 160B tokens, systematically tuning layer distribution, time schedule, and optimizer hyperparameters to establish best practices and scaling analysis for layer dropout in LLM pretraining. All experiments were run on Cerebras CS-3 systems.

Result: With optimal hyperparameters, layer dropout achieves lower validation loss at the same training FLOPs. It enables saving up to 25% of training FLOPs for a given number of training steps while maintaining lower or similar validation loss. Layer dropout also enables post-training optimizations (early exit, intermediate-layer skipping, self-speculative decoding) yielding up to 1.5x inference speedup with negligible accuracy loss. Findings scale reliably to large-scale regimes.

Conclusion: Layer dropout should be reinstated as a standard component in state-of-the-art LLM pretraining recipes, providing both training efficiency and post-training inference benefits without sacrificing model quality when configured optimally.

Abstract: Layer dropout (a.k.a. stochastic depth) has been shown to enable faster training, higher accuracy, and robustness to zero-shot layer pruning in both language and vision transformers. However, as models and datasets have scaled, dropout - particularly layer dropout - has largely disappeared from large language models (LLMs) pre-training recipes. While some prior work has reported that dropout can degrade accuracy, no comprehensive study has quantified, let alone mitigated, this effect. In this study, we show that layer dropout should be used in state-of-the-art LLM training, establishing best practices and scaling analysis for both training and post-training benefits. Concretely, with optimal layer distribution, time schedule, and optimizer hyperparameters, we observe that at the same training FLOPs layer dropout leads to lower loss. For a given number of training steps, LLMs can achieve lower or similar validation loss while saving upto 25% of training FLOPs. Moreover, layer dropout enables significant post-training optimizations, such as early exit, intermediate-layer skipping, and self-speculative decoding, yielding up to 1.5x inference speedup with negligible accuracy loss. Across more than 2400 training experiments, spanning models from 271M to 8.2B parameters and datasets up to 160B tokens, we demonstrate that these findings extend reliably to large-scale training regimes. All pre-training experiments were run on Cerebras CS-3 systems.

</details>


### [93] [Testing Interchangeability in LLM Agent Teams](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.05279)
*Jianxin Gao,Tianyi Yu,Linna Deng,Runze Li,Zining Wang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper tests the assumption that agents in production multi-agent systems are interchangeable when filling the same role. By swapping role-matched agents between independently formed teams across eight teams per setting, the authors find that roster changes cost little in task score but increase communication overhead by 16-63%, with swapped agents in Hanabi being more expensive than inexperienced ones due to convention interference. The swap penalty correlates with how far independently formed teams drift apart, scaling with formation length.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Production multi-agent systems treat agents as interchangeable interchangeable when filling a role, but this assumption may fail because agents develop private conventions and coordination patterns with their specific teammates over time (e.g., keeping private notebooks across formation episodes). The authors set out to empirically test whether this fungibility assumption holds.

Method: Eight teams per setting are formed independently from one base model on identical tasks, with each agent maintaining a private notebook across ten formation episodes. Role-matched agents are then traded between teams, and performance is measured on held-out tasks. A placebo condition reproduces the disruption of a roster change without actually changing the agent, and three ablations vary base models, decoding temperature, and formation length to study what drives the swap penalty.

Result: Swapping agents costs little in task score but increases communication per unit of progress by 16-63%. In Hanabi, a swapped agent is more expensive than an inexperienced one, indicating interference from conventions learned with its former partner. In Collab-Overcooked, when the agenda-setting agent is replaced, most of the extra communication comes from the agent that stayed. Ablations show the swap penalty tracks how far independently formed teams drift apart: greedy decoding lowers both, while doubling team history raises both.

Conclusion: Agents are more fungible in task outcome than in coordination efficiency. Roster changes incur meaningful coordination costs—manifested as communication overhead rather than task performance degradation—and these costs grow with longer formation histories, as teams increasingly specialize their conventions with specific partners.

Abstract: Production multi-agent systems replace agents constantly, on the assumption that an agent filling a role is interchangeable with any other agent that can do the job. We test that assumption. Eight teams per setting are formed independently from one base model on the same tasks, each agent keeping a private notebook across ten formation episodes; we then trade role-matched agents between teams and measure what changes on held-out tasks. Against a placebo that reproduces the disruption of a roster change without changing who occupies the seat, a swap costs little in task score but raises the communication a team spends per unit of progress by 16 to 63 percent, and in Hanabi a swapped agent is more expensive than an inexperienced one, consistent with interference from conventions learned with its former partner. In Collab-Overcooked, when the agent that sets the agenda is replaced, most of the extra communication comes from the agent that stayed. Three ablations, over base models, decoding temperature and formation length, move the swap penalty alongside one other quantity: how far independently formed teams drift apart. Greedy decoding lowers both; doubling a team's history raises both. In these settings, agents are more fungible in task outcome than in coordination efficiency, with larger swap effects after longer formation histories.

</details>


### [94] [GUT: Quantifying and Optimizing the Reasoning Uncertainty of LLMs via Graph Complexity](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.05284)
*Shuang Liang,Xin-Yu Hu,Xiang-Jun Ou,Shao-Qun Zhang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper proposes GUT (Graph-complexity-based UncerTainty), a method to quantify and reduce reasoning uncertainty in LLMs by representing potential reasoning branches as a directed acyclic graph and using graph complexity to measure uncertainty, with reinforcement learning applied for optimization.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: LLM reasoning processes often exhibit uncertainty, producing divergent and sometimes nonsensical reasoning chains even when given identical inputs. There is a need to systematically investigate and address this uncertainty.

Method: GUT characterizes potential branches of each reasoning chain using a directed acyclic graph (DAG) to comprehensively cover all possible branches. It has two modules: GUT-Q (Quantification) which measures reasoning uncertainty by approximating reasoning space complexity with graph complexity, and GUT-O (Optimization) which reduces uncertainty by treating negative uncertainty as the reward function in reinforcement learning.

Result: Experiments conducted on four LLMs and five datasets validate the effectiveness of GUT in quantifying and reducing reasoning uncertainty.

Conclusion: The graph-based framework provides an effective approach to both measure and optimize LLM reasoning uncertainty, improving reasoning reliability.

Abstract: Recent years have witnessed great advances in the reasoning ability of Large Language Models (LLMs). However, the reasoning processes of LLMs often exhibit uncertainty, where LLMs often produce a proliferation of divergent branches at each reasoning step even when fed the same prompting inputs, and certain branches exhibit evidently incredible, even nonsensical, reasoning chains and results. In this paper, we propose the Graph-complexity-based UncerTainty (GUT) method for investigating the reasoning uncertainty of LLMs. The key idea of GUT is to characterize the potential branches of each reasoning chain with a directed acyclic graph, thereby ensuring that all potential branches are comprehensively covered within the graph space. Building upon this recognition, we further build two modules of GUT, that is, a Quantification (GUT-Q) module and an Optimization (GUT-O) module, for quantifying and reducing the reasoning uncertainty of LLMs, respectively. GUT-Q measures LLM reasoning uncertainty by approximating the reasoning space complexity with graph complexity. GUT-O implements uncertainty optimization by treating negative uncertainty as the reward function in reinforcement learning. Experimental results conducted on four LLMs and five datasets validate the effectiveness of GUT.

</details>


### [95] [Beyond Aggregate Scores: Behavioral Correctness Assumptions for Assessing Reference-Based Automatic Evaluation Methods](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.05289)
*Maria Mahbub,Ashley Rice,Michael R. Munroe,Amidu Kamara,Amir Sadovnik*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper introduces a complementary framework called "behavioral correctness assumptions" for meta-evaluating reference-based automatic evaluation methods. Instead of only measuring aggregate agreement with human judgments, the framework defines correctness-preserving and correctness-altering assumptions operationalized through controlled response transformations. Evaluating diverse evaluators (lexical, character-level, semantic, LLM-based, hybrid) reveals that no evaluator satisfies all assumptions, and evaluators with similar aggregate performance can exhibit very different behavioral profiles, providing diagnostic insights obscured by conventional meta-evaluation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing meta-evaluation of automatic evaluation methods primarily measures correlation/agreement with human judgments or benchmark labels, which provides limited insight into how evaluators actually behave under controlled conditions. There is a need for a framework that can diagnose evaluator behavior in a more granular, assumption-testable manner.

Method: The paper (1) defines a taxonomy of correctness-preserving and correctness-altering behavioral correctness assumptions for reference-based evaluators; (2) operationalizes these assumptions using controlled response transformations that encode expected scoring behaviors; (3) applies this framework to evaluate diverse evaluator types including lexical, character-level, semantic, LLM-based, and hybrid approaches; (4) analyzes assumption-level behavior, stability, sensitivity, repeat-run variability, configuration sensitivity, and reproducibility.

Result: Experiments reveal distinct behavioral trade-offs across evaluation paradigms. No evaluated evaluator satisfies all proposed correctness assumptions. Crucially, evaluators with similar aggregate performance can exhibit substantially different behavioral profiles, and evaluator differences emerge that are obscured by conventional aggregate meta-evaluation metrics.

Conclusion: Behavioral correctness assumptions provide complementary, diagnostic information about automatic evaluators that is not captured by conventional aggregate meta-evaluation, and should be adopted as a framework for more nuanced analysis of reference-based evaluation methods.

Abstract: Automated reference-based evaluation methods play a critical role in assessing natural language generation systems. Existing meta-evaluation primarily measures agreement with human judgments or benchmark labels, providing limited insight into evaluator behavior under controlled conditions. We introduce behavioral correctness assumptions, a complementary framework for evaluating reference-based automatic evaluation methods. We define a taxonomy of correctness-preserving and correctness-altering assumptions and operationalize them through controlled response transformations that specify expected scoring behaviors. We evaluate diverse lexical, character-level, semantic, LLM-based, and hybrid evaluators and analyze their assumption-level behavior, stability, sensitivity, repeat-run variability, configuration sensitivity, and reproducibility. Our experiments reveal distinct behavioral trade-offs across evaluation paradigms: no evaluator satisfies all proposed correctness assumptions, and evaluators with similar aggregate performance can exhibit substantially different behavioral profiles. These findings demonstrate that behavioral correctness assumptions provide diagnostic information obscured by conventional aggregate meta-evaluation.

</details>


### [96] [RISE: Recursive Improvement via Self-Extrapolating Policy Distillation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.05295)
*Yang Li,Semih Yavuz,Shafiq Joty*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: RISE is a method that constructs a synthetic teacher directly from a model's own RLVR training trajectory via parameter/logit-space extrapolation between the current checkpoint and a trailing anchor, converting sparse outcome-level rewards into dense token-level supervision without external models or privileged conditioning. It outperforms RLVR-only and on-policy self-distillation across math, STEM, code, and agentic tasks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: On-policy distillation (OPD) is effective for post-training but bottlenecked by teacher quality: external teachers cause distribution mismatch, while self-distillation with privileged conditioning is limited by in-context learning capacity. A better source of dense supervision is needed.

Method: RISE constructs a synthetic teacher by extrapolating the displacement between the current checkpoint and a trailing anchor during RLVR training, either in parameter space or output logit space. This converts sparse outcome-induced parameter updates into dense token-level distillation targets without external models or privileged conditioning. RISE runs RLVR and OPD in a complementary loop where outcome rewards ground extrapolation and the extrapolated teacher refines token-level decisions, with the teacher refreshed each iteration to enable recursive improvement.

Result: Experiments across mathematical reasoning, multi-domain STEM, code generation, and multi-turn agentic tasks show RISE consistently outperforms RLVR-only training and on-policy self-distillation baselines in all settings.

Conclusion: RISE demonstrates that leveraging a model's own training trajectory to construct extrapolated synthetic teachers enables recursive, iterative distillation that surpasses both RLVR-only training and existing on-policy self-distillation approaches.

Abstract: On-policy distillation (OPD) provides dense, per-token supervision for language model post-training, but its effectiveness is bottlenecked by teacher quality: external teachers suffer from distribution mismatch, while self-distillation with privileged conditioning is limited by in-context learning capacity. We propose \textbf{RISE} (\textbf{R}ecursive \textbf{I}mprovement via \textbf{S}elf-\textbf{E}xtrapolating Policy Distillation), which constructs a synthetic teacher directly from the model's own RLVR training trajectory. By extrapolating the displacement between the current checkpoint and a trailing anchor---in parameter space or output logit space---RISE converts a sparse outcome-induced parameter update into a dense token-level target, without any external model or privileged conditioning. RISE combines RLVR and OPD in a complementary loop: outcome rewards ground the extrapolation toward correct reasoning, while the extrapolated teacher refines token-level decisions. Moreover, since the teacher is refreshed every iteration as the student improves, distillation becomes a recursive improvement mechanism rather than a one-shot compression step. Experiments spanning mathematical reasoning, multi-domain STEM, code generation, and multi-turn agentic tasks show that RISE outperforms RLVR-only training and on-policy self-distillation across all settings.

</details>


### [97] [Large Language Models for HVAC Operations in Building Energy Systems: A Critical Review of Methods, Applications, and Deployment Readiness](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.05314)
*Alexander Neubauer,Tianzhen Hong,Han Li,Mengbo Yu,Amin Darbandi,Yannick Fürst,Martin Kriegel*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: A systematic review of 66 peer-reviewed studies (2023–March 2026) on LLMs for HVAC operations finds the field concentrated in building energy modelling (32 papers), with only 4 pilot-level studies, zero sustained deployments, and no "ready-now" solutions. LLMs are best suited as semantic and workflow layers (e.g., point-name normalisation, document-grounded operator support) rather than autonomous controllers; conventional ML/MPC/RL/ontology methods remain superior for high-frequency control and numerical forecasting.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Building automation systems generate rich sensor data but remain insight-poor due to heterogeneous point naming, missing metadata, and fragmented documentation, which obstruct operational use. The authors conduct a systematic review to understand the current state and realistic deployment readiness of LLMs for HVAC operations.

Method: A systematic review that analyses and codes 66 peer-reviewed studies on LLMs for HVAC operations published between 2023 and March 2026. Studies are classified across five application families and three LLM method families, and assessed along three dimensions: evidence realism, deployment readiness, and the responsibility boundary between LLM and physical HVAC decisions.

Result: The corpus is concentrated in building energy modelling (BEM, 32/66 papers); load forecasting is too sparse for subfield conclusions. Only 4 studies reach pilot-level evidence; none report sustained operational deployment. No study was classified as ready-now; 3 were near-term and 63 research-only. Bounded human-in-the-loop uses (point-name normalisation, document-grounded operator support, BEM workflow assistance, advisory interfaces) merit near-term trials, while autonomous agentic operation and unvalidated occupant proxies remain research-stage.

Conclusion: Current evidence supports LLMs primarily as semantic and workflow layers rather than autonomous HVAC controllers. Future work should prioritise field-validated benchmarks, orchestration evaluation under operational constraints, and LLM-MPC/RL architectures with bounded latency and verifiable safety properties.

Abstract: Building automation systems generate rich sensor data yet remain insight-poor because heterogeneous point naming, missing metadata, and fragmented documentation obstruct their operational use. This systematic review analyses and codes 66 peer-reviewed studies on large language models (LLMs) for HVAC operations published between 2023 and March 2026. Each study is classified across five application families and three LLM method families and assessed for evidence realism, deployment readiness, and the responsibility boundary between the LLM and physical HVAC decisions. The corpus is concentrated in building energy modelling (BEM, 32 of 66 papers), while load forecasting remains too sparse for subfield-level conclusions. Only four studies reach pilot-level evidence, and none reports sustained operational deployment. No study was classified as ready-now for industry adoption; three were near-term and 63 research-only. Nevertheless, several bounded, human-in-the-loop uses merit near-term trials, including point-name normalisation, document-grounded operator support, BEM workflow assistance, and advisory interfaces around physics-based controllers. Conventional machine learning (ML), model predictive control (MPC), reinforcement learning (RL) and ontology-based tools remain more adopted for high-frequency control, short-horizon numerical forecasting, and well-posed ontology mapping, while autonomous agentic operation and unvalidated occupant proxies remain research-stage. Current evidence therefore supports LLMs primarily as semantic and workflow layers rather than autonomous HVAC controllers. Future work should prioritise field-validated benchmarks, orchestration evaluation under operational constraints, and LLM-MPC/RL architectures with bounded latency and verifiable safety properties.

</details>


### [98] [LLM-Driven Algorithm Design for Quantum Circuit Synthesis based on Binary Decision Diagrams](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.05327)
*Yoonju Sim,Federico Berto,Chuanbo Hua,Jinkyoo Park,Changhyun Kwon*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: QuantumEvo is an evolutionary framework that uses LLMs as heuristic generators to search for QCC-aware BDD variable ordering heuristics for reversible circuit synthesis. The discovered heuristic HGA-QE modifies the sifting step in a genetic algorithm to better align with quantum circuit cost, achieving 70.9% tie-or-win rate and 13.5% strict wins against per-function best baselines.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing BDD variable ordering heuristics optimize for BDD size as a proxy for circuit cost, but BDD size is an imperfect surrogate for the actual quantum circuit cost (QCC). Since QCC is the ultimate metric of interest in reversible circuit synthesis, directly optimizing for it during variable ordering is needed.

Method: QuantumEvo is an evolutionary framework where an LLM iteratively generates candidate ordering heuristics, initialized from multiple heuristic families. Rather than predicting orderings directly, candidates manipulate variable orderings using standard BDD operations and are evaluated/selected by downstream quantum circuit cost (QCC). The discovered heuristic HGA-QE modifies the sifting step inside a genetic algorithm to align ordering search with QCC.

Result: Across benchmarks, HGA-QE achieves a 70.9% tie-or-win rate against per-function best baselines and is strictly best on 13.5% of functions. The best generalization is observed on benchmark suites drawn from sources different from the heuristic discovery data, showing robust out-of-distribution performance.

Conclusion: QCC-aware BDD variable ordering via LLM-driven evolutionary heuristic search yields broadly competitive quantum circuit costs. The approach demonstrates that optimizing directly for quantum cost rather than BDD size is viable, with strong generalization to unseen benchmark distributions.

Abstract: Quantum circuits are central to implementing quantum algorithms on quantum devices, where quantum gates must be reversible. Many quantum algorithms rely on Boolean functions, which must therefore be implemented reversibly within quantum circuits. Reversible circuit synthesis provides a way to translate such Boolean functions into reversible circuits. Binary decision diagrams (BDDs) offer a scalable approach to this task, but the resulting BDDs and circuits depend heavily on variable ordering. Existing ordering heuristics commonly minimize BDD size because it is closely tied to the circuit size. However, BDD size is an imperfect proxy for the quantum cost of the synthesized circuit (QCC). We propose \texttt{QuantumEvo}, an evolutionary framework that uses an LLM as a heuristic generator for QCC-aware BDD variable ordering. Instead of predicting orderings directly, \texttt{QuantumEvo} searches over ordering heuristics initialized from multiple heuristic families. Candidate heuristics directly manipulate variable orderings using standard BDD operations and are selected by downstream QCC. The discovered heuristic, HGA-QE, modifies the sifting step inside a genetic algorithm so that the procedure is better aligned with QCC. Across the benchmark set, HGA-QE achieves a 70.9\% tie-or-win rate against the per-function best baseline and is strictly best on 13.5\% of the functions. The results demonstrate broadly competitive QCC performance, with HGA-QE showing a clearer relative advantage in strict wins on the two benchmark suites drawn from sources different from the data used for heuristic discovery.

</details>


### [99] [Technical Manual for a Toolkit for Measuring Contextual Individuation in Transformer Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.05333)
*José Luciano Verçosa Marques,Frederico Jorge Heitmann,Daniel Omar Perez,Marcelo Vinicius de Paula,Tárcio André dos Santos Barros*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper documents an open-source toolkit and methodological pipeline for testing whether transformer language models exhibit context-dependent word sense differentiation. It introduces "bridge forms"—single written words that recur unchanged across multiple subject domains with different senses—and describes a complete analysis pipeline from bridge-form specification through corpus acquisition, representation extraction, and separation measurement. The paper serves as a methodological and implementation reference only, presenting design choices alongside the failure modes they avoid, without reporting empirical results.




<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The paper addresses a gap between the common belief that transformer language models individuate word occurrences by context in higher layers, despite assigning a single context-independent embedding vector at the input layer, and the lack of a clean, controlled construct to test this belief empirically. There is a need for a rigorous methodological framework that isolates word sense variation while holding word form constant.



Method: The pipeline consists of several stages: (1) declarative specification of bridge forms and their source domains; (2) corpus acquisition from Wikipedia; (3) occurrence localization; (4) layer-wise representation extraction; (5) a domain-pairwise silhouette measurement of separation in representation space (chosen to avoid the multi-group bias of the standard silhouette coefficient); and (6) a paired visualization protocol. Each design decision is explicitly linked to a methodological failure mode it avoids, including sense contamination from broad category labels, subword-tokenization misalignment, and axis-comparability artifacts in dimensionality-reduced plots.



Result: This manuscript reports no empirical outcomes. Its contribution is the toolkit itself, the methodological justifications for each pipeline stage, and the archived corpora and source code under a persistent identifier, intended to be cited as an instrument by future studies that produce empirical results.



Conclusion: The paper concludes by positioning the toolkit as an open, citable methodological reference and instrument for subsequent empirical investigations into how transformer models represent word sense variation across contexts. It emphasizes that design choices were driven by specific known failure modes in measuring sense separation, and that the toolkit is intended for external use in producing and interpreting empirical findings.



Abstract: A transformer language model assigns a single, context-independent vector to a word type at its embedding layer, yet is widely believed to individuate that word's occurrences by context in its later layers. Testing this belief cleanly requires a construct that holds the word form fixed while its context and intended sense vary in a controlled, labeled way. This manual documents an open toolkit built around such a construct, which we call a bridge form: a single written word that recurs, unchanged, across two or more subject domains with a different sense in each. We describe, and justify, every stage of the pipeline: the declarative specification of bridge forms and their source domains, corpus acquisition from Wikipedia, occurrence localization, layer-wise representation extraction, a domain-pairwise silhouette measurement of separation in the model's representation space, and a paired visualization protocol. Each design choice is presented together with the methodological failure mode it is meant to avoid (sense contamination from overly broad category labels, the multi-group bias of the silhouette coefficient, subword-tokenization misalignment, and axis-comparability artifacts in dimensionality-reduced plots, among others). This manuscript is a methodological and implementation reference: it does not report or interpret empirical outcomes of running the toolkit on any particular model or bridge-form set. The toolkit, its full source, and the corpora used to exercise it are archived separately (Section 9) under a persistent identifier, and are intended to be cited as an instrument by studies that use it to produce and interpret empirical results.

</details>


### [100] [Does Your Agent's Memory Survive a Model Upgrade? A Controlled Study of Memory Portability](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.05339)
*Ankit Goyal,Jaideep Ray*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper compares four memory storage formats—long-context raw text (LC-RAW), chunked retrieval-augmented generation (RAG), compressed natural-language notes (NOTES), and fixed-schema knowledge graphs (KG-fixed)—under model upgrades to assess memory migration reliability. Using 48 synthetic histories and sub-10B open-weight models, it finds that fixed-schema KGs transfer reliably, compressed NOTES are highly model-coupled, partial embedding migrations in RAG lose most retrieval gains, and store-only repair of NOTES fails while retaining source histories enables recovery.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Model upgrades are routine but memory migrations are not—agents can forget even with the same memory store when models change. This paper investigates how different memory representations hold up across model swaps, since new models may misinterpret old notes, mixed embedding versions may break retrieval, and repair may fail without original evidence.

Method: The study uses 48 synthetic histories with randomized answer codes and exact scoring across four memory formats: LC-RAW (untouched long-context), RAG (chunked with retrieval), NOTES (model-compressed natural language), and KG-fixed (fixed-schema knowledge graph). Two open-weight models under 10 billion parameters are swapped in both migration directions.

Result: KG-fixed transfers reliably with accuracy shifting only +0.0004 ± 0.0020 after writer swap. NOTES shows high model coupling with asymmetric accuracy shifts of +9.91 or −13.28 percentage points depending on migration direction. RAG partial embedding migration (50/50 mixed index) captures only 4.96-point gain vs. 11.90-point full re-embedding gain. Diagnostic decomposition shows 80% (0.467 ± 0.014) of the NOTES deficit stems from initial construction information loss, while retrieval failures drive 81% (0.364 ± 0.012) of RAG deficits. Store-only NOTES repair fails to reach 90% recovery in all 48 cases, while retaining raw source history enables recovery in 34/48 cases.

Conclusion: The findings underscore the necessity of direction-specific migration testing, strict embedding space isolation, and retaining source histories for effective memory repair. Fixed-schema structures are the most robust memory format across model upgrades, while compressed representations tie memory tightly to the generating model.

Abstract: Model upgrades are routine; memory migrations are not. An agent can keep the same memory store and still forget: a new model may interpret old notes differently, mixed embedding versions may break retrieval, and repair may fail without the original evidence. We compare memory as the same history is preserved verbatim for long-context reading (LC-RAW), divided into chunks for retrieval-augmented generation (RAG), compressed by a model into natural-language notes (NOTES), or normalized into a fixed-schema knowledge graph (KG-fixed). The study uses 48 synthetic histories with randomized answer codes, exact scoring, and two open-weight models with sub 10 billion parameters.
  Our measurements show that fixed-schema structures transfer reliably, with KG-fixed accuracy changing by only $+0.0004 \pm 0.0020$ following a writer swap. Conversely, compressed NOTES exhibit high model coupling, with accuracy shifting asymmetrically by $+9.91$ or $-13.28$ percentage points depending on the specific migration direction. In RAG systems, partial embedding migrations using a 50/50 mixed index capture only a 4.96-point accuracy improvement, forfeiting the majority of the 11.90-point gain achieved through full re-embedding. Diagnostic decomposition attributes 80% ($0.467 \pm 0.014$) of the NOTES accuracy deficit to information lost during initial construction, whereas retrieval failures drive 81% ($0.364 \pm 0.012$) of the RAG deficit. Finally, store-only repair of NOTES fails to reach a 90% performance recovery target in all 48 test cases, whereas retaining the raw source history enables successful recovery in 34 of 48 cases for one tested direction. These findings highlight the necessity of direction-specific migration testing, strict embedding space isolation, and the retention of source histories for memory repair.

</details>


### [101] [Who Should Grade My Work? Student Perspectives on Transparent AI-Assisted Writing Assessment in Higher Education](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.05346)
*Rayed AlGhamdi*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This qualitative study in an undergraduate technical communication course at a Saudi public university examined how 13 male computing students interpret feedback and scores when explicitly informed that ChatGPT generated them. Thematic analysis of student reflections revealed four themes: perceived usefulness of feedback, awareness of AI's contextual/pedagogical limitations, conditional trust (distinguishing feedback utility from evaluative authority), and reflection on the human instructor's institutional role. Students found GenAI feedback useful for surface-level revision but maintained the human instructor as the proper authority over grading decisions.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: While prior research has examined whether GenAI-generated feedback improves writing and how students perceive its usefulness, little is known about how students interpret AI-mediated evaluation when they are explicitly told that an AI system, not a human instructor, produced both the feedback and the score.

Method: A qualitative pedagogical inquiry was conducted in an undergraduate technical communication course for computing students at a Saudi public university. Thirteen male undergraduate computing students completed an in-class handwritten writing task; scanned submissions were evaluated by ChatGPT using a rubric-based prompt aligned with task objectives. Students were explicitly informed that ChatGPT generated the score and feedback, then invited to write reflective responses. These reflections were analyzed using inductive thematic analysis.

Result: Four themes emerged: (1) perceived usefulness of feedback; (2) awareness of AI's contextual and pedagogical limitations; (3) conditional trust that separates feedback utility from evaluative authority; and (4) reflection on the institutional and pedagogical role of the human instructor. Participants accepted GenAI feedback as useful for surface-level revision but consistently positioned the human instructor as the appropriate authority over grading decisions.

Conclusion: The study identifies a conceptual distinction between feedback utility and evaluative authority—two judgments students treat as analytically separate rather than opposite ends of a single approval scale—suggesting implications for how AI-mediated assessment should be designed and communicated in higher education contexts.

Abstract: The integration of GenAI tools into higher education assessment raises important questions about how students understand, interpret, and respond to AI-mediated evaluation. As instructors increasingly explore AI tools for providing feedback, prior research has examined whether GenAI-generated feedback improves writing performance and how students perceive its usefulness; comparatively little is known, however, about how students interpret such evaluation when they are explicitly informed that an AI system, rather than a human instructor, produced the feedback and the score. This study reports findings from a qualitative pedagogical inquiry conducted in an undergraduate technical communication course for computing students at a Saudi public university. Thirteen male undergraduate computing students completed an in-class handwritten writing task; the scanned submissions were evaluated by ChatGPT using a rubric-based prompt aligned with the task objectives. Students were then explicitly informed that ChatGPT had generated the score and feedback and were invited to reflect on the evaluation in writing. Inductive thematic analysis of these reflections identified four themes: perceived usefulness of feedback; awareness of AI's contextual and pedagogical limitations; conditional trust, distinguishing feedback utility from evaluative authority; and reflection on the institutional and pedagogical role of the human instructor. Participants accepted GenAI feedback as useful for surface-level revision but consistently positioned the human instructor as the appropriate authority over grading decisions. The study identifies this as a distinction between feedback utility and evaluative authority, two judgments that students treat as analytically separate rather than as opposite ends of a single approval scale...

</details>


### [102] [CUA-Universe: A Scalable and Dynamic Environment for Hybrid GUI+CLI Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.05374)
*Haoting Shi,Wenhao Wang,Weicheng Fang,Yaozhong Liang,Tian Jin,Pengxiang Zhao,Guangyi Liu,Siheng Chen,Yanfeng Wang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: CUA-Universe is a scalable environment-to-data pipeline that converts real desktop applications into hybrid GUI+CLI environments, enabling computer-use agents to coordinate both visual inspection and command-line operations. It introduces three components: App-Forge (adapts apps into reproducible VMs with command-line surfaces across 16 applications), Task-Weave (synthesizes diverse hybrid tasks with controllable difficulty), and Path-Steer (guides rollouts along efficient hybrid paths for post-training). A 9B model trained on this data achieves significant improvements in both success rate and efficiency across CUA-Verse, OSWorld, and OSWorld-MCP benchmarks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing computer-use agents operate almost exclusively through GUI, producing inefficient trajectories. Real-world computer work is inherently hybrid—requiring both visual-state inspection and high-throughput command-line operations over shared application state. However, scalable hybrid environments are scarce because supporting both GUI and CLI over real applications requires substantial manual engineering, and existing agents fail to use the two interfaces complementarily.

Method: CUA-Universe consists of three components: (1) App-Forge converts real applications into reproducible VMs and command-line surfaces it discovers, wraps, or generates, achieving scalability to 16 applications; (2) Task-Weave synthesizes diverse hybrid tasks of controllable difficulty from reusable operations over seed files; (3) Path-Steer steers rollouts along efficient hybrid paths and harvests verified trajectories for post-training.

Result: A 9B model trained on CUA-Universe data improves both success and efficiency: +39.3 pts Score with -37% steps and -60% tokens on CUA-Verse; +16.8 pts SR with -57% steps and -44% tokens on OSWorld; +7.84 pts Score with -27% steps and -30% tokens on OSWorld-MCP. The training shifts agent behavior from inefficient GUI interaction and brittle CLI scripting toward effective GUI+CLI orchestration.

Conclusion: CUA-Universe provides a scalable path toward more capable and efficient computer-use agents by enabling effective hybrid GUI+CLI orchestration, addressing the critical gap between current GUI-dominant agents and the reality of hybrid computer work.

Abstract: Computer-use agents have advanced on benchmarks like OSWorld and AndroidWorld, but still act mostly through the GUI, often producing inefficient trajectories. Real-world computer work is hybrid, combining visual-state inspection with precise, high-throughput command-line operations, so capable agents must coordinate both modalities over shared application state. Yet scalable hybrid environments remain scarce because supporting both GUI and CLI over real applications typically requires substantial manual engineering for each application. Existing agents also struggle to use the two interfaces complementarily: CLI-native agents lack visual perception for tasks involving interface state or layout, while GUI-native agents are inefficient for operations better executed through commands. We introduce CUA-Universe, a scalable environment-to-data pipeline that turns real desktop software into hybrid GUI+CLI environments. App-Forge adapts applications into reproducible VMs and command-line surfaces it discovers, wraps, or generates, scaling to 16 applications; Task-Weave synthesizes diverse hybrid tasks of controllable difficulty from reusable operations over seed files; and Path-Steer steers rollouts along efficient hybrid paths and harvests verified trajectories for post-training. Training on this data shifts behavior from inefficient GUI interaction and brittle CLI scripting toward effective GUI+CLI orchestration. Our 9B model improves both success and efficiency on CUA-Verse (Score +39.3 pts; -37% steps, -60% tokens), OSWorld (SR +16.8 pts; -57% steps, -44% tokens), and OSWorld-MCP (Score +7.84 pts; -27% steps, -30% tokens). CUA-Universe provides a scalable path toward more capable and efficient computer-use agents.

</details>


### [103] [Molecular Déjà Vu: Digit-Level Retrieval of Published Values in Frontier Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.05381)
*Matthias Busch,Marius Tacke,Sviatlana V. Lamaka,Mikhail L. Zheludkevich,Christian J. Cyron,Roland C. Aydin,Christian Feiler*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper audits 22 frontier LLMs across 12 molecular regression benchmarks to quantify verbatim retrieval (memorization) of published property values versus genuine prediction. It finds retrieval is widespread but benchmark-specific, increases with higher reasoning levels, and that suppressing retrieval makes model errors converge, suggesting memorization—not predictive skill—drives some performance differences.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: LLM accuracy on molecular property benchmarks cannot distinguish genuine prediction from retrieval of memorized published values, so the authors systematically audit frontier models to characterize the prevalence and depth of verbatim memorization.

Method: The authors audit 22 frontier LLMs on 12 regression benchmarks for verbatim retrieval, test retrieval behavior at two reasoning levels, and attempt to interrupt retrieval via transformed SMILES strings combined with original labels while measuring changes in prediction errors across models.

Result: Verbatim retrieval is widespread but benchmark-specific: on five datasets over 50% of LLMs show retrieval. Higher reasoning levels increase retrieval flagging by 89%. Strongest models sometimes still recognize transformed SMILES with original labels. Suppressing retrieval brings models' prediction errors closer together, while varied verbatim retrieval use spreads them apart.

Conclusion: The general predictive capability of an LLM is not determined solely by the amount of memorized values; the paper provides an overview of the depth and breadth of verbatim retrieval in molecular regression benchmarks using LLMs.

Abstract: Large language models (LLMs) are increasingly evaluated on molecular property benchmarks, but accuracy cannot distinguish a model that predicts a property from one that retrieves a published number. We audit 22 frontier models on 12 regression benchmarks for verbatim retrieval and find that it is widespread but relatively benchmark-specific: on five datasets more than $50\%$ of the LLMs show verbatim retrieval, while on the remaining datasets it appears only in isolated cells. We run our experiments at two reasoning levels and find that reasoning changes retrieval. The same experiments, on the same molecules and with the same prompt, are flagged $89\%$ more often at the higher reasoning level than at the lowest one. Finally, we test a way to interrupt retrieval in our most contaminated cases, and find that the strongest models in some cases still recognise a combination of transformed SMILES strings and original labels. Furthermore, suppressing retrieval moves the prediction errors of the different models closer together in relative terms, while their differing use of verbatim retrieval spreads them apart. This indicates that the general predictive capability of an LLM is not determined solely by the amount of memorised values. This work provides an overview of the amount and depth of verbatim retrieval in molecular regression benchmarks using LLMs.

</details>


### [104] [Necessary or Sufficient? Evaluating LLM Explanations With Behavioural Evidence](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.05385)
*Urja Pawar,Rajitha Ramanayake,Nabeel Kemal,Ashwin Kandath,Owen O'Neill,Guillaume Bourgeon,Houssem Chatbri*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper investigates whether the named factors (explanations) provided by LLM decision components in agent workflows align with their actual observable decision behavior. Using necessity (changing a factor changes the output) and sufficiency (retaining a factor while removing others preserves the output) interpretations, the authors evaluate eight models from Claude, GPT, and Gemini families across two synthetic use cases: advisor recommendation and prompt monitoring. Results show mean Spearman correlations of only 0.349/0.354 (necessity/sufficiency) for advisor recommendation and 0.431/0.580 for prompt monitoring, with uncited factors frequently outperforming cited ones. The cited top-three factors contain useful but not reliably accurate information about which factors truly drive model outputs.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: LLM decision components in agent workflows produce explanations alongside action-relevant recommendations. Operators rely on these named factors to monitor systems, diagnose errors, or decide on escalation — a practice that assumes explanations faithfully reflect the component's observable decision behavior. The paper tests whether this assumption holds.

Method: The authors define two interpretations of named factors: necessity (changing a factor would change the output) and sufficiency (retaining a factor while removing other changeable information preserves the output). They evaluate these across two synthetic use cases — advisor recommendation and prompt harmfulness/risk judging — using controlled black-box interventions. For each factor, necessity scores measure how often changing it changes the output, and sufficiency scores measure how often retaining it preserves the output. Eight models from Claude, GPT, and Gemini families are tested, comparing the models' cited factor rankings against computed necessity/sufficiency scores via Spearman correlations.

Result: Mean Spearman correlations between cited rankings and necessity/sufficiency scores are 0.349/0.354 for advisor recommendation and 0.431/0.580 for prompt monitoring. An uncited factor scored above the lowest-scoring cited factor in 57.6% (necessity) and 58.1% (sufficiency) of advisor responses, and 25.8% (necessity) and 8.9% (sufficiency) for prompt monitoring. The cited top-three factors contain useful information but do not reliably identify the strongest measured influence factors.

Conclusion: LLM-generated explanations (cited top-three factors) are only weakly to moderately aligned with actual decision behavior under both necessity and sufficiency interpretations. The framework provides a black-box reliability check for explanations used in agent oversight, but remains scoped to individual LLM decisions rather than broader workflow reliability.

Abstract: LLM decision components that can operate within agent workflows often produce action-relevant recommendations or judgements together with explanations. Operators may use the named factors to monitor a system, diagnose errors, or decide when to escalate an output. Such use assumes that the explanations agree with the component's observable decision behaviour. We test two interpretations of the named factors: necessity, meaning that changing a factor would change the output, and sufficiency, meaning that retaining it while removing other changeable information would preserve the output. We evaluate these interpretations in two synthetic use cases: recommending advisors to clients and judging prompts for harmfulness or risk. Models return an output and the top three factors that most influenced it. Controlled black-box interventions estimate a necessity score for each factor by measuring how often changing it changes the output, and a sufficiency score by measuring how often retaining it preserves the output. Across eight models from the Claude, GPT, and Gemini families, the mean Spearman correlations between the cited ranking and the necessity and sufficiency scores are 0.349 and 0.354 for advisor recommendation, and 0.431 and 0.580 for prompt monitoring. Furthermore, an uncited factor scores above the lowest-scoring cited factor in 57.6% of advisor responses under necessity and 58.1% under sufficiency; the corresponding prompt-monitoring rates are 25.8% and 8.9%. The cited top three contain useful information but do not reliably identify the three factors with the strongest measured influence under necessity or sufficiency. The framework provides a black-box reliability check for explanations used in agent oversight while remaining scoped to individual LLM decisions.

</details>


### [105] [Multi-Step Tool-Calling over Korean Open Public APIs: A Benchmark and a Data-Synthesis Recipe](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.05395)
*Dain Kim,Eungi Cho,Kyumin Kim,Shinyeong Noh,Kyuseong Lim*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper introduces KOPA-Bench, a benchmark of 145 real-world Korean public API tasks for evaluating open-source LLM agents in multi-step tool-calling under data-sovereignty constraints, and EDGE (Execution-grounded Dynamic Graph), a data synthesis method that builds and verifies tool-interconnection graphs via live API execution to generate executable multi-step trajectories. A 9B model fine-tuned via GRPO on EDGE-synthesized data nearly matches an untuned 27B model from the same family, also improving on the BFCL benchmark.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Public institutions subject to data-sovereignty regulations must deploy open-source, on-premise LLM agents that chain multiple tool-calls across live government APIs, but open-source models underperform in this multi-step setting and no existing benchmark measures this gap.

Method: The authors introduce KOPA-Bench with 145 real-world tasks, and propose EDGE, which constructs a graph representing how each tool's output can feed another's input, filters edges by actually executing calls against live APIs to keep only successful links, and traverses these verified links to synthesize executable multi-step trajectories. Models are fine-tuned via GRPO on the resulting dataset.

Result: A 9B model fine-tuned with GRPO on EDGE-synthesized data nearly matches the performance of an untuned 27B model from the same family, with substantial improvements on both KOPA-Bench and the BFCL benchmark.

Conclusion: Execution-grounded synthetic data generation can effectively close the gap between smaller open-source models and larger untuned counterparts in multi-step tool-calling for public API settings, suggesting that verified synthetic trajectories are a viable path toward compliant, on-premise deployment.

Abstract: Data-sovereignty regulations increasingly require public institutions to deploy open-source, on-premise LLM agents that chain multiple tool-calls across live government APIs. However, open-source models consistently underperform in this multi-step setting, and no existing benchmark measures the gap. We introduce the Korean Open Public API Benchmark (KOPA-Bench), comprising 145 real-world tasks. To close this gap, we present EDGE, an Execution-grounded Dynamic Graph for tool-calling data synthEsis driven by live execution. EDGE builds a graph of how each tool's output can feed another's input, keeps only the links that succeed when actually called against the live APIs, and traverses these verified links to synthesize executable multi-step trajectories. Fine-tuned via GRPO on the resulting dataset, our 9B model nearly matches the untuned 27B model from the same family, improving substantially not only on KOPA-Bench but also on the BFCL benchmark.

</details>


### [106] [A Deep Generative Model for Synthesizing Labeled Wireless Signals](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.05396)
*Yuxiao Li,Keke Hu,Santiago Mazuelas,Yuan Shen*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper proposes IIns-GAN, a deep learning-based method using Inter-Instance Generative Adversarial Networks to generate realistic labeled wireless signals for sensing tasks, addressing the high costs and limitations of real-world data collection and traditional simulation approaches.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Real-world labeled wireless signal datasets are costly and difficult to obtain due to significant measurement and labeling overhead, while traditional signal synthesis methods rely on environmental models that require extensive hyper-parameter tuning and produce signals with inadequate realism for model training.

Method: IIns-GAN, a novel deep learning-based Generative Adversarial Network architecture designed to generate realistic labeled wireless signals that are adaptive across different environment scenarios, producing signals suitable for tasks such as distance estimation and environment identification.

Result: Experiments on public Ultra-Wideband (UWB) datasets show that IIns-GAN generated signals mirror the physical characteristics of real-world measurements and significantly improve model training performance across diverse wireless sensing tasks.

Conclusion: IIns-GAN effectively addresses the data scarcity problem in wireless sensing by generating physically realistic, labeled signals that enhance downstream task performance, offering a viable alternative to costly real-world data collection.

Abstract: Wireless signals with position-related labels are pivotal for both performance evaluation and model training in the realm of wireless sensing. However, acquiring real-world datasets is often challenged by significant measurement and labeling costs. Traditional methods for synthesizing labeled wireless signals typically rely on environmental models, leading to extensive hyper-parameter tuning and inadequate realism for comprehensive model training purposes. To address these limitations, we introduce a novel deep learning (DL)-based method, namely Inter-Instance Generative Adversarial Networks (IIns-GAN), to generate realistic labeled wireless signals. The generated signals are particularly adaptive to different environment scenarios and well-suited for various model training tasks, including distance estimation and environment identification. We have conducted extensive experiments on public Ultra-Wideband (UWB) datasets to evaluate the realism and utility of the generated signals. The results demonstrate that the signals generated by IIns-GAN mirror the physical characteristics of real-world measurements, and significantly contribute to the improvement of model training in diverse wireless sensing tasks.

</details>


<div id='cs.RO'></div>

# cs.RO [[Back]](#toc)

### [107] [FailureSpot: Label-Efficient Timestamp-Level Failure Detection for Vision-Language-Action Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.04277)
*Jie Ma,Zongxi Liu,Yi Zhu*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: This paper proposes a data-efficient framework for timestamp-level VLA failure detection that uses unlabeled action chunks to construct weak supervision signals (capturing abnormal patterns like inconsistent chunks, frozen actions, and random motions), then employs active learning to select only the most uncertain trajectories for timestamp-level annotation and fine-tuning.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing proactive failure detection methods for VLA policies are supervised with trajectory-level labels, causing normal pre-failure behavior in unsuccessful trajectories to be mislabeled as failures. This supervision mismatch introduces label noise and limits both trajectory-level detection accuracy and precise timestamp-level failure localization. Additionally, dense timestamp-level annotation is expensive.
