Method: The framework first leverages unlabeled VLA action chunks to construct action-derived weak supervision signals that capture abnormal patterns: inconsistent consecutive chunks, frozen or idle actions, and aggressive random motions. Active learning is then used to select only the most uncertain trajectories for timestamp-level annotation, and the detector is fine-tuned with these informative labels.

Result: Experiments across multiple VLA policies demonstrate that the proposed method improves both timestamp-level and trajectory-level failure detection performance.

Conclusion: A data-efficient active-learning-based framework with action-derived weak supervision effectively addresses timestamp-level VLA failure detection while mitigating the annotation cost and supervision mismatch problems of prior proactive methods.

Abstract: Vision-language-action (VLA) policies have shown strong potential for general-purpose robotic manipulation, but they can still fail unpredictably during long-horizon execution, making reliable failure detection essential for safe deployment. Existing methods either rely on visual models that typically detect failures only after erroneous actions have occurred, or use lightweight proactive detectors trained on VLA internal representations. However, these proactive methods are often supervised with trajectory-level labels, causing normal pre-failure behavior in unsuccessful trajectories to be incorrectly labeled as failure. This supervision mismatch introduces label noise and limits both trajectory-level detection accuracy and precise timestamp-level failure localization. In this work, we study fine-grained timestamp-level VLA failure detection while addressing the cost of dense annotation. We propose a data-efficient framework that first leverages unlabeled VLA action chunks to construct action-derived weak supervision signals, capturing abnormal patterns such as inconsistent consecutive chunks, frozen or idle actions, and aggressive random motions. We then use active learning to select only the most uncertain trajectories for timestamp-level annotation and fine-tune the detector with these informative labels. Experiments across multiple VLA policies show that our method improves both timestamp-level and trajectory-level failure detection performance.

</details>


### [108] [VLA-Precision: Asymmetric Co-Bootstrapping for Efficient Real-World Online RL of Vision-Language-Action Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.04355)
*Chenyu Su,Zhaolong Shen,Yuan Qian,Chen Qian,Rui Zhang,Feng Yan,Weixing Chen,Fei Zhang,Jiamin Wang,Shuang Cong,Weiwei Shang*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: VLA-Precision is an efficient real-world online RL framework for post-training vision-language-action models on precision manipulation tasks. It introduces the Asymmetric Co-Bootstrapping (ACoB) algorithm combining early behavior intervention with progressive value calibration to prevent policy drift, and the ACoB-Stream architecture using invariant-state decoupling and on-demand streaming to overcome large-VLA throughput bottlenecks. Evaluated on nine high-precision chemistry tasks across four robot embodiments, it achieves 98.3% mean success rate in 45.8 min/task with up to 10.9x throughput improvement.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Pretrained VLA models excel at broad manipulation but fail in tasks requiring precision and repeatability. Real-world online RL post-training offers autonomous improvement but faces two bottlenecks: unreliable value signals causing policy drift, and large-VLA overhead limiting throughput and sample efficiency.

Method: Introduces VLA-Precision with two key components: (1) ACoB algorithm that performs asymmetric co-bootstrapping across timescales—early intervention-guided behavioral learning rapidly boosts policy performance and online experience quality, then global return propagation and local preference ranking progressively calibrate value estimates to yield relative action advantages for reference-regularized policy improvement; (2) ACoB-Stream architecture enabling closed-loop experience-policy processing through invariant-state decoupling and on-demand streaming.

Result: On nine high-precision chemistry tasks across four robot embodiments, VLA-Precision achieves 98.3% mean success rate in 45.8 min/task with 27.6 s episodes running at 1.2x and 1.8x the speeds of VLA and RL baselines, plus up to 10.9x improvements in throughput and computational efficiency.

Conclusion: VLA-Precision demonstrates that the combination of asymmetric co-bootstrapping and a streamlined experience-policy architecture enables effective, efficient, and reliable online RL post-training of large VLAs for high-precision manipulation tasks, addressing both value-signal drift and throughput bottlenecks.

Abstract: Pretrained vision-language-action (VLA) models enable broad manipulation but remain unreliable in tasks demanding precision and repeatability. Applying real-world online reinforcement learning (RL) to VLA post-training enables autonomous trial-and-error improvement beyond demonstrations alone, but exposes two bottlenecks: 1) unreliable value signals can induce policy drift; 2) large-VLA overhead constrains throughput and sample efficiency. To address these challenges, we present VLA-Precision, an efficient real-world online RL framework featuring the Asymmetric Co-Bootstrapping (ACoB) algorithm and the ACoB-Stream architecture. Specifically, ACoB establishes asymmetric co-bootstrapping across timescales: early intervention-guided behavioral learning rapidly improves policy performance while enhancing online experience quality. As autonomous experience accumulates, global return propagation and local preference ranking progressively calibrate value estimates, yielding relative action advantages for reference-regularized policy improvement while suppressing drift. To enable ACoB on large VLAs, we develop ACoB-Stream, a closed-loop experience--policy architecture that establishes invariant-state decoupling and on-demand streaming as design principles, delivering up to 10.9$\times$ improvements in throughput and computational efficiency. Extensive evaluations on nine high-precision chemistry tasks across four categories and four robot embodiments show that VLA-Precision achieves 98.3\% mean success rate in 45.8 min/task, with 27.6 s episodes running at 1.2$\times$ and 1.8$\times$ the speeds of VLA and RL baselines. Resources are available at https://vla-precision.github.io.

</details>


### [109] [Scalable Edge-assisted Fusion and Path Prediction for Connected Autonomous Vehicles](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.04364)
*Tyler Landle,Jackson Isenberg,Abhijit Chatterjee,Alexandros Daglis,Umakishore Ramachandran*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: Presents Conductor, an edge-based system that creates a unified world model from an RSU anchor's perspective and predicts AV trajectories while adhering to Age of Information (AoI) time budgets via an occlusion-aware AV selector and a runtime controller that jointly adapts fusion inputs and prediction loads.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: AV planning relies on sensor data with limited line of sight due to occlusions and traffic conditions. Edge-assisted unified world models and trajectory prediction can enhance AV planning, but state-of-the-art per-CAV fusion scales poorly and doesn't guarantee AoI delivery. There is a need for a scalable, edge-centric approach that respects tight AoI budgets.

Method: Conductor is an edge-based solution that builds a unified world model from a fixed RSU anchor perspective. It uses an occlusion-aware selector to prioritize AVs that detect objects not covered by RSUs, paired with a runtime controller that dynamically adapts the number of AV inputs fused and the amount of trajectory prediction per cycle to stay within the AoI time budget.

Result: Evaluation on a CAV simulation infrastructure shows Conductor meets the AoI safety bound across traffic scenarios with up to 31 CAVs. Fusion fidelity is close to an Oracle baseline and significantly better than a random selector under the same AoI constraint.

Conclusion: Edge-based unified world modeling with occlusion-aware actor selection and runtime-adaptive resource allocation can deliver high-quality fusion results while respecting stringent AoI constraints for CAV planning, outperforming random selection and approaching oracle fidelity.

Abstract: The planning algorithms inside an Autonomous Vehicle (AV) rely on information from on-board sensors whose line of sight is limited by emerging traffic conditions and occlusions. Edge-assisted creation of a unified world model fusing information from AVs and Road Side Units (RSUs) in a geographical locale, and the prediction of AVs' future trajectories, can enhance the
  planning algorithms inside AVs to improve quality metrics, such as better traffic flow and collision prevention. AVs participating in such enhancements are called Connected Autonomous Vehicles (CAVs). However, such information generated by the edge (world model and motion predictions) must reach the planners within a tight Age of Information (AoI) time budget to be
  useful. The state of the art fuses per-CAV information: each AV fuses inputs from other actors locally, which limits both scalability with actor count and quality of results.
  We present Conductor, an edge-based solution for creating a unified world model from the perspective of a fixed anchor (e.g., an RSU) in a locale and predicting future trajectories of AVs in that locale. Our solution adheres to the AoI time budget by dynamically limiting the number of AVs that would lead to the best quality of results. Specifically, we introduce an
  occlusion-aware selector that favors information contribution by AVs that detect objects in the locale not covered by RSUs. We pair this selector with a runtime controller that adapts both the number of AV inputs to fuse and the amount of trajectory predictions in each cycle to stay within the AoI time budget. Evaluation on CAV simulation infrastructure shows our joint
  selector-controller meets the AoI safety bound across traffic scenarios with up to 31 CAVs, with fusion fidelity close to an Oracle and much better than a random selector under the same AoI constraint.

</details>


### [110] [AquaBEV: Monocular Underwater BEV Occupancy with 3D Sonar Supervision](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.04411)
*Trung Tien Dong,Shengji Jin,Chen Chen,Yi Sheng,Xiaomin Lin*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: AquaBEV is a monocular underwater occupancy model that predicts local bird's eye view (BEV) occupancy from a single RGB image. It uses paired 3D imaging sonar as geometric supervision during training, mapping visual features into a calibration-free polar representation with causal decoding along the range dimension. It achieves 31.4 Visible IoU and 38.6 Observed IoU, outperforming strong transferred baselines.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Autonomous underwater robots require safe navigation, which depends on understanding free and occupied space via BEV occupancy. However, predicting BEV occupancy from a single underwater RGB image is difficult due to limited and unreliable geometric cues from appearance alone, motivating the use of 3D imaging sonar as complementary geometric supervision.

Method: AquaBEV is a monocular underwater occupancy network trained with paired 3D imaging sonar as geometric supervision. It maps visual features into a calibration-free polar representation and applies causal decoding along the range dimension before reconstructing predictions in Cartesian BEV coordinates. A controlled underwater occupancy benchmark was established, with representative occupancy methods adapted to the same RGB-to-sonar task under a unified protocol.

Result: AquaBEV achieves 31.4 Visible IoU and 38.6 Observed IoU, corresponding to 4.0% and 4.3% relative improvements over the strongest transferred baseline.

Conclusion: AquaBEV demonstrates that 3D imaging sonar provides effective geometric supervision for monocular underwater BEV occupancy prediction, and its calibration-free polar representation with causal range decoding offers a robust approach for underwater scene understanding, validated on a newly established benchmark.

Abstract: Autonomous underwater robots are widely used for exploration, monitoring, and inspection, where safe navigation depends on understanding the surrounding free and occupied space. Bird's eye view (BEV) occupancy provides such a representation, but predicting it from a single underwater RGB image is difficult due to limited, unreliable geometric cues from appearance alone. 3D imaging sonar offers complementary geometric measurements to supervise this task.
  We introduce AquaBEV, a monocular underwater occupancy model that predicts local BEV occupancy from a single RGB image, using paired 3D imaging sonar as geometric supervision during training. AquaBEV maps visual features into a calibration free polar representation and applies causal decoding along the range dimension before reconstructing the prediction in Cartesian BEV coordinates. A controlled underwater occupancy benchmark was established, adapting representative occupancy methods to the same RGB to sonar task under a unified protocol. AquaBEV achieves 31.4 Visible IoU and 38.6 Observed IoU, 4.0% and 4.3% relative improvements over the strongest transferred baseline.

</details>


### [111] [Achieving Asymptotic Near-Optimality Without $δ$-Similarity](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.04464)
*Michael Moncton,Eric Frew*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: This paper identifies an unstated assumption in the proof of asymptotic δ-similarity for sampling-based motion planners: that δ-similar trajectory segments will always be kept once sampled. The authors describe a "crowding out" phenomenon where locally low-cost paths prevent δ-similar trajectories from being added to the tree, demonstrate its occurrence with an example, and show that asymptotic near-optimality can still be achieved when crowding out is properly accounted for.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Sampling-based motion planning algorithms claim asymptotic near-optimality by relying on proofs of almost sure sampling of trajectories δ-similar to the optimal one. The authors identify that these proofs contain a hidden, unverified assumption: that sampled δ-similar trajectory segments are always retained in the tree. Since this assumption does not hold generally, the theoretical guarantees of many planners may be invalid, motivating a need to understand and address this gap.

Method: The paper formally analyzes the proof structure behind asymptotic δ-similarity, exposing the implicit retention assumption. It introduces and characterizes the "crowding out" phenomenon where locally low-cost path segments displace or prevent δ-similar optimal trajectories from being incorporated into the search tree. It then demonstrates how to achieve asymptotic near-optimality guarantees without relying on δ-similar solution trajectory retention, and provides a concrete example environment and dynamical system where crowding out provably occurs, making inductive sampling of δ-similar trajectories impossible.

Result: The paper shows that the standard δ-similarity argument in sampling-based motion planning relies on an unstated and generally false assumption about trajectory retention. It identifies "crowding out" as a concrete failure mechanism, proves it can render inductive δ-similar sampling impossible with an example, and demonstrates that asymptotic near-optimality can nevertheless still be guaranteed when crowding out is properly treated in the analysis.

Conclusion: Existing proofs of asymptotic near-optimality in sampling-based planners contain a subtle but significant flaw via the unstated trajectory retention assumption. While crowding out can break δ-similar trajectory guarantees, the paper shows these limitations are surmountable: asymptotic near-optimality remains achievable with appropriate accounting of crowding out, though future work must revise theoretical frameworks accordingly.

Abstract: Sampling-based motion planning algorithms are a popular class of trajectory planning algorithm due to their speed in complex, high-dimensional environments and ability to handle kinodynamic constraints, specifically through the use of forward dynamics propagation. Many such planners claim to achieve asymptotic near-optimality by proving the almost sure sampling of trajectories that are close to an optimal trajectory in the state space, known as $δ$-similar trajectories. This paper shows that the proof behind asymptotic $δ$-similarity relies on an unstated assumption that $δ$-similar trajectory segments will always be kept once sampled. This assumption does not hold in general. A problematic case, referred to as ``crowding out,'' is described, where locally low-cost paths prevent trajectories that are $δ$-similar to the optimal trajectory from being added to the tree. It is shown, however, that asymptotic near-optimality guarantees can still be achieved without guarantees of $δ$-similar solution trajectories when crowding out is properly accounted for. An example environment and system are provided where crowding out is shown to occur, demonstrating a scenario where inductively sampling a $δ$-similar solution trajectory is impossible.

</details>


### [112] [SocioGesture: Real-Time and Adaptive Social Gesture Perception for Human-Robot Interaction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.04545)
*Wenjin Fu,Li-Fan Wu,Jerin Peter,Chip Huyen,Boyuan Chen,Jan Liphardt*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: SocioGesture is a real-time adaptive social gesture perception system for HRI that uses a compact confidence-aware body-hand skeleton representation and a lightweight dual-stream model to recognize social cues (invitations, refusals, unavailability) from noisy onboard perception under occlusion and latency constraints. It achieves strong recognition accuracy, robustness under joint occlusion, real-time performance on edge devices, and supports vocabulary expansion via offline labeling of uncertain segments.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Robots must recognize social cues beyond explicit commands in real-world deployments, where these cues must be inferred from noisy onboard perception under partial occlusion, changing viewpoints, and strict latency constraints. Existing approaches lack robustness to these real-world challenges.

Method: SocioGesture employs (1) a compact confidence-aware body-hand skeleton representation, (2) a lightweight dual-stream model fusing body motion with hand articulation for low-latency onboard recognition, (3) occlusion-aware skeleton corruption during training (missing hands, occluded arms, temporally unstable keypoints) without increasing inference cost, and (4) a deployment-time mechanism that saves uncertain interaction segments for offline labeling and adaptive vocabulary expansion.

Result: On a mixed indoor-outdoor HRI dataset, SocioGesture achieves strong held-out-subject recognition, substantially improved robustness under structured joint occlusion, real-time performance on a robot-mounted edge device, and successful vocabulary expansion while preserving performance on original classes.

Conclusion: The results demonstrate a practical path toward robust, efficient, and adaptive social perception for interactive robots, balancing recognition accuracy, occlusion robustness, low latency, and continual learning capabilities in real deployments.

Abstract: Robots interacting with people must recognize not only explicit commands, but also social cues such as invitations, refusals, and unavailability. In real deployments, these cues must be inferred from noisy onboard perception under partial occlusion, changing viewpoints, and strict latency constraints. We present SocioGesture, a real-time adaptive social gesture perception system for human-robot interaction (HRI). SocioGesture uses a compact confidence-aware body-hand skeleton representation and a lightweight dual-stream model that fuses body motion with hand articulation for low-latency onboard recognition. To improve deployment robustness, we train the model with occlusion-aware skeleton corruption, exposing it to missing hands, occluded arms, and temporally unstable keypoints without increasing the inference cost. On a social gesture dataset collected in mixed indoor-outdoor HRI scenarios, SocioGesture achieves strong held-out-subject recognition, substantially improves robustness under structured joint occlusion, and runs in real time on a robot-mounted edge device. During deployment, uncertain interaction segments are saved for offline labeling and adaptation, enabling SocioGesture to expand its gesture vocabulary while preserving performance in the original classes. These results demonstrate a practical path toward robust, efficient, and adaptive social perception for interactive robots.

</details>


### [113] [Continual Field-Adaptive Models (CFAMs) for Post-Deployment Physical AI](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.04552)
*Amarjot Singh,Tanmay R. Pancholi,Jainam Kothari,Shrirang Mahajan,Ketan Bansal,Zackory Erickson,Giuseppe Loianno,Alexandre M. Bayen,Jeff Schneider,Vince Nakayama*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: CFAM enables unattended interactive autonomy with a complementary learning architecture (frozen slow-learning cortices + fast-learning Capsule Field) that supports gradient-free, on-device continual learning after deployment. It matches full-data-trained policies with 40% of training data, improves near-OOD action success by 13.9 pp, and shows only -0.5% backward transfer versus -11.4% for LoRA.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Mission-critical deployments require machines that operate autonomously in dangerous environments with human tools, but these domains have scarce training data, limited onboard compute, and must handle novelty without catastrophic forgetting of prior competence.

Method: CFAM uses a complementary learning system: a frozen slow-learning component with Sensor, Reasoning, and Action cortices, plus a fast-learning Capsule Field that stores one-shot, gradient-free Competence Capsules. Skills are installed few-shot in the lab and continually updated in the field via autonomous verified near-OOD capture.

Result: Evaluated on five embodiments (manipulator, quadruped, humanoid, quadrotor, off-road vehicle). CFAM achieves the operating point of a full-policy-trained baseline with only 40% of data (2.5x fewer trajectories). Autonomous near-OOD capture improves action success by 13.9 percentage points. Backward transfer is -0.5 pp versus -11.4 pp for LoRA in sequential simulation.

Conclusion: CFAM provides bounded post-deployment physical intelligence: few-shot skill learning, autonomous field growth from verified near-OOD experiences, and retention of prior competence, while open-world novelty remains out of scope.

Abstract: Unattended interactive autonomy - machines that step into danger in place of humans and complete tasks with human tools - remains a missing capability in mission-critical operations. These domains offer scarce training data and only onboard compute, yet deployed systems must face novelty without erasing prior competence.
  We introduce Continual Field-Adaptive Models (CFAMs), which learn efficiently in the lab and continue learning after deployment through autonomous, gradient-free, on-device updates. CFAM uses a complementary learning architecture with a frozen slow-learning component and a fast-learning Capsule Field. The slow component contains three cortices: Sensor, which maps multimodal input into 3D-grounded geometry; Reasoning, which decomposes tasks into skills and evaluates outcomes; and Action, which executes geometric skills. The Capsule Field stores field learning one-shot and gradient-free as Competence Capsules. Skill installation is few-shot in the lab and continual in the field; open-world novelty is outside scope.
  We evaluate CFAM across five embodiments: manipulator, quadruped, humanoid, quadrotor, and off-road vehicle. Baselines (pi0, CogACT, SpatialVLA) use the same in-house multi-embodiment dataset for physical-platform comparisons. CFAM reaches the operating point of a standard policy trained on the full prior-training dataset using 40% of the data, or 2.5x fewer trajectories. At test time, autonomous capture of verified near-OOD cases improves action success by 13.9 percentage points. In sequential simulation, backward transfer is -0.5 percentage points versus -11.4 for LoRA. CFAM therefore provides a bounded form of post-deployment physical intelligence: few-shot skill learning, autonomous field growth from verified near-OOD experience, and retention of prior competence.

</details>


### [114] [NavArena: Automated Construction of Goal-Oriented Navigation Benchmarks from 3D Gaussian Splatting Reconstructions](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.04602)
*Junhui Wang,Wei Yang,Xinyao Li,Ningjing Fan,Yuehao Yin,Xuecheng Chen,Chao Gao*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: NavArena is an automated framework that converts fixed 3D Gaussian Splatting (3DGS) reconstructions into large-scale benchmarks for goal-oriented visual navigation, providing closed-loop evaluation protocols without requiring physical traversability or custom-built environments.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Fixed 3DGS reconstructions produce realistic novel views but lack traversability constraints, valid goals, and closed-loop protocols needed for navigation evaluation, making it difficult to use them as navigation benchmarks.

Method: NavArena integrates three components over a frozen 3DGS model: (1) egocentric RGB-D rendering, (2) an occupancy costmap built from Gaussian density and height statistics for reachability and collision queries, and (3) semantic goal candidates extracted from multi-view open-vocabulary masks. Together these enable automatic generation and unified closed-loop evaluation of goal-oriented navigation episodes.

Result: Across over 2,000 scenes, NavArena generates 22.2 million expert trajectories. Spatial and semantic evaluations assess the derived navigation representations, and policy rollouts validate the diagnostic value of the unified evaluation protocol.

Conclusion: NavArena enables scalable and reproducible navigation evaluation on large-scale 3DGS reconstructions, with all benchmark-generation tools, evaluation protocols, and derived assets to be released publicly.

Abstract: Fixed 3D Gaussian Splatting (3DGS) reconstructions provide realistic novel views but lack the traversability constraints, valid goals, and closed-loop protocols required for navigation evaluation. We introduce NavArena, an automated framework that transforms fixed 3DGS reconstructions into benchmarks for goal-oriented visual navigation. NavArena integrates a frozen 3DGS model for egocentric RGB-D rendering, an occupancy costmap derived from Gaussian density and height statistics for reachability and collision queries, and semantic goal candidates lifted from multi-view open-vocabulary masks. These components support the automatic generation and unified closed-loop evaluation of goal-oriented navigation episodes. Across more than 2{,}000 scenes, NavArena generates 22.2 million expert trajectories. Spatial and semantic evaluations assess the derived navigation representations, while policy rollouts demonstrate the diagnostic value of the unified evaluation protocol. NavArena enables scalable and reproducible navigation evaluation on large-scale 3DGS reconstructions, and all benchmark-generation tools, evaluation protocols, and derived assets will be released publicly.

</details>


### [115] [Open-Set 3D Scene Graphs for Field Robotics: An Outdoor Case Study](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.04607)
*Chad R. Samuelson,Gabriel R. Slade,Joshua G. Mangelson*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: This field report analyzes the behavior of three-dimensional scene graphs (3DSGs) combined with open-set vision-language models across five outdoor robotic datasets, using Terra 3DSG as a case study. It introduces novel consistency metrics and reveals challenges including VLM point embedding outliers, traversability failures, inefficient routing (~66% path efficiency), and low region-level F1 scores (~0.359), while demonstrating that outdoor 3DSGs can remain compact (<600MB) and relatively consistent for multi-kilometer trajectories.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: While 3DSGs are promising for high-level robotic reasoning as hierarchical, semantically informed maps, their behavior in real-world outdoor deployments is poorly understood, especially when paired with open-set vision-language models.

Method: The authors analyze common 3DSG components across five outdoor robotic datasets using Terra 3DSG as a case study, investigating semantic point embeddings, place-node graph navigation, region-level understanding, and memory size, while introducing novel consistency metrics to evaluate semantic and structural stability across repeated traversals.

Result: VLM point embeddings exhibit outliers and multiple modes across all datasets with outlier ratios above 0.1 for ~30% of points. Navigation-based object retrieval achieves ~70% success, limited by traversability failures and inefficient routing (~66% path efficiency). Region-level F1 scores average ~0.359. Maps remain compact (<600MB for multi-kilometer trajectories) and relatively consistent.

Conclusion: Outdoor 3DSGs can produce compact and consistent large-scale environment representations, but open challenges remain in handling multiple semantic modes, integrating traversability into graph structures, and improving higher-level region understanding.

Abstract: Three-dimensional scene graphs (3DSGs) have emerged as a promising approach for building geometrically grounded, semantically informed, hierarchical general-purpose maps to support high-level robotic reasoning. However, the behavior of 3DSGs in real-world outdoor deployments remains poorly understood, particularly when combined with open-set vision-language models (VLMs). In this field report, we analyze the components common to most 3DSG representations across five outdoor robotic datasets to characterize challenges that arise in complex outdoor environments. Using the recently proposed Terra 3DSG as a case study, we investigate semantic point embeddings, place-node graph navigation, region-level understanding, and memory size across the five diverse datasets. We additionally introduce novel consistency metrics to evaluate whether semantic and structural graph properties remain stable across repeated traversals of the same environment. Our analysis reveals that outliers and multiple modes are common in VLM point embeddings across all tested datasets with outlier ratios above $0.1$ for around $30\%$ of points. We demonstrate the feasibility of outdoor 3DSGs for navigation-based object retrieval, achieving success rates near $70\%$, though performance is limited by traversability failures and inefficient routing, with trajectories averaging approximately $66\%$ suboptimal path efficiency. Region-level understanding remains challenging in complex natural environments, with low average F1 scores around $0.359$. Overall, our results show that outdoor 3DSGs can maintain compact (less than $600$MB for multi-kilometer trajectories) and relatively consistent large-scale environment representations, while highlighting open challenges in handling multiple semantic modes, incorporating traversability into graph structures, and improving higher-level region understanding.

</details>


### [116] [Pack It My Way: Triadic Human-Robot Collaboration for Personalized Autonomous Packing](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.04620)
*Sandeep Chowdary Kotapati,Yanxin Gao,Tsung-Chi Lin*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: This paper investigates triadic human-robot collaboration (resident, correction mediator, robot) for personalized autonomous packing, comparing human-expert versus voice-agent mediators across Protection, Compactness, and Grouping tasks. Voice-agent mediation performs comparably to human experts in two of three preference categories despite shorter instructions, but the human expert is perceived as more reliable.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Personalized autonomous packing requires capturing resident preferences that cannot be inferred from scene geometry alone. Continuous expert teleoperator involvement limits scalable deployment, motivating the need for alternative low-cost mediation approaches.

Method: A user study comparing two mediation conditions (human-expert vs. voice-agent) in triadic human-robot collaboration, using a Show-Correct-Generalize process to assess preference correction and generalization after surrounding object rearrangement across three preference categories: Protection, Compactness, and Grouping.

Result: Voice-agent mediation achieves outcomes comparable to human-expert mediation in two of three preference categories despite shorter and less detailed instructions. Both mediators are similarly easy to use, though the human expert is perceived as more reliable.

Conclusion: Voice agents show potential for reducing expert involvement in personalized robot packing, but perceived reliability and preference generalization remain open challenges requiring further work.

Abstract: Personalized autonomous packing requires robots to account for resident preferences that cannot be inferred from scene geometry alone. Expert teleoperators can interpret these preferences and translate them into feasible robot actions, but continuous expert involvement limits scalable deployment. In this paper, we investigate triadic human-robot collaboration among a resident, a correction mediator, and a robot by comparing human-expert and voice-agent mediation. We evaluate the two conditions in a user study across Protection, Compactness, andGrouping tasks, using a Show-Correct-Generalize process to assess preference correction and subsequent generalization after the surrounding objects are rearranged. Results show that voice-agent mediation achieves outcomes comparable to human-expert mediation in two of the three preference categories, despite receiving shorter and less detailed instructions. Both mediators are similarly easy to use, although the human expert is perceived as more reliable. These findings demonstrate the potential of voice agents to reduce expert involvement while identifying perceived reliability and preference generalization as remaining challenges.

</details>


### [117] [Dressing in Motion: A Human Motion-Aware Diffusion Policy for Robot-Assisted Dressing](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.04759)
*Haoxiang Sun,Fangyuan Wang,Songhao Huang,Justina Y. W. Liu,Jihong Zhu,Peng Zhou,David Navarro-Alarcon*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: This paper proposes a visuomotor policy for robotic dressing assistance that learns from static demonstrations and generalizes to dynamic human motion. It uses a PDE-diffusion-based object-centric representation to capture arm axial distribution, enabling reactive trajectory adaptation during dressing. The method is evaluated in simulation and a real-world study with nine participants, three garment types, and six arm-motion patterns, outperforming baselines in dressing progress, freedom of movement, and user comfort.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Robotic dressing assistance is promising for supporting older adults with physical impairments, but dressing during human motion remains challenging due to complex garment-human contact and occlusions that hinder action generation aligned with arm movements.

Method: The authors propose a visuomotor policy that learns dressing skills from static expert demonstrations and generalizes to dynamic user motion. A diffusion policy tailored to garment-human interaction geometry learns from partially observed point clouds with varied arm postures. An object-centric representation based on PDE diffusion captures the arm's axial distribution, and by sampling motion-relevant regions and registering them across observations, the method approximates arm motion and reactively adapts the executed trajectory.

Result: Evaluation in simulation and a real-world study with nine participants, three garment types, and six arm-motion patterns shows that the proposed method outperforms baselines in dressing progress, freedom of movement, and user comfort.

Conclusion: The proposed approach effectively bridges the gap between static-demonstration training and dynamic human motion in robotic dressing, achieving superior performance and user experience in real-world dressing assistance scenarios.

Abstract: Robotic dressing assistance is a promising solution for supporting older adults with physical impairments in daily living. However, dressing under human motion remains challenging, as complex garment--human contact and occlusions make it difficult to generate actions aligned with arm movements. In this letter, we propose a visuomotor policy that learns dressing skills from static expert demonstrations and generalizes to dynamic user-motion scenarios. A diffusion policy tailored to garment--human interaction geometry learns from partially observed point clouds with varied arm postures. We then introduce an object-centric representation based on PDE diffusion to capture the axial distribution of the arm. By sampling motion-relevant regions and registering them across consecutive observations, the proposed method approximates arm motion and reactively adapts the executed trajectory. We evaluate our method in simulation and a real-world human study involving nine participants, three garment types, and six arm-motion patterns. Results show that our method outperforms baselines in dressing progress, freedom of movement, and user comfort. The project website is https://anonymous.4open.science/w/dressing-in-motion.

</details>


### [118] [Continuous Cognitive Coverage for Autonomous Robots via Event-Dependent Cognitive Treatment and Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.04770)
*Hong Su*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: This paper proposes a continuous cognitive coverage framework for autonomous robots that assigns event-dependent cognitive treatments to every cognitively admitted event based on its state, context, and history. The framework supports suspended/resumed/interleaved cognitive processes, automates familiar event processing via learned mechanisms, and invokes explicit deliberation for unfamiliar events. Experiments report 96.76% structured treatment accuracy, 93.66% automatic processing, 92.64% cognitive coverage under bursty-delayed workloads, and 79.53% continual-learning joint accuracy.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing task-driven, reactive, or fixed-reasoning approaches in autonomous robotics process only selected events or apply predefined reasoning procedures, failing to provide continuous cognitive coverage with differentiated treatment for every event an autonomous system encounters.

Method: The framework assigns each cognitively admitted event a treatment (description, memory, risk prediction, planning, diagnosis, analogy, or learned treatments) determined by its state, context, and history. Learned mechanisms handle familiar events automatically, while explicit deliberation or fallback reasoning handles unfamiliar/uncertain events. Cognitive processes can be suspended, resumed, and interleaved, and validated experiences are continuously learned to automate, refine, and revise event-specific treatments.

Result: The framework achieves 96.76% structured treatment accuracy, 93.66% automatic processing rate, 92.64% cognitive coverage under bursty-delayed workloads, and 79.53% continual-learning joint accuracy. Novel-event reuse achieves 100% automatic processing.

Conclusion: Continuous cognitive coverage with differential event-dependent treatments is feasible for autonomous robots, enabling every cognitively admitted event to receive appropriate treatment while supporting continual learning to automate and refine processing over time.

Abstract: Autonomous robots continuously encounter objects, changes, and situations, and every event admitted into cognition should receive an appropriate cognitive treatment rather than remain untreated until an explicit task requires attention. However, existing task-driven, reactive, or fixed-reasoning approaches generally process only selected events or apply predefined reasoning procedures, making it difficult to provide continuous cognitive coverage with differentiated treatment. This paper proposes a continuous cognitive coverage framework in which every cognitively admitted event is assigned an event-dependent cognitive treatment according to its state, context, and history. Different events may therefore invoke description, memory, risk prediction, planning, diagnosis, analogy, or other learned treatments. Familiar events can be processed automatically by learned mechanisms, whereas unfamiliar or uncertain events invoke explicit deliberation or fallback reasoning. Multiple cognitive processes can be suspended, resumed, and interleaved so that cognitive processing continues as new events arrive or existing events await evidence. Validated experiences are continuously learned to automate, refine, and revise event-specific treatments. Experiments achieve 96.76% structured treatment accuracy with 93.66% automatic processing, 92.64% cognitive coverage under bursty-delayed workloads, and 79.53% continual-learning joint accuracy, with novel-event reuse reaching 100% automatic processing.

</details>


### [119] [HaptiNet: Networked Haptic Robots Enable Physical Co-presence in Geographically-Unconstrained Rehabilitation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.04799)
*Chenyang Sun,Mingjie Dong,Haodong Deng,Yudong Liu,Yi-Feng Chen,Jun Lin,Changlong Huang,Jie Guo,Yantong Liu,Yang Liu,Yuzhou Lin,Jianjun Long,Zheng Xing,Sining Zhao,Xuemin Zhang,Zhiyong Wang,Zhenhong Li,Dongrui Wu,Honghai Liu,Jian S. Dai,Mingming Zhang*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: HaptiNet is a networked haptic robotic system that enables physical, force-mediated co-presence for geographically distributed users during cooperative rehabilitation. Validated with 284 healthy participants and 111 neurological patients across laboratory, cross-city (~4,000 km), and clinical settings, it improved task performance by 24% vs solo and 22% vs visual cooperative training, with greater engagement, synchrony, and a 3.87-fold greater score improvement and 106% higher patient effort in telerehabilitation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Cooperative rehabilitation benefits from haptic, force-mediated social-motor interaction, but current telerehabilitation systems are predominantly audiovisual, leaving distributed users physically and haptically isolated. This limits access for patients constrained by distance, mobility, or clinical disparities.

Method: HaptiNet consists of robotic terminals with low-inertia, long-stroke, high-force-feedback designs tailored for upper-limb haptic rendering, connected into a distributed haptic network that employs imitation-learning-based delay compensation. Validation proceeded across laboratory, cross-city deployments, and clinical applications with healthy participants and neurologically impaired patients.

Result: HaptiNet preserved task-level force rendering consistency across single- and multi-user scenarios. Haptic cooperation outperformed solo and visual cooperative training by 24% and 22% in task performance, respectively, while improving engagement and interpersonal motor synchrony. Across intercity links of ~4,000 km, patients with neurological impairments achieved a 3.87-fold greater baseline-to-training score improvement and 106% higher patient-applied effort versus solo condition, with stable haptic interaction.

Conclusion: HaptiNet demonstrates that stable, force-mediated physical co-presence over distance is feasible and clinically beneficial, expanding access to high-quality cooperative rehabilitation for geographically distributed patients while preserving the engagement and performance advantages of in-person haptic cooperation.

Abstract: Cooperative rehabilitation enhances engagement, task performance, and social-motor interaction, yet it demands physical co-presence: users must transmit forces, coordinate movements, and infer intent through haptic contact. Telerehabilitation promises to expand access for patients constrained by distance, mobility, or clinical disparities, yet current techniques remain predominantly audiovisual while leaving users haptically and physically isolated. Here, we introduce HaptiNet, a networked haptic robotic system enabling physical co-presence for geographically distributed users via force-mediated interaction. Each robotic terminal features a low-inertia, long-stroke design with high force-feedback capacity, tailored for haptic rendering in upper-limb training. Building on these terminals, HaptiNet creates a distributed haptic network with an imitation-learning-based delay compensator, enabling users to physically perceive and coordinate with one another over distance. We validated HaptiNet in 284 healthy participants and 111 patients with neurological impairments across progressively realistic settings, including laboratory tests, cross-city deployments, and clinical applications. HaptiNet preserved task-level force rendering consistency across single-user and multi-user scenarios. Compared with solo and visual cooperative training, haptic cooperation improved task performance by 24% and 22%, respectively, while also boosting engagement and interpersonal motor synchrony. Across three intercity links totaling approximately 4,000 km, HaptiNet maintained stable haptic interaction among patients with neurological impairments, producing a 3.87-fold greater baseline-to-training score improvement and a 106% higher patient-applied effort over the solo condition.

</details>


### [120] [CoLMIN: LLM-based Multi-Decision Path Negotiation for Cooperative Autonomous Driving](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.04807)
*Zhe Huang,Zhaoxin Fan,Shuo Wang,Wenjun Wu,Xuan Zhao,Min Liu*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: This paper proposes CoLMIN, an LLM-based multi-decision path negotiation framework for cooperative autonomous driving that addresses premature convergence to suboptimal solutions in multi-vehicle negotiation by combining multi-intent generation, shallow reflection, and deep reflection mechanisms.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing LLM-based negotiation methods for cooperative autonomous driving often converge prematurely to suboptimal solutions because driving decisions in complex traffic scenarios are inherently multi-solution. This premature convergence hinders consensus formation and limits practical deployment of cooperative autonomous driving systems.

Method: CoLMIN consists of three key components: (i) LMin, an LLM-based Multi-Intent Negotiation module using a Negotiator-Evaluator paradigm that generates multiple candidate driving intentions for joint evaluation; (ii) ESRM, an Evaluation-based Shallow Reflection Module that analyzes negotiation outcomes and provides feedback to accelerate consensus formation; and (iii) LDRM, an LLM-based Deep Reflection Module that performs long-term reflection over negotiation histories to mitigate cognitive fixation and avoid converging to suboptimal solutions.

Result: Experiments in the CARLA simulation environment demonstrate that CoLMIN significantly outperforms existing methods in challenging interactive driving scenarios.

Conclusion: CoLMIN achieves stable and high-quality decision consensus in cooperative autonomous driving by exploring multiple decision paths and incorporating reflective reasoning at both shallow and deep levels, effectively overcoming the premature convergence problem in multi-vehicle negotiation.

Abstract: Multi-vehicle cooperative autonomous driving enhances the safety and reliability of autonomous driving systems through information sharing among connected vehicles, demonstrating significant potential for improving traffic safety. LLM-based approaches leverage strong reasoning capabilities of LLMs to enable effective inter-vehicle negotiation and improve cooperative driving performance. However, driving decisions in complex traffic scenarios are inherently multi-solution in nature. As a result, existing negotiation-based methods often converge prematurely to suboptimal solutions, hindering consensus formation and limiting the practical deployment of cooperative autonomous driving systems. To address this challenge, we propose CoLMIN, the LLM-based multi-decision path negotiation framework for cooperative autonomous driving, achieving stable decision consensus through multi-decision path negotiation and reflective reasoning. To achieve stable and high-quality consensus in cooperative autonomous driving, CoLMIN consists of three key components: (i) an LLM-based Multi-Intent Negotiation module (LMin), which adopts a Negotiator-Evaluator paradigm and generates multiple candidate driving intentions for joint evaluation; (ii) an Evaluation-based Shallow Reflection Module (ESRM), which analyzes negotiation outcomes and provides feedback to guide subsequent negotiations, thereby accelerating consensus formation; and (iii) an LLM-based Deep Reflection Module (LDRM), which performs long-term reflection over negotiation histories to mitigate cognitive fixation and prevent the system from converging to suboptimal solutions. Experimental results in the CARLA simulation environment demonstrate that CoLMIN significantly outperforms existing methods in challenging interactive driving scenarios.

</details>


### [121] [Strict Modes Everywhere - Bringing Order Into Dynamics of Mechanical Systems by a Potential Compatible With the Geodesic Flow](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.04817)
*Arne Sachtler,Alin Albu-Schäffer*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: The paper designs a potential (realizable via nonlinear elastic elements) that densely fills the configuration space with strict nonlinear normal modes, each passing through a common equilibrium point. This enables energy-efficient execution of various periodic trajectories in conservative mechanical systems. Controllers for mode stabilization, swing-up, and energy compensation are designed and validated on a two-DoF manipulator.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Strict nonlinear normal modes in conservative systems are typically isolated curves in configuration space, which limits their practicality. The authors aim to densely cover the configuration space with such modes so that any desired periodic trajectory can be realized using elastic elements rather than actuators, significantly improving energy efficiency.

Method: The authors design a potential function such that every configuration belongs to a strict normal mode, with all modes passing through a common equilibrium point. The potential is realized using nonlinear elastic elements. Additionally, they develop a controller to stabilize the system to a desired mode, and controllers for swing-up and compensating dissipated energy.

Result: The proposed approach is demonstrated on a two-DoF manipulator, where periodic trajectory execution was largely driven by the elastic elements. Experiments confirm that the approach performed well for the example system, validating both the potential design and the accompanying controllers.

Conclusion: Densely populating configuration space with strict nonlinear normal modes via a suitably designed elastic potential is a viable strategy for efficient execution of periodic motions. The experimental results support the feasibility and effectiveness of the approach for robotic manipulators.

Abstract: Strict nonlinear normal modes provide very regular families of oscillations within conservative mechanical systems. However, a strict normal mode will generally be an isolated curve within the configuration space of the system. In this letter, we design a potential that will densely fill the configuration space with strict normal modes such that each configuration belongs to one mode and each mode passes through a common point, the equilibrium. As the potential can be realized by (nonlinear) elastic elements it can be used to execute a variety of periodic trajectories very efficiently. Most of the required torques will come from the elastic elements in the system and not from the actuators. We also design a controller stabilizing the system to a desired target mode and a controller performing swing-up and compensating dissipated energy. Finally, we showcase the approach for a two DoF manipulator. The experiments show that the approach performed well for the example system.

</details>


### [122] [Coupled Control and Wireless World Models for Resilient Remote Robotic Control](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.04851)
*H. P. Madushanka,Sumudu Samarakoon,Mehdi Bennis*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: This paper proposes a resilient, communication-aware remote robotic control framework using coupled control and wireless Joint Embedding Predictive Architecture (JEPA) world models to jointly predict robot dynamics and wireless channel states from visual and RF data (spectrograms and Persistence Images), enabling predictive communication scheduling and adaptive resilience without full policy retraining. Evaluated in a Gazebo-ROS-Sionna simulated environment, it outperforms PID, DQN, and ViT-based predictive baselines in communication efficiency, robustness, and resilience while maintaining navigation performance.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Remote robots over wireless networks face challenges from limited communication resources, changing channel conditions, and environmental disturbances. Continuously transmitting high-dimensional sensory data (e.g., camera images) increases overhead, energy consumption, and vulnerability under unreliable connectivity, necessitating a more intelligent and communication-aware control approach.

Method: The framework couples control and wireless world models based on JEPA, jointly learning latent dynamics from visual observations and hybrid RF representations (spectrograms plus Persistence Images). Latent embeddings enable predictive communication scheduling by forecasting future robot states and wireless conditions to reduce uplink transmissions. An adaptive resilience layer detects latent prediction discrepancies and adjusts perception embeddings to handle wireless and visual changes without retraining the complete policy. Validation uses a synchronized Gazebo-ROS-Sionna simulation under diverse channel and perception perturbations.

Result: Experiments show significant improvements in communication efficiency, robustness, and resilience compared to PID, model-free DQN, and ViT-based predictive baselines, while preserving navigation performance across various wireless propagation and perception perturbation scenarios.

Conclusion: The joint JEPA-based world modeling of robot dynamics and wireless evolution, together with adaptive embedding mitigation, offers an effective solution for reliable, low-overhead remote robotic control in dynamic wireless environments.

Abstract: Remote robotic systems operating over wireless networks must maintain reliable control despite limited communication resources, changing channel conditions, and environmental disturbances.However, continuously transmitting high-dimensional sensory observations, such as camera images, increases communication overhead and energy consumption while reducing robustness under unreliable connectivity.To address these challenges, this paper proposes a resilient communication-aware remote robotic control framework based on coupled control and wireless Joint Embedding Predictive Architecture (JEPA) world models that jointly capture robot dynamics and wireless channel evolution from visual observations and a combination of raw and structured radio frequency (RF) representations based on spectrograms and Persistence Images(PIs).The learned latent representations enable predictive communication scheduling by jointly forecasting future robot states and wireless conditions, thereby reducing unnecessary uplink transmissions while maintaining reliable control performance.Furthermore, an adaptive resilience mechanism detects latent prediction discrepancies and efficiently adapts perception embeddings to accommodate wireless and visual environmental changes without retraining the complete control policy.The proposed framework is evaluated in a synchronized Gazebo-Robot Operating System (ROS)-Sionna robot-wireless simulation environment under diverse wireless propagation and perception perturbations.Experimental results demonstrate significant improvements in communication efficiency, robustness, and resilience while maintaining navigation performance compared with conventional Proportional Integral Derivative (PID), model-free Deep Q-Network (DQN), and predictive approaches based on Vision Transformers(ViTs).

</details>


### [123] [Reasoning Without Inference Cost: Latent Semantic Scaffolding for Robot VLA Policies](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.04893)
*Andrew Ting Yan Li,Zhuo Li,Zhelin Yang,Zhipeng Dong,Quentin Rouxel,Fei Chen*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: Latent Semantic Scaffolding (LSS) introduces an auxiliary loss during pretraining that aligns action-token representations of a VLA model to text embeddings of physical-reasoning rationales, with the projection head dropped at inference for zero added cost. Dense (per-phase) alignment outperforms pooled (episode-level) alignment in both in-distribution success and transfer to unseen tasks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: VLA models trained by imitation capture what action to take but not why. Adding causal reasoning improves manipulation but incurs costly inference-time overhead (generating reasoning tokens or rolling out future states) that compounds over long horizons. The authors ask whether this benefit can be captured during training and discarded before deployment.

Method: LSS applies an auxiliary loss during human-demonstration pretraining that aligns action-token representations to text embeddings of physical-reasoning rationales through a small projection head. Two alignment strategies are compared: Pooled LSS (single episode-level embedding) and Dense LSS (aligning each action token to the rationale of its own manipulation phase). The projection head is removed at inference, leaving the base policy unmodified.

Result: Dense LSS attains the best in-distribution success and best transfer to tasks unseen during alignment, while pooled alignment over-specializes to the training task. A representational probe shows Dense LSS induces roughly twice the per-phase separability in the backbone, supporting that phase-local alignment is the operative mechanism.

Conclusion: Phase-local alignment of action-token representations to rationale embeddings during training captures the benefits of causal reasoning without any inference-time cost, and alignment granularity is the key factor determining transfer performance.

Abstract: Vision-language-action (VLA) models are trained by imitation and capture what action to take but not why; adding causal reasoning improves manipulation, but current methods pay for it at inference time - generating reasoning tokens or rolling out predicted future states at every step, a cost that compounds over long horizons. We ask whether this benefit can instead be captured during training and discarded before deployment. We introduce Latent Semantic Scaffolding (LSS), an auxiliary loss applied during human-demonstration pretraining that aligns a VLA's action-token representations to text embeddings of physical-reasoning rationales through a small projection head. The head is dropped at inference, leaving the unmodified base policy with zero added cost. Our central finding concerns alignment granularity: aligning each action token to the rationale of its own manipulation phase (Dense LSS) rather than to a single pooled episode-level embedding (Pooled LSS) yields representations that transfer markedly better to held-out tasks. Dense LSS attains both the best in-distribution success and the best transfer to tasks unseen during alignment, whereas pooled alignment over-specializes to the training task. A representational probe shows Dense LSS induces roughly twice the per-phase separability in the backbone, supporting that phase-local alignment is the operative mechanism.

</details>


### [124] [ToPos: Automated Optimal Positioning on Topographic Manifolds using Constrained Geodesic Voronoi Decomposition](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.05084)
*Rajesh Raveendran,Akseli Vanhamaa,Jaakko Suutala,Antti Tikanmäki,Juha Röning*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: ToPos is an automated framework for topography-aware optimal sampling on 3D terrain manifolds, replacing flat Euclidean distances with geodesic distances and solving a Constrained Geodesic Voronoi Decomposition via Riemannian Nesterov Accelerated Gradient optimization, achieving ~74% improvement in surface-area-balanced distribution while restricting placements to feasible "safe zones."


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Conventional 2D Euclidean methods for distributing Spatial Reference Sites (SRS) fail in high-relief environments because they ignore topographic variation and physical obstructions, causing planimetric distortion, spatial clustering, and placement of targets in inaccessible or shadowed areas, which compromises both data integrity and operational safety in applications like autonomous mapping, environmental sampling, and infrastructure deployment.

Method: The terrain is modeled as a discrete 2-dimensional manifold embedded in 3D Euclidean space. Non-Euclidean geodesic distances that follow the actual surface replace flat-map distances. Point distribution is formulated as an optimization problem using Constrained Geodesic Voronoi Decomposition, solved via a Riemannian Nesterov Accelerated Gradient (NAG) engine, with placement restricted to feasible "safe zones" that exclude non-traversable slopes, vegetation, and environmental occlusions. The framework is deployed as a GIS-ready micro-service.

Result: Evaluations on non-convex sinusoidal manifolds show that ToPos mitigates planimetric distortion via geodesic metrics, yielding ~74% improvement in optimal surface area-balanced distribution as measured by the coefficient of variation (CV) of Voronoi cell areas.

Conclusion: ToPos provides a principled, topography-aware alternative to flat-Euclidean spatial sampling methods, with substantial gains in distribution quality across high-relief terrain, delivered as a GIS-integrated micro-service architecture to support reliable field operations.

Abstract: Reliable autonomous mapping, environmental sampling, last-mile logistics, and infrastructure deployment depend on the optimal surface area-balanced distribution of Spatial Reference Sites (SRS). Conventional 2D Euclidean methods often fail in high-relief environments by neglecting topographic variations and physical obstructions. This leads to significant planimetric distortion, spatial clustering, and the placement of targets in inaccessible or shadowed regions, compromising both data integrity and operational safety. This paper introduces ToPos, an automated framework for TOPography-aware Optimal Sampling on topographic manifolds. We treat the terrain as a discrete 2-dimensional manifold embedded in 3D Euclidean space and replace standard flat-map distances with non-Euclidean geodesic distances that follow the actual surface geometry. The point distribution is formulated as an optimization problem using a Constrained Geodesic Voronoi Decomposition, solved via a Riemannian Nesterov Accelerated Gradient (NAG) engine. Our approach restricts target locations to a feasible "safe zone," accounting for non-traversable slopes, vegetation, environmental occlusions, etc. Through evaluations on non-convex sinusoidal manifolds, we show that ToPos mitigates planimetric distortion by utilizing geodesic metrics. This approach results in a $\sim$74% improvement in optimal surface area-balanced distribution, as measured by the coefficient of variation (CV) of the Voronoi cell areas. The framework is architected as a Geographic Information System (GIS)-ready micro-service to bolster the mentioned applications.
  Index Terms: Topographic Manifolds, Geodesic Voronoi Decomposition, Infrastructure Deployment, 3D Mapping, Spatial Sampling, and Non-Euclidean Optimization.

</details>


### [125] [A Schema Bounded Language Model for Refining Robot Policies Without Destabilizing Local Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.05133)
*Chongwen Dong,Mithun Paul Saint-Germain,Pinjari Asif,Carlo R. daCunha*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: This paper presents a decentralized multi-robot navigation system where heterogeneous robots (using different LLM backends) operate at distinct temporal levels: LLM policies are generated and refined at the round level, while a policy-conditioned Double DQN handles tick-level action selection. A UCB bandit selects refinement modes, and robots share a round summary for cross-LLM communication. The full configuration achieved the goal in all 90 correlated robot-round records with a median completion time of 42 ticks, outperforming baseline configurations by 25.0-39.1%.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The authors aim to address the challenge of navigating composite heterogeneous robots in a decentralized framework where high-level policy reasoning (LLM) and low-level control (reinforcement learning) operate at different update frequencies, and where no central LLM coordinates team actions.

Method: The system combines per-robot LLM policy agents, a UCB bandit for refinement-mode selection, and a policy-conditioned Double DQN for tick-level action selection. LLM inference is restricted to round-level policy generation/refinement (not tick-level). Cross-LLM communication occurs via a shared round summary. The implementation uses NetLogo-Python with three robots sharing motion dynamics but using different LLM backends. Four configurations were evaluated over 30 rounds.

Result: The complete configuration reached the goal in all 90 correlated robot-round records with the lowest median completion time (42 ticks) and P90 (73.2 ticks). Its median was 25.0-39.1% lower than those of the other configurations.

Conclusion: The hierarchical, decentralized scheme where LLM-based policy reasoning operates at the round level and Double DQN handles tick-level control provides descriptive, configuration-level evidence of superior navigation performance in composite heterogeneous robot teams, though broader generalization claims are not made beyond the evaluated configurations.

Abstract: This paper addresses navigation by composite heterogeneous robots in a decentralized system when policy reasoning and local control operate at different update levels. In a NetLogo--Python implementation, three robots share motion dynamics but use different LLM backends. Each robot independently combines a large language model (LLM) policy agent, an Upper Confidence Bound (UCB) bandit, and a Double Deep Q-Network (Double DQN) controller; no central LLM generates team actions. LLM inference is confined to round-level policy generation and refinement rather than tick-level action selection. The robots perform cross-LLM communication through a shared round summary containing policies, outcomes, and learning feedback. UCB performs refinement-mode selection, and the policy-conditioned Double DQN performs tick-level action selection from navigation variables, active policy parameters, and the LLM action prior. Each of the four configurations was evaluated over 30 rounds. In the fixed simulation, the complete configuration reached the goal in all 90 correlated robot--round records and achieved the lowest median completion time (42 ticks) and P90 (73.2 ticks); its median was 25.0--39.1\% lower than those of the other configurations. These observations provide descriptive, configuration-level evidence from the evaluated configurations.

</details>


### [126] [LIBERO-RECOVER: Beyond Task Success Towards Failure Recovery in Robotic Manipulation Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.05178)
*Lin Liu,Zhicheng Bao,Lu Zhang,Ziying Song,Wu Yang,Shuai Tao,Wulong Liu,Huchuan Lu*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: The paper introduces LIBERO-Recover, the first large-scale benchmark for failure recovery in robotic manipulation. It collects 1,000+ real failure scenarios from SOTA embodied models across four recovery levels (Action Retry, Action Adaptation, Object State Recovery, Environmental Recovery) and evaluates four core capabilities (spatial understanding, object structure reasoning, interaction understanding, topological reasoning), shifting evaluation from task success to failure recovery.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing robotic benchmarks like LIBERO achieve near-100% success rates under ideal conditions, but this is misleading since real-world interactions inevitably involve failures (failed grasps, collisions, unintended object movements). Current benchmarks measure task completion from predefined initial states but fail to measure a robot's ability to recognize and recover from failures—creating a critical gap between benchmark performance and real-world reliability.

Method: Built upon LIBERO, the authors collect real execution failures from SOTA embodied models and construct a benchmark with 1,000+ recovery scenarios classified into four levels: (1) Action Retry, (2) Action Adaptation, (3) Object State Recovery, and (4) Environmental Recovery. The benchmark evaluates four core capabilities: spatial understanding, object structure reasoning, interaction understanding, and topological reasoning.

Result: The paper presents LIBERO-Recover as the first large-scale benchmark for embodied failure recovery, though specific experimental results from evaluated models are not detailed in the abstract.

Conclusion: LIBERO-Recover reframes robotic evaluation from "Can the robot succeed?" to "Can the robot recover after failure?", aiming to promote the development of robust and generalizable embodied agents by measuring a critical capability that existing benchmarks overlook.

Abstract: Vision-Language-Action (VLA) or World Action (WAM) models have recently demonstrated remarkable performance in robotic manipulation. On LIBERO, SOTA method have achieved nearly 100\% success rates, seemingly suggesting that the models are ready for deployment in real world. However, near perfect performance on existing benchmarks can be misleading: success under ideal conditions does not imply real world robustness. Existing benchmarks primarily evaluate task completion from predefined initial states, while real world interactions inevitably involve failures such as failed grasps, collisions, and unintended object movements. A robot must therefore not only execute tasks successfully, but also recognize and recover from failures to continue the task. Yet this capability remains largely unmeasured, revealing a critical gap between benchmark performance and real world reliability. To address this gap, we introduce LIBERO-Recover Benchmark, a large scale benchmark for failure recovery in robotic manipulation. Built upon LIBERO, we collect real execution failures from SOTA embodied models and construct 1,000+ scenarios across four recovery levels: (1) Action Retry, (2) Action Adaptation, (3) Object State Recovery, and (4) Environmental Recovery. We evaluate four core capabilities: spatial understanding, object structure reasoning, interaction understanding, and topological reasoning. As the first large-scale benchmark for embodied failure recovery, LIBERO-Recover shifts evaluation from \emph{Can the robot succeed?''} to \emph{Can the robot recover after failure?''}, promoting robust and generalizable embodied agents. The project will be avaible in \textcolor{blue}{https://liulin815.github.io/LIBERO-Recovery/}.

</details>


### [127] [Morphology and actuation as inductive biases in robotic hand manipulation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.05206)
*Zalán Tari,Eszter Birtalan,Péter Polcz,Miklós Koller*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: A unified framework analyzes robotic hands by separately examining kinematic and actuation stages via conditioning of task Jacobians, actuation matrices, and their products. Applied to the Shadow Dexterous Hand and the Anatomically Correct, Biomechatronic Hand, it reveals that anatomical fidelity has no uniform advantage—oblique axes improve thumb conditioning but worsen long fingers, while branching tendon networks improve long-finger control but worsen thumb control. Metrics are validated against reinforcement learning experiments (PPO, DDPG+HER, TQC+HER) across three tasks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Robotic hands vary in anatomical fidelity and mechanical complexity, but these structural choices differentially impact joint coordination and control difficulty. A unified framework is needed to understand how morphological design choices affect controllability.

Method: The paper decomposes hand analysis into kinematic and actuation stages, examined separately and in composition via conditioning of the task Jacobian, actuation matrix, and their product. Two hands representing opposing design philosophies are compared across four morphological aspects: joint axis geometry, actuator-to-DOF ratio, coupling architecture, and authority distribution. All parameters are derived from canonical digital representations, and predictions are evaluated with reinforcement learning using PPO, DDPG+HER, and TQC+HER across three tasks.

Result: Anatomical fidelity confers no uniform advantage. Oblique (anatomically-inspired) axes improve thumb conditioning but worsen long-finger conditioning relative to orthogonal axes. The branching tendon network improves effective control mapping for all long fingers but significantly worsens it at the thumb, where actuator authority is concentrated on thumb opposition.

Conclusion: Morphological design choices in robotic hands have task- and structure-dependent trade-offs rather than universal superiority. The proposed conditioning-based framework provides interpretable predictions about control difficulty that are consistent with reinforcement learning performance.

Abstract: Robotic hands vary widely in anatomical fidelity and mechanical complexity, and these structural choices influence the coordination of joint motions and the difficulty of controlling the system. A unified framework is presented in which the kinematic and actuation stages are analysed separately and in composition, through the conditioning of the task Jacobian, the actuation matrix, and their product. It is applied to two hands representing opposing design philosophies, the Shadow Dexterous Hand and the Anatomically Correct, Biomechatronic Hand, along four morphological aspects: joint axis geometry, actuator-to-DOF ratio, coupling architecture, and authority distribution. All parameters are derived from the hands' canonical digital representations. Anatomical fidelity carries no uniform advantage: oblique axes improve thumb conditioning but leave the long fingers worse conditioned than the orthogonal-axis design, while the branching tendon network improves the effective control mapping at every long finger and worsens it significantly at the thumb, where actuator authority is concentrated on thumb opposition. Predictions derived from these metrics are evaluated against reinforcement learning experiments using PPO, DDPG+HER, and TQC+HER, across three different tasks.

</details>


### [128] [One Word, Different Action: A Real-Robot Benchmark for Language-Conditioned Embodied Reasoning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.05260)
*Yiwei Liu,Luwei Yang,Shunbo Lei*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: The paper introduces "One Word, Different Action," a real-robot benchmark evaluating whether embodied systems remain invariant to task-preserving instruction changes and sensitive to task-changing instruction changes, while also assessing performance under multi-constraint reasoning and real-RGB grounding.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To assess whether modern embodied systems can reliably distinguish between instruction changes that alter the task versus those that do not, since a reliable system should preserve behavior for unchanged tasks and update correctly for changed tasks.

Method: The benchmark uses physical decision states and executable actions with task-preserving and task-changing instruction pairs to jointly evaluate Decision Invariance and Decision Sensitivity, including extra evaluation under multi-constraint reasoning and real-RGB grounding.

Result: Modern models are near saturation on single-constraint instruction changes, but several models degrade noticeably when multiple task constraints must be integrated into a single executable decision.

Conclusion: The key remaining challenge is not recognizing isolated instruction changes, but reliably composing multiple task requirements into correct robot action decisions.

Abstract: Natural-language instruction changes can directly alter robot behavior. A reliable embodied system should preserve its action when the task is unchanged and update it correctly when the task itself changes. We introduce One Word, Different Action, a real-robot benchmark built on physical decision states and executable actions, using task-preserving and task-changing instruction pairs to jointly evaluate Decision Invariance and Decision Sensitivity, with further evaluation under multi-constraint reasoning and real-RGB grounding. Experiments show that modern models are near saturation on single-constraint instruction changes, yet several models degrade noticeably when multiple task constraints must be integrated into one executable decision. These results suggest that the more salient remaining challenge is no longer recognizing an isolated instruction change, but reliably composing multiple task requirements into a correct robot action decision.

</details>


### [129] [TacPAC: Tactile Prediction and Real-Time Action Correction in World-Action Models for Contact-Rich Manipulation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.05266)
*Zipei Ma,Xiaofei Wei,Junzhe Jiang,Shunlin Lu,Li Zhang*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: TacPAC turns tactile prediction into real-time action correction for contact-rich manipulation by caching predicted contacts alongside the action plan and having a tactile expert correct unexecuted actions against that cache, improving success rates from 22% (vision-only baseline) to 64% across five real-robot tasks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Vision-centric world-action models miss local contact cues critical for contact-rich manipulation, yet naively adding tactile prediction as extra views only recovers a third of achievable gains due to a timing mismatch: predictions precede execution while tactile feedback arrives during it.

Method: TacPAC caches predicted tactile contacts conditioned on the action plan alongside the plan's own representation. A tactile expert then reads each newly observed tactile image against this cache to correct remaining unexecuted actions.
